### ViCrit: A Verifiable Reinforcement Learning Proxy Task for Visual Perception in VLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10128v1)
- **Authors**: Xiyao Wang, Zhengyuan Yang, Chao Feng, Yongyuan Liang, Yuhang Zhou, Xiaoyu Liu, Ziyi Zang, Ming Li, Chung-Ching Lin, Kevin Lin, Linjie Li, Furong Huang, Lijuan Wang
- **Abstract**: Reinforcement learning (RL) has shown great effectiveness for fine-tuning large language models (LLMs) using tasks that are challenging yet easily verifiable, such as math reasoning or code generation. However, extending this success to visual perception in vision-language models (VLMs) has been impeded by the scarcity of vision-centric tasks that are simultaneously challenging and unambiguously verifiable. To this end, we introduce ViCrit (Visual Caption Hallucination Critic), an RL proxy task that trains VLMs to localize a subtle, synthetic visual hallucination injected into paragraphs of human-written image captions. Starting from a 200-word captions, we inject a single, subtle visual description error-altering a few words on objects, attributes, counts, or spatial relations-and task the model to pinpoint the corrupted span given the image and the modified caption. This formulation preserves the full perceptual difficulty while providing a binary, exact-match reward that is easy to compute and unambiguous. Models trained with the ViCrit Task exhibit substantial gains across a variety of VL benchmarks. Crucially, the improvements transfer beyond natural-image training data to abstract image reasoning and visual math, showing promises of learning to perceive rather than barely memorizing seen objects. To facilitate evaluation, we further introduce ViCrit-Bench, a category-balanced diagnostic benchmark that systematically probes perception errors across diverse image domains and error types. Together, our results demonstrate that fine-grained hallucination criticism is an effective and generalizable objective for enhancing visual perception in VLMs.
- **Summary**: This paper introduces ViCrit, a reinforcement learning (RL) proxy task designed to improve visual perception in vision-language models (VLMs). ViCrit trains VLMs to identify subtle, synthetically injected visual hallucinations in paragraph-length image captions. The core idea is that pinpointing these errors requires fine-grained visual perception, and the resulting improvements should generalize beyond the specific training data. The paper also presents ViCrit-Bench, a benchmark for evaluating VLMs on hallucination detection across various image domains and error types. Experimental results show that VLMs trained with ViCrit exhibit substantial gains across several vision-language benchmarks, including improved performance on abstract image reasoning and visual math tasks, suggesting better visual understanding rather than mere memorization.

**Novelty and Significance Assessment:**

The paper's core novelty lies in the formulation of the ViCrit task itself. RL has been successfully applied to language tasks by creating environments that are both challenging and easily verifiable through rule-based rewards (code generation, math problems). However, translating this paradigm to vision has been difficult because the space is much larger and harder to decompose into verifiable units. The insight to use subtle, synthetic hallucinations within long, human-generated captions is clever. It provides a well-defined, easy-to-grade reward signal (exact string match) while maintaining the perceptual complexity inherent in real images and descriptions. ViCrit-Bench, while a useful resource, is less novel, as it follows the well-established practice of creating targeted benchmarks to assess model capabilities. The paper does a good job in ensuring that the benchmark is well-balanced and challenges various visual properties and hallucination types.

The significance stems from the potential to address a persistent problem in VLMs: the tendency to hallucinate or misrepresent visual information. While prior work exists on improving visual perception in VLMs, ViCrit offers a new avenue by leveraging RL with a cleverly designed proxy task. The reported improvements across various benchmarks, including transfer to abstract image reasoning and visual math, provide compelling evidence for the effectiveness of the approach. One of the major strength of this paper is its ability to extend on the training of natural image data to abstract images.

**Strengths:**

*   **Novel Task Formulation:** The ViCrit task is a unique and well-motivated approach to training VLMs for improved visual perception.
*   **Strong Empirical Results:** The paper presents strong empirical evidence for the effectiveness of ViCrit, with improvements observed across multiple benchmarks.
*   **Generalization:** The results demonstrate that ViCrit training leads to generalization beyond the training domain (natural images) to abstract reasoning tasks.
*   **Clear Presentation:** The paper is well-written and easy to follow, with clear explanations of the proposed method and experimental setup.
*   **Benchmark Contribution:** The ViCrit-Bench contributes a useful resource for evaluating VLMs on hallucination detection.
* A thorough analysis and code are shared.
* Excellent use of reinforcement learning paradigms in the vision space.
* Tackles the visual hallucination problem in language models.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Reliance on LLMs for Data Generation:** The process of generating synthetic hallucinations relies on prompting GPT-4, which introduces a dependency on a closed-source model and potentially biases the generated data. The selection of hallucinations has to be manually done as well.
*   **Limited Benchmark Diversity:** While ViCrit-Bench covers various image domains and hallucination types, it is still relatively small (607 samples), particularly for evaluating highly capable VLMs. The number of unique images is also constrained as the benchmark uses images from PixMo-Cap which are synthetically modified to test VLMs.
*   **Potential for Task-Specific Tuning:** There is a risk that the observed improvements are due to task-specific tuning on the ViCrit task, rather than a general improvement in visual perception. While the authors provide evidence for generalization, further investigation is needed to rule out this possibility.
*   **Marginal Improvements for 72B Model.** It seems the constructed training set is not as useful since the 72B model has more capacity.
*   Some lack of clarity, such as the ViCrit evaluation setup in a QA setting. How open ended and what is the format? Does the LM output the phrase and is this compared to the hallucinated phrase?

**Justification for Score:**

Overall, the paper presents a significant contribution to the field of vision-language modeling. The clever design of the ViCrit task, coupled with strong empirical results and a useful benchmark, make this a valuable addition to the literature. While there are some limitations, such as the reliance on LLMs for data generation and the limited benchmark diversity, the strengths of the paper outweigh these weaknesses. The paper has the potential to influence future research in VLM training and evaluation, particularly in the area of hallucination mitigation.

Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 8/10

### Diffusion prior as a direct regularization term for FWI
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10141v1)
- **Authors**: Yuke Xie, Hervé Chauris, Nicolas Desassis
- **Abstract**: Diffusion models have recently shown promise as powerful generative priors for inverse problems. However, conventional applications require solving the full reverse diffusion process and operating on noisy intermediate states, which poses challenges for physics-constrained computational seismic imaging. In particular, such instability is pronounced in non-linear solvers like those used in Full Waveform Inversion (FWI), where wave propagation through noisy velocity fields can lead to numerical artifacts and poor inversion quality. In this work, we propose a simple yet effective framework that directly integrates a pretrained Denoising Diffusion Probabilistic Model (DDPM) as a score-based generative diffusion prior into FWI through a score rematching strategy. Unlike traditional diffusion approaches, our method avoids the reverse diffusion sampling and needs fewer iterations. We operate the image inversion entirely in the clean image space, eliminating the need to operate through noisy velocity models. The generative diffusion prior can be introduced as a simple regularization term in the standard FWI update rule, requiring minimal modification to existing FWI pipelines. This promotes stable wave propagation and can improve convergence behavior and inversion quality. Numerical experiments suggest that the proposed method offers enhanced fidelity and robustness compared to conventional and GAN-based FWI approaches, while remaining practical and computationally efficient for seismic imaging and other inverse problem tasks.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel approach for incorporating diffusion models as priors in Full Waveform Inversion (FWI).  Instead of using the full reverse diffusion process, which can be computationally expensive and introduce instability due to operations on noisy intermediate states, the authors propose using a pre-trained Denoising Diffusion Probabilistic Model (DDPM) denoiser directly as a regularization term via a score rematching strategy. This avoids operating in the noisy latent space, allows for inversion directly in the clean image space, and requires fewer iterations compared to traditional diffusion-based FWI.  The method is presented as a simple regularization term that can be easily integrated into existing FWI pipelines, promoting stable wave propagation and improving convergence and inversion quality. Numerical experiments on synthetic seismic data are used to validate the approach, demonstrating enhanced fidelity and robustness compared to conventional and GAN-based FWI. The paper also explores the generalization capabilities of the diffusion prior by training a new DDPM on a more diverse synthetic dataset.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**  The core novelty lies in the *direct* integration of a pre-trained DDPM as a *regularization term* within FWI via score rematching, *avoiding the reverse diffusion process*. While using generative models, including diffusion models, as priors for inverse problems like FWI is not entirely new, the specific implementation detailed in this paper presents a distinct advantage in terms of computational efficiency and stability. Most prior work on diffusion models for FWI relies on sampling from the posterior distribution using reverse diffusion, which involves operating on noisy models and can be problematic for non-linear solvers like FWI. By sidestepping this issue, the paper offers a practical alternative.
*   **Significance:** The significance of the work stems from its potential to improve the robustness and efficiency of FWI. FWI is a computationally intensive and ill-posed problem. Introducing a good prior can significantly improve the solution. By directly incorporating a diffusion model prior through score matching, the proposed method overcomes the limitations of traditional diffusion-based approaches for FWI and makes them more practical for real-world applications. The claim of improved convergence behavior and inversion quality, backed by numerical experiments, provides a compelling argument for the potential impact of this work. The potential for improved stability is particularly important for complex geological settings.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Computational efficiency:** Avoiding the full reverse diffusion process significantly reduces computational cost.
    *   **Improved stability:** Operating in the clean image space promotes stable wave propagation.
    *   **Ease of integration:** The method can be easily incorporated into existing FWI pipelines.
    *   **Clear presentation:** The method is clearly explained, and the numerical results are well-presented.
    *   **Exploration of Generalization:** The paper makes an attempt to test the generalization of the method by retraining it on different and more diverse synthetic datasets, this effort, however, needs to be further explored.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited evaluation:** While the synthetic experiments are promising, the method's performance on real-world seismic data needs to be investigated. The experiments focus primarily on a modified Overthrust model, and the extent of testing on Marmousi is not extensive.
    *   **Hyperparameter tuning:** The choice of the regularization weight and the annealing schedule for the diffusion time-step significantly affect the performance. The authors acknowledge this but don't offer a robust strategy for selecting these hyperparameters.
    *   **Memory demands** The paper notes the increased memory demands and suggests methods that could be used to ameliorate it. Further exploration of such approaches would strengthen the method.
    *   **Limited exploration of prior's influence**: The paper lacks a thorough analysis of the learned prior's influence and sensitivity to the training dataset and architectures.
    *   **Uncertainty quantification**: The lack of an uncertainty quantification estimate remains a challenge for the method.
*   **Potential Impact:**  If validated on real-world data, this method could have a significant impact on seismic imaging, enabling higher-resolution subsurface models to be constructed more robustly and efficiently. It could be easily integrated into already established workflows. The flexible design of the prior gives it potential to also be adapted to other inverse problems in geophysics and beyond.

**Score:** 7

**Rationale:**

The paper presents a novel and promising approach for incorporating diffusion models into FWI. The direct regularization strategy via score rematching addresses key limitations of traditional diffusion-based methods, leading to improved efficiency and stability. While the synthetic results are encouraging and well supported, the lack of real-world validation and a more in-depth exploration of hyperparameter selection and prior sensitivity slightly temper the overall assessment. The method is well-presented and potentially impactful. The paper deserves a 7 as a significant methodological improvement.

- **Classification**: physics.geo-ph
- **Score**: 7/10

### RoCA: Robust Cross-Domain End-to-End Autonomous Driving
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10145v1)
- **Authors**: Rajeev Yasarla, Shizhong Han, Hsin-Pai Cheng, Litian Liu, Shweta Mahajan, Apratim Bhattacharyya, Yunxiao Shi, Risheek Garrepalli, Hong Cai, Fatih Porikli
- **Abstract**: End-to-end (E2E) autonomous driving has recently emerged as a new paradigm, offering significant potential. However, few studies have looked into the practical challenge of deployment across domains (e.g., cities). Although several works have incorporated Large Language Models (LLMs) to leverage their open-world knowledge, LLMs do not guarantee cross-domain driving performance and may incur prohibitive retraining costs during domain adaptation. In this paper, we propose RoCA, a novel framework for robust cross-domain E2E autonomous driving. RoCA formulates the joint probabilistic distribution over the tokens that encode ego and surrounding vehicle information in the E2E pipeline. Instantiating with a Gaussian process (GP), RoCA learns a set of basis tokens with corresponding trajectories, which span diverse driving scenarios. Then, given any driving scene, it is able to probabilistically infer the future trajectory. By using RoCA together with a base E2E model in source-domain training, we improve the generalizability of the base model, without requiring extra inference computation. In addition, RoCA enables robust adaptation on new target domains, significantly outperforming direct finetuning. We extensively evaluate RoCA on various cross-domain scenarios and show that it achieves strong domain generalization and adaptation performance.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces ROCA (Robust Cross-Domain End-to-End Autonomous Driving), a novel framework designed to improve the robustness and adaptability of end-to-end autonomous driving systems across different domains (e.g., cities, lighting conditions).  Instead of relying on large language models, ROCA learns a compact "codebook" of basis tokens representing diverse ego and agent states. This codebook is integrated into a Gaussian Process (GP) framework.  During inference, given a new scene, the GP probabilistically predicts future trajectories by leveraging correlations between the current scene's embedding and the learned basis tokens. The GP also provides a measure of prediction uncertainty, which is used to weight the training loss, emphasizing difficult or uncertain scenarios. ROCA can be used for source-domain training and domain adaptation, showing improvements over direct finetuning.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the integration of a Gaussian Process with a learned codebook of basis tokens within an end-to-end autonomous driving pipeline for cross-domain generalization. While GPs have been used for uncertainty estimation in other contexts (e.g., semantic segmentation), the specific application to trajectory prediction and adaptation in end-to-end driving, combined with the learned basis token concept, is a significant contribution.  The use of uncertainty to drive active learning is also a valuable addition. The approach of using basis tokens instead of relying on LLMs for cross-domain generalization is also a novel contribution.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a crucial challenge in autonomous driving: the brittleness of E2E systems when deployed in unseen domains. The proposed approach has the potential to improve the reliability and safety of autonomous vehicles in real-world settings. The ability to adapt the model with minimal data and computational resources through active learning is a significant advantage. While LLMs are getting a lot of attention, the cost to train these large models are very large. The ROCA technique provides an attractive alternative.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Principled Approach:**  The GP-based framework provides a mathematically sound way to model uncertainty and guide adaptation.
    *   **Efficient Adaptation:** The uncertainty-based active learning strategy allows for efficient adaptation with minimal data annotation.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper includes thorough experiments on various cross-domain scenarios and image degradation settings, demonstrating the robustness of ROCA. Comparison to state-of-the-art methods shows compelling performance gains.
    *   **Modularity:** The ROCA module can be integrated with different base E2E architectures.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **GP Scalability:** Gaussian Processes can become computationally expensive with large datasets. The paper does not extensively discuss scalability issues, particularly as the size of the learned codebook increases. Even though this code book only needs to be created once during source domain training, the GP inference does need to be run with the E2E system.
    *   **Basis Token Selection:** The method of selecting the initial set of basis trajectories and clustering them could be further explored. The current approach involves clustering ground-truth data, which might not be optimal for all scenarios.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper is likely to influence the field by providing a practical and effective approach to cross-domain generalization in end-to-end autonomous driving. The GP-based uncertainty estimation and active learning strategies could be adopted and extended by other researchers. The work demonstrates the value of uncertainty modeling in robust autonomous systems. The ability to use ROCA as an alternative to LLM is also a key point.

**Score: 8**

**Rationale:**

The paper presents a novel and significant contribution to the field of autonomous driving. The proposed ROCA framework addresses a critical challenge (cross-domain generalization) in a principled and effective manner. The experimental results demonstrate the advantages of ROCA over existing methods. While the scalability of the GP component and the basis token selection process could be further investigated, the strengths of the paper outweigh its weaknesses. The potential impact on the development of more robust and adaptable autonomous driving systems justifies a high score.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 8/10

### When Large Language Models are Reliable for Judging Empathic Communication
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10150v1)
- **Authors**: Aakriti Kumar, Nalin Poungpeth, Diyi Yang, Erina Farrell, Bruce Lambert, Matthew Groh
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) excel at generating empathic responses in text-based conversations. But, how reliably do they judge the nuances of empathic communication? We investigate this question by comparing how experts, crowdworkers, and LLMs annotate empathic communication across four evaluative frameworks drawn from psychology, natural language processing, and communications applied to 200 real-world conversations where one speaker shares a personal problem and the other offers support. Drawing on 3,150 expert annotations, 2,844 crowd annotations, and 3,150 LLM annotations, we assess inter-rater reliability between these three annotator groups. We find that expert agreement is high but varies across the frameworks' sub-components depending on their clarity, complexity, and subjectivity. We show that expert agreement offers a more informative benchmark for contextualizing LLM performance than standard classification metrics. Across all four frameworks, LLMs consistently approach this expert level benchmark and exceed the reliability of crowdworkers. These results demonstrate how LLMs, when validated on specific tasks with appropriate benchmarks, can support transparency and oversight in emotionally sensitive applications including their use as conversational companions.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the reliability of large language models (LLMs) in judging empathic communication in text-based conversations. It compares the annotations of experts, crowdworkers, and LLMs across four different frameworks derived from psychology, natural language processing, and communication studies, using 200 real-world conversations. The study assesses inter-rater reliability between these groups and finds that while expert agreement varies across frameworks, LLMs consistently approach expert-level benchmarks and outperform crowdworkers. The results suggest that LLMs, when properly validated, can support transparency and oversight in emotionally sensitive applications such as conversational companions.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its systematic evaluation of LLMs' *judgment* of empathic communication, rather than focusing solely on their ability to *generate* empathic responses. While LLMs' generative capabilities in this area are well-documented, the ability to reliably *assess* empathy is crucial for responsible deployment, which this paper directly addresses. The comparison to expert and crowd annotations is another strength. The investigation into which specific subcomponents of empathic communication frameworks are judged more reliably by LLMs is also valuable.
*   **Significance:** The findings have important implications for the ethical deployment of LLMs in emotionally sensitive contexts like mental health support, customer service, and education. Demonstrating LLMs' capacity to reliably assess empathy can contribute to improved accountability and transparency in these applications. By identifying strengths and limitations in LLM judgments across different frameworks, the study offers practical guidance for developers and researchers working on AI companions. Also, the paper highlights the importance of benchmark performance and provides insight on the evaluation of more subjective NLP tasks.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Rigorous methodology: The study uses a well-defined methodology with a clear experimental setup, a carefully curated dataset, and a comprehensive statistical analysis.
    *   Multiple perspectives: By comparing LLM performance against expert and crowd annotations, the study provides a nuanced understanding of the strengths and weaknesses of each group.
    *   Practical implications: The findings offer concrete guidance for developers and researchers working on LLM-based conversational agents.
    *   Addresses a critical ethical concern: The paper directly tackles the question of whether LLMs can be reliably used in contexts where empathy is essential.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   Limited scope of conversation type: The focus is on text-based conversations between strangers. The generalizability of the findings to other types of interactions, such as long-term relationships or more emotionally intensive situations, remains an open question.
    *   Framework Dependence: The reliability of LLM judgments is highly dependent on the framework used for evaluation, suggesting that further research is needed to develop more robust and generalizable metrics for empathic communication.
    *   Potential for Over-Interpretation: While the study demonstrates a certain level of reliability, there is still a risk of over-interpreting LLMs' capabilities in assessing empathy, which is a complex and multifaceted human trait. A reliance on a computational evaluation risks diminishing important aspects of human understanding.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper is likely to influence the design and evaluation of LLM-based conversational agents, encouraging a greater emphasis on responsible development and ethical considerations. It also has the potential to stimulate further research on the development of more robust and generalizable metrics for evaluating LLMs' ability to understand and respond to human emotions. The study will likely push future efforts to prioritize the ethical and robust integration of these tools into everyday applications.

**Score: 8**

**Justification:** The paper makes a significant contribution by systematically evaluating LLMs' ability to judge empathic communication, an area that is crucial for responsible AI development. While the study's scope is limited and there are still open questions about the generalizability of the findings, the rigorous methodology, the multiple perspectives, and the practical implications of the findings justify a score of 8. The paper pushes the field to look at this critical component when assessing future models.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### Can LLMs Generate Good Stories? Insights and Challenges from a Narrative Planning Perspective
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10161v1)
- **Authors**: Yi Wang, Max Kreminski
- **Abstract**: Story generation has been a prominent application of Large Language Models (LLMs). However, understanding LLMs' ability to produce high-quality stories remains limited due to challenges in automatic evaluation methods and the high cost and subjectivity of manual evaluation. Computational narratology offers valuable insights into what constitutes a good story, which has been applied in the symbolic narrative planning approach to story generation. This work aims to deepen the understanding of LLMs' story generation capabilities by using them to solve narrative planning problems. We present a benchmark for evaluating LLMs on narrative planning based on literature examples, focusing on causal soundness, character intentionality, and dramatic conflict. Our experiments show that GPT-4 tier LLMs can generate causally sound stories at small scales, but planning with character intentionality and dramatic conflict remains challenging, requiring LLMs trained with reinforcement learning for complex reasoning. The results offer insights on the scale of stories that LLMs can generate while maintaining quality from different aspects. Our findings also highlight interesting problem solving behaviors and shed lights on challenges and considerations for applying LLM narrative planning in game environments.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Can LLMs Generate Good Stories? Insights and Challenges from a Narrative Planning Perspective" investigates the ability of Large Language Models (LLMs) to generate stories that adhere to principles of computational narratology. The authors focus on three key aspects: causal soundness, character intentionality, and dramatic conflict. They create a benchmark based on established narrative planning examples, translated into Answer Set Programming (ASP) for automated validation.  Experiments with various LLMs show that current models (GPT-4 tier) can generate causally sound stories at smaller scales. However, planning involving character intentionality and dramatic conflict remains a significant challenge, often requiring reinforcement learning or more complex reasoning abilities. The paper provides insights into the limitations of LLMs in narrative generation, particularly in complex scenarios, and highlights challenges and considerations for applying LLMs in game environments.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its application of computational narratology principles as a structured benchmark for evaluating LLM story generation capabilities. Existing evaluations often rely on subjective human judgment or superficial metrics.  By formalizing narratological aspects using ASP, the authors provide a more objective and scalable method for assessing LLMs' ability to generate coherent and believable stories.  The development of the parameterized benchmark datasets is also a valuable contribution. While individual components (LLM evaluation, narrative planning, ASP encoding) are not entirely new, their combination for this specific purpose is novel.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a crucial question in the field of AI and creative applications: Can LLMs truly understand and generate meaningful narratives, or are they simply producing statistically plausible text? The results provide concrete evidence of the limitations of current LLMs in capturing complex narrative elements like intentionality and conflict. This has important implications for game development and other areas where narrative coherence and believability are critical. The insights into "reward hacking" behaviors and the impact of prior knowledge are also valuable contributions to understanding how LLMs approach story generation. The analysis is well-structured, and the methodology is clearly described, making it reproducible. The release of the benchmark is valuable for the research community.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Rigorous methodology:** The use of ASP for automated validation and the creation of parameterized benchmarks add significant rigor to the evaluation process.
    *   **Clear focus:** The paper is well-focused on the specific aspects of causal soundness, intentionality, and conflict, allowing for a deep dive into the challenges.
    *   **Practical relevance:** The insights are directly relevant to the application of LLMs in game development and other creative domains.
    *   **Well-defined problem statement**: Clear description of computational narratology concepts and integration of these concepts for planning.
    *   **Transparency:** Publicly available datasets, ASP encoding, and prompts enable the replication of experiments and facilitate follow-up studies.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited scope:** The evaluation is limited to three specific narrative planning examples. While these examples are well-established, expanding the benchmark to include a wider range of scenarios and narrative structures would further strengthen the results.
    *   **LLM selection:** The study is mainly based on a few models. While GPT-4 tier models are used, examining more models and architectures may provide a broader perspective.
    *   **Potential for bias:** Although formalized, the choice of narratological principles and their implementation in ASP may reflect specific theoretical biases.
    *   **Lack of exploration of sophisticated prompting:** The paper primarily explores one-off prompting and external calibration, but not more sophisticated prompting strategies.
    *   **No manual evaluation:** Even with rigorous automated validation, incorporating some degree of human assessment could strengthen the validity of results.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research on LLM-based story generation by promoting the use of more rigorous evaluation methods and by highlighting the specific challenges of capturing complex narrative elements. It encourages the development of LLMs that can reason more effectively about character motivations and plot conflicts. The paper also raises questions about the role of prior knowledge and the need for better control mechanisms to prevent hallucinations.

**Score:** 7.5

**Justification:**

The paper makes a solid contribution to the field by introducing a more objective and structured approach to evaluating LLM story generation capabilities. The use of ASP-based validation and the development of parameterized benchmarks are significant strengths. However, the limited scope of the evaluation and the potential for bias prevent it from achieving a higher score. While the findings are insightful and practically relevant, the paper's impact would be amplified by expanding the benchmark, exploring a wider range of models, and incorporating some degree of human validation. The relatively small sample size and lack of exploration on advanced prompting strategies also reduce its overall impact.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### SPARKE: Scalable Prompt-Aware Diversity Guidance in Diffusion Models via RKE Score
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10173v1)
- **Authors**: Mohammad Jalali, Haoyu Lei, Amin Gohari, Farzan Farnia
- **Abstract**: Diffusion models have demonstrated remarkable success in high-fidelity image synthesis and prompt-guided generative modeling. However, ensuring adequate diversity in generated samples of prompt-guided diffusion models remains a challenge, particularly when the prompts span a broad semantic spectrum and the diversity of generated data needs to be evaluated in a prompt-aware fashion across semantically similar prompts. Recent methods have introduced guidance via diversity measures to encourage more varied generations. In this work, we extend the diversity measure-based approaches by proposing the Scalable Prompt-Aware R\'eny Kernel Entropy Diversity Guidance (SPARKE) method for prompt-aware diversity guidance. SPARKE utilizes conditional entropy for diversity guidance, which dynamically conditions diversity measurement on similar prompts and enables prompt-aware diversity control. While the entropy-based guidance approach enhances prompt-aware diversity, its reliance on the matrix-based entropy scores poses computational challenges in large-scale generation settings. To address this, we focus on the special case of Conditional latent RKE Score Guidance, reducing entropy computation and gradient-based optimization complexity from the $O(n^3)$ of general entropy measures to $O(n)$. The reduced computational complexity allows for diversity-guided sampling over potentially thousands of generation rounds on different prompts. We numerically test the SPARKE method on several text-to-image diffusion models, demonstrating that the proposed method improves the prompt-aware diversity of the generated data without incurring significant computational costs. We release our code on the project page: https://mjalali.github.io/SPARKE
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces SPARKE (Scalable Prompt-Aware Rény Kernel Entropy Diversity Guidance), a method for improving the diversity of generated samples in prompt-guided diffusion models. SPARKE leverages conditional entropy to guide diversity measurement, dynamically conditioning it on similar prompts to enable prompt-aware diversity control.  It addresses the computational challenges associated with entropy-based guidance by focusing on the order-2 Rényi kernel entropy (RKE) score, reducing the complexity of entropy computation and gradient-based optimization. The method is tested on several text-to-image diffusion models and demonstrates improved prompt-aware diversity without significant computational cost.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper's main novelty lies in its integration of conditional entropy for prompt-aware diversity guidance in diffusion models, combined with a computationally efficient RKE score formulation. Previous methods either relied on unconditional diversity measures or were computationally expensive, limiting their scalability. SPARKE's adaptation of RKE for conditional guidance and its application within the latent space of diffusion models is a relatively novel approach. The prompt conditioning is a key contribution, as it allows for a more nuanced control over diversity, adapting to the semantic similarity of different prompts.  However, the underlying concept of using entropy to encourage diversity isn't entirely new (Vendi score), but the specific combination with prompt conditioning and efficient RKE calculation represents a significant improvement.

* **Significance:** The paper addresses a crucial limitation of prompt-guided diffusion models: ensuring sufficient diversity in generated samples while maintaining fidelity. This is particularly important for real-world applications where diverse and balanced outputs are desired. By introducing SPARKE, the paper contributes to making diffusion models more practical for tasks like geographically diverse image synthesis and data augmentation.  The reduced computational complexity is also significant, enabling diversity-guided sampling at scale, which was previously infeasible. However, more rigorous evaluations with real-world downstream tasks would significantly enhance the significance of the results.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Computational Efficiency:** The RKE score formulation addresses a major bottleneck in entropy-based diversity guidance, allowing for scalable application.
    * **Prompt-Awareness:** Conditioning the diversity measure on prompt similarity enables more nuanced and controllable diversity.
    * **Experimental Validation:** The method is tested on several text-to-image diffusion models, showing improvements in diversity without significant fidelity loss.
    * **Clear Problem Statement:** The paper clearly identifies the challenge of prompt-aware diversity and presents a well-defined solution.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Limited Real-World Application:**  While the paper demonstrates improved diversity metrics, it lacks extensive evaluations on real-world downstream tasks where diversity directly impacts performance (e.g., using generated images for data augmentation to train a classifier). The synthetic dataset experiments provide limited insights into practical utility.
    * **Dependency on Kernel Choice:** Performance relies on the choice of appropriate kernel functions (Gaussian and text). Justification for the chosen kernel parameters is somewhat limited. Further exploration of other kernel functions, including adaptive kernels, would strengthen the work.
    * **Incremental Improvement:** While the combination of techniques is novel, each component builds upon existing methods, somewhat diminishing the overall impact.
    * **Qualitative Evaluation Bias:** Qualitative results are often prone to subjective interpretation.  More quantitative analysis is needed to back the qualitative claims regarding semantic diversity.
    * **Lack of Ablation Studies on Individual Components:** The paper lacks specific ablation studies isolating the individual contribution of key components such as, latent space guidance and conditional entropy.

* **Potential Influence:**  SPARKE has the potential to influence the development of more controllable and scalable diffusion models. Its prompt-aware diversity guidance could be useful for applications requiring a wide range of outputs while maintaining relevance to the input prompts. The efficient RKE implementation could also be adopted in other entropy-based generative modeling approaches. However, the lack of comprehensive real-world evaluations somewhat reduces the potential impact.

**Justification for Score:**

I am assigning a score of **6/10**.

While SPARKE offers a valuable contribution by enabling scalable and prompt-aware diversity guidance in diffusion models, the paper has several weaknesses that limit its overall impact. The novelty is moderate, as it primarily combines existing techniques in a new way. The lack of thorough evaluation on real-world tasks and dependence on specific hyperparameters reduce the practical impact of the method. The lack of thorough analysis of the impact of the individual components on its performance also detracts from the research.  While the improvements are noteworthy, the limitations prevent it from being considered a highly significant breakthrough. I believe it warrants further investigation, particularly in demonstrating its real-world benefits and robustness.

Score: 6

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 6/10

### AURA: A Multi-Agent Intelligence Framework for Knowledge-Enhanced Cyber Threat Attribution
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10175v1)
- **Authors**: Nanda Rani, Sandeep Kumar Shukla
- **Abstract**: Effective attribution of Advanced Persistent Threats (APTs) increasingly hinges on the ability to correlate behavioral patterns and reason over complex, varied threat intelligence artifacts. We present AURA (Attribution Using Retrieval-Augmented Agents), a multi-agent, knowledge-enhanced framework for automated and interpretable APT attribution. AURA ingests diverse threat data including Tactics, Techniques, and Procedures (TTPs), Indicators of Compromise (IoCs), malware details, adversarial tools, and temporal information, which are processed through a network of collaborative agents. These agents are designed for intelligent query rewriting, context-enriched retrieval from structured threat knowledge bases, and natural language justification of attribution decisions. By combining Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) with Large Language Models (LLMs), AURA enables contextual linking of threat behaviors to known APT groups and supports traceable reasoning across multiple attack phases. Experiments on recent APT campaigns demonstrate AURA's high attribution consistency, expert-aligned justifications, and scalability. This work establishes AURA as a promising direction for advancing transparent, data-driven, and scalable threat attribution using multi-agent intelligence.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces AURA, a multi-agent intelligence framework for knowledge-enhanced cyber threat attribution. AURA uses Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) with Large Language Models (LLMs) to automate and improve the interpretability of APT attribution. It ingests diverse threat data, processes it through collaborative agents for query rewriting, context-enriched retrieval, and justification, and links threat behaviors to APT groups, supporting reasoning across attack phases. Experiments demonstrate AURA's high attribution consistency, expert-aligned justifications, and scalability. The work aims to advance transparent, data-driven threat attribution using multi-agent intelligence.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its specific combination of several existing techniques within a coherent framework for cyber threat attribution. While RAG, multi-agent systems, and LLMs have been individually applied in cybersecurity contexts, AURA's integrated approach offers several potential improvements:

*   **Integrated Approach:** Rather than applying individual techniques, AURA combines RAG, agentic systems and LLMs specifically tailored to the cyber threat attribution challenge.
*   **Explainability:** Generating natural language justifications of attribution decisions provides a significant advantage over black-box methods by allowing analysts to validate and trust the system's outputs.
*   **Contextual Reasoning:** The agent design, focused on specific stages (query rewriting, retrieval, actor inference, explanation), appears to improve the contextual reasoning capabilities.

However, the individual components are not groundbreaking. The innovation is in the system's architecture and specific application of LLMs within this context. Many similar approaches have been proposed and are being actively researched in the field.

**Significance:** The paper addresses a significant challenge in cybersecurity: accurate and timely APT attribution. Effective attribution is crucial for response, deterrence, and accountability. The potential significance stems from AURA's promise to:

*   **Improve Attribution Accuracy:** By leveraging knowledge bases and contextual reasoning, AURA aims to reduce errors in attributing attacks to the correct threat actors.
*   **Increase Analyst Efficiency:** Automation of the attribution process could significantly reduce the time and effort required for threat analysis.
*   **Enhance Trust and Transparency:** Justifications can build trust and enable better decision-making by providing insights into the reasoning behind attribution decisions.
*   **Scalability**: Multi-agent architectures can be scaled to handle greater volumes of data.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Evaluation Limitations:** The evaluation uses a limited dataset (30 reports in the test set). While this mitigates potential LLM pretraining bias, it limits the generalizability of the results. More diverse datasets would strengthen the findings.
*   **Web Search limitations**: The paper mentions the intention to use web search to increase accuracy but admits limitations to controlling evaluation.
*   **LLM Dependence:** AURA heavily relies on the capabilities of LLMs, which can be expensive, prone to biases, and susceptible to adversarial attacks. The paper needs to address these limitations more explicitly.
*   **Lack of comparative analysis with state-of-the-art frameworks**: There is little discussion or empirical data relating to comparative analysis with existing models.
*   **Overclaiming of Novelty**: The paper presents itself with more groundbreaking novelty. More clearly outlining existing approaches and highlighting their differences would better position the core contributions.

**Justification:**

AURA's combination of RAG, agentic systems, and LLMs offers a potentially valuable approach to improve cyber threat attribution. However, the limited scope of the evaluation and the inherent limitations of LLMs temper enthusiasm. While this approach may be a reasonable advancement on existing models and frameworks, the paper oversells its originality and novelty.

Score: 6

- **Classification**: cs.CR
- **Score**: 6/10

### Geometric Regularity in Deterministic Sampling of Diffusion-based Generative Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10177v1)
- **Authors**: Defang Chen, Zhenyu Zhou, Can Wang, Siwei Lyu
- **Abstract**: Diffusion-based generative models employ stochastic differential equations (SDEs) and their equivalent probability flow ordinary differential equations (ODEs) to establish a smooth transformation between complex high-dimensional data distributions and tractable prior distributions. In this paper, we reveal a striking geometric regularity in the deterministic sampling dynamics: each simulated sampling trajectory lies within an extremely low-dimensional subspace, and all trajectories exhibit an almost identical ''boomerang'' shape, regardless of the model architecture, applied conditions, or generated content. We characterize several intriguing properties of these trajectories, particularly under closed-form solutions based on kernel-estimated data modeling. We also demonstrate a practical application of the discovered trajectory regularity by proposing a dynamic programming-based scheme to better align the sampling time schedule with the underlying trajectory structure. This simple strategy requires minimal modification to existing ODE-based numerical solvers, incurs negligible computational overhead, and achieves superior image generation performance, especially in regions with only $5 \sim 10$ function evaluations.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Geometric Regularity in Deterministic Sampling of Diffusion-based Generative Models" investigates the deterministic sampling dynamics in diffusion models (specifically using probability flow ODEs). The authors reveal a consistent "boomerang" shape in the sampling trajectories, observing that each trajectory lies within a low-dimensional subspace. This regularity holds regardless of model architecture, conditions, or generated content. They further analyze this regularity using kernel density estimates (KDEs) and derive closed-form solutions for the denoising trajectory. Based on these findings, they propose a dynamic programming-based time scheduling scheme (GITS) to optimize sampling, leading to improved image generation performance, particularly with few function evaluations (NFE).

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Strengths:**

*   **Novel Observation:** The core observation of a recurring geometric structure ("boomerang" shape) within diffusion model sampling trajectories is novel and intriguing. Visualizing high-dimensional processes is challenging, and identifying this regularity provides a valuable insight.
*   **Theoretical Justification:** The paper attempts to explain the empirical observation with a theoretical analysis rooted in KDEs and mean-shift intuition. Deriving closed-form solutions provides a useful framework for understanding the deterministic process.
*   **Practical Application:**  The proposed dynamic programming approach (GITS) effectively leverages the identified regularity for time schedule optimization. The experimental results showcase performance improvements, especially in few-shot settings.
*   **Thorough Experimental Validation:** The experiments span various datasets and model architectures, strengthening the generality of the findings. Ablation studies are included to assess the impact of key parameters.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Explanation of "Why":** While the paper demonstrates and characterizes the trajectory regularity, the underlying reasons for *why* diffusion models exhibit this behavior are not fully elucidated. The KDE-based explanation offers some insight, but a more fundamental understanding of the interaction between model architecture, score function, and sampling dynamics is missing. The explanation tends to be descriptive rather than causal.
*   **Marginal Novelty in Time Scheduling:**  Dynamic programming for time scheduling is not entirely new in the context of diffusion models (Watson et al., 2021). The paper distinguishes itself by focusing on a trajectory-based cost function, but the algorithm itself is standard. The contribution is thus the application of DP to this specific regularity, not the DP approach in itself.
*   **Dependence on iPNdM and Specific Setups** A significant weakness is the reliance on iPNDM which uses a fourth-order multistep Runge-Kutta method with a lower-order warming start, which gives specific benefit to small NFE samplings. The generalizability with other solvers and ODE integrators could be tested more deeply.
*   **Limited comparison against AFS (Analytical First Step) strategy.** The authors state that AFS isn't generally helpful outside of low resolution problems, but no data showing the trade-off of the AFS method versus GITS is supplied. This limits the scope, and it might be the case that AFS methods coupled with dynamic timescheduling could exceed the results reported in the manuscript.

**Significance:**

The identification of geometric regularity can potentially inspire new approaches to model design, loss function formulation, and sampling strategies. The GITS algorithm showcases the practical benefits of understanding trajectory behavior. However, the lack of a more profound theoretical explanation and the existing literature on dynamic time scheduling temper the overall impact. The potential application of AFS methods as a comparison also undercuts the importance.

**Score:** 6.5

**Justification:**

The paper presents a valuable and original empirical observation (trajectory regularity) and explores a corresponding theoretical justification. The practical application in time scheduling is effective. However, the "why" is only partially answered, the optimization algorithm itself is not groundbreaking, the limitations for some sampling methods, and the existing related work diminish the overall novelty. The results warrant a score that reflects the paper's contribution as a significant, yet not transformative, step in understanding and optimizing diffusion models.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 6/10

### Scalable Non-Equivariant 3D Molecule Generation via Rotational Alignment
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10186v1)
- **Authors**: Yuhui Ding, Thomas Hofmann
- **Abstract**: Equivariant diffusion models have achieved impressive performance in 3D molecule generation. These models incorporate Euclidean symmetries of 3D molecules by utilizing an SE(3)-equivariant denoising network. However, specialized equivariant architectures limit the scalability and efficiency of diffusion models. In this paper, we propose an approach that relaxes such equivariance constraints. Specifically, our approach learns a sample-dependent SO(3) transformation for each molecule to construct an aligned latent space. A non-equivariant diffusion model is then trained over the aligned representations. Experimental results demonstrate that our approach performs significantly better than previously reported non-equivariant models. It yields sample quality comparable to state-of-the-art equivariant diffusion models and offers improved training and sampling efficiency. Our code is available at https://github.com/skeletondyh/RADM
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a method called RADM (Rotationally Aligned Diffusion Model) for generating 3D molecules using a non-equivariant diffusion model.  Instead of directly using an SE(3)-equivariant denoising network (common in 3D molecule generation), RADM learns a sample-dependent SO(3) transformation to align molecules into a latent space.  A non-equivariant diffusion model is then trained on these aligned representations. This approach aims to improve scalability and efficiency compared to equivariant models while maintaining comparable sample quality.  The method uses an autoencoder to construct the aligned representations in an unsupervised manner, with a graph neural network generating the rotations.  Experiments on QM9 and GEOM-Drugs datasets demonstrate that RADM outperforms previous non-equivariant models and achieves performance comparable to state-of-the-art equivariant models, with improved training and sampling efficiency.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of aligning molecules before applying a non-equivariant diffusion model has some novelty, drawing inspiration from successful techniques in 3D vision.  The method of learning the alignment using an autoencoder with a sample-dependent rotation generation network is a reasonable approach. The combination of these ideas, along with the use of a transformer-based diffusion model (DiT), represents a non-trivial engineering effort.
*   **Significance:** The paper addresses an important issue in the field: the computational cost and architectural limitations of equivariant networks. Successfully using a non-equivariant model while achieving comparable performance to equivariant ones is a significant step towards more scalable and efficient 3D molecule generation. The paper demonstrates that equivariance, while commonly used, may not be strictly *necessary* for good performance. This opens the door for exploring simpler and more scalable architectures. The empirical results support this claim and provide a strong basis for future work. However, other works are already using transformer-based approaches and the significance is diminished slightly by concurrent work using DiT with stochastic equivariance.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Strong empirical results demonstrating improved performance over existing non-equivariant methods and comparability to state-of-the-art equivariant models.
    *   Improved training and sampling efficiency compared to equivariant models.
    *   A well-motivated approach with a clear explanation of the methodology.
    *   Ablation studies demonstrating the importance of the alignment step.
    *   Addresses an important practical problem: the scalability and efficiency of equivariant models.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The reliance on an autoencoder introduces an additional set of hyperparameters and training steps. The stability and robustness of this combined training process could be a concern.
    *   While the paper shows comparable performance, there's a slight performance gap between RADM and the best equivariant models in some metrics. Further improvements may be necessary to fully close this gap.
    *   The paper focuses on unconditional generation.  The approach's applicability to conditional generation tasks (e.g., generating molecules with specific properties) is not explored.
    *   The performance advantages are somewhat incremental; it's an important step but not a revolutionary breakthrough.
    *   Concurrent works (e.g., Zhang et al., Joshi et al., 2025) are also exploring DiT for molecule generation and the potential for stochastic equivariance, which somewhat diminishes the impact of this approach being the first to leverage DiT.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper presents a solid, well-executed approach with strong empirical results that contribute to the field of 3D molecule generation. It tackles a relevant problem and offers a practical solution. The empirical results are convincingly demonstrate the effectiveness of RADM in scaling non-equivariant diffusion models. The concurrent work employing DiT has reduced the perceived novelty slightly, however it is still very significant that a *completely* non-equivariant model can be used and the data augmentation step is not necessary. Therefore, despite the limitations and the presence of concurrent work, the paper represents a worthwhile contribution that merits consideration.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Prompt Variability Effects On LLM Code Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10204v1)
- **Authors**: Andrei Paleyes, Radzim Sendyka, Diana Robinson, Christian Cabrera, Neil D. Lawrence
- **Abstract**: Code generation is one of the most active areas of application of Large Language Models (LLMs). While LLMs lower barriers to writing code and accelerate development process, the overall quality of generated programs depends on the quality of given prompts. Specifically, functionality and quality of generated code can be sensitive to user's background and familiarity with software development. It is therefore important to quantify LLM's sensitivity to variations in the input. To this end we propose a synthetic evaluation pipeline for code generation with LLMs, as well as a systematic persona-based evaluation approach to expose qualitative differences of LLM responses dependent on prospective user background. Both proposed methods are completely independent from specific programming tasks and LLMs, and thus are widely applicable. We provide experimental evidence illustrating utility of our methods and share our code for the benefit of the community.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the sensitivity of Large Language Models (LLMs) in code generation to variations in input prompts. It proposes two evaluation methods: a synthetic evaluation pipeline that uses methods like typos, synonyms, and paraphrasing to alter prompts and measures the resulting changes in generated code; and a persona-based evaluation that generates prompts from different personas with varying software engineering experience to qualitatively assess the LLM’s sensitivity to user background and knowledge. The study then presents experimental results for multiple LLMs, showing that the models exhibit varying degrees of sensitivity to different prompt alterations and user personas, which highlights the need for user-centric approaches in code generation with LLMs. The code is openly available.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its systematic exploration of LLM code generation sensitivity using both synthetic and persona-based approaches. While other work investigates code generation robustness, the combination of synthetic prompt engineering *and* a persona-based evaluation is a unique and valuable contribution. The exploration of prompt alterations using methods like keyboard typos, synonyms, and paraphrasing isn’t entirely new on its own, but applying them systematically to code generation context and linking that to the impact of simulated "user types" is a good innovation.

*   **Significance:** The significance of the paper is in highlighting the importance of prompt engineering and user background in code generation with LLMs. Demonstrating that LLMs are sensitive to seemingly minor prompt variations has important implications for building trust and reliability in these models. It also points to the need for user guidance and tailored LLM interactions for users with differing expertise. The work also provides evidence of data contamination effects in common LLM code generation benchmarks. Furthermore, the open availability of the code promotes reproducibility and allows others to build upon this work.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The use of both synthetic and persona-based evaluations provides a more complete picture of LLM code generation sensitivity.
    *   **Systematic Approach:** The synthetic evaluation pipeline is well-defined and allows for controlled experiments.
    *   **Real-World Relevance:** The persona-based evaluation addresses the important issue of user background and its impact on LLM performance.
    *   **Open Source Contribution:** Sharing the code promotes further research and development in this area.
    *   **Addresses Data Contamination:** The paper highlights the issue of data contamination and takes steps to mitigate it by using newer and self-created datasets.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Qualitative Persona Analysis:** The analysis of the persona-generated code is somewhat qualitative and lacks rigorous quantitative metrics, which could be an area for future enhancement.
    *   **Limited Personas:** The study used a relatively small number of personas. Expanding this to a broader set of user profiles could provide more generalizable insights.
    *   **LLM Selection:** While several LLMs were tested, the rapidly evolving landscape may mean these results quickly become dated. However, the methodology is designed to easily incorporate new models.
    *   **Task Complexity:** The evaluation tasks could be extended with more complex or real-world programming projects to better reflect practical application scenarios.
    *   **Focus on syntax, not semantics.** TSED measures syntactic differences, but it doesn't guarantee the changes actually affect code functionality.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper can influence future research directions in several ways:
    *   **User-Centric LLM Design:** It can encourage the development of LLMs that are more adaptable to user background and expertise.
    *   **Improved Prompt Engineering Techniques:** It can guide the development of better prompt engineering techniques to improve the reliability and consistency of LLM code generation.
    *   **More Robust Evaluation Metrics:** It can motivate the development of more comprehensive and reliable metrics for evaluating LLM code generation performance.
    *   **Benchmarking Datasets:** Can guide the development of novel benchmarking datasets for code generation that are more robust against data contamination.

**Score: 7.5**

**Justification:**

The paper offers a good contribution to the field by systematically investigating the prompt sensitivity of LLMs for code generation and linking this sensitivity to variations in user background and technical expertise. The methods are robust and the experimental design carefully considers the rapidly evolving nature of LLMs and the problem of data contamination. While the quantitative persona analysis could be more robust and the study has some limitations in task complexity and the range of personas considered, the work is novel, significant, and provides a valuable foundation for future research. The publicly available code is a significant bonus that facilitates replication and further exploration.

- **Classification**: cs.SE
- **Score**: 7/10

### AWP: Activation-Aware Weight Pruning and Quantization with Projected Gradient Descent
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10205v1)
- **Authors**: Jing Liu, Toshiaki Koike-Akino, Ye Wang, Hassan Mansour, Matthew Brand
- **Abstract**: To address the enormous size of Large Language Models (LLMs), model compression methods, such as quantization and pruning, are often deployed, especially on edge devices. In this work, we focus on layer-wise post-training quantization and pruning. Drawing connections between activation-aware weight pruning and sparse approximation problems, and motivated by the success of Iterative Hard Thresholding (IHT), we propose a unified method for Activation-aware Weight pruning and quantization via Projected gradient descent (AWP). Our experiments demonstrate that AWP outperforms state-of-the-art LLM pruning and quantization methods. Theoretical convergence guarantees of the proposed method for pruning are also provided.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "AWP: Activation-Aware Weight Pruning and Quantization with Projected Gradient Descent":

**Summary:**

The paper proposes a novel method called Activation-Aware Weight pruning and Quantization via Projected gradient descent (AWP) to compress Large Language Models (LLMs). AWP is a layer-wise post-training quantization and pruning technique drawing inspiration from sparse approximation methods and Iterative Hard Thresholding (IHT).  It formulates the LLM compression problem as a sparse approximation problem and employs Projected Gradient Descent (PGD) to unify pruning and quantization without computationally expensive operations like SVD.  The authors present experimental results demonstrating that AWP outperforms state-of-the-art LLM compression methods and provide theoretical convergence guarantees for pruning.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**

    *   **Methodological Contribution:** The core novelty lies in formulating the LLM compression problem within a sparse approximation framework and leveraging PGD/IHT for activation-aware pruning and quantization. While IHT and PGD are established algorithms, their application to LLM compression with the explicit consideration of activation awareness is relatively novel. The use of column scaling of weights based on activations is not completely new and has been used in other pruning and quantization techniques. However, this paper clearly formulates it as a gradient descent problem and analyzes its convergence.
    *   **Unification of Pruning and Quantization:** AWP's ability to handle both pruning and quantization within a single framework is a valuable feature, offering potential efficiency and flexibility in model compression pipelines. However, other methods also address joint pruning and quantization; thus, this aspect is incrementally novel rather than revolutionary.

*   **Significance:**

    *   **Performance Improvement:** The experimental results, if robust, indicate a significant improvement over existing LLM compression techniques.  Outperforming state-of-the-art methods, especially at higher pruning ratios, is a tangible contribution. The perplexity gains are not marginal.
    *   **Theoretical Guarantees:** Providing convergence guarantees for the pruning process adds substantial value, offering a degree of confidence in the method's behavior that is often lacking in heuristic compression techniques.  The analysis, based on RIP and RSC/RSM properties, is a standard but important step towards establishing the reliability of AWP.
    *   **Practicality:** The method avoids computationally expensive operations (like SVD), making it more practical for compressing large models. This is a major advantage, as SVD is a major bottleneck in other compression algorithms.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Clear problem formulation and motivation.
    *   Novel application of PGD/IHT to LLM compression.
    *   Strong empirical results demonstrating performance improvements.
    *   Theoretical analysis providing convergence guarantees for pruning.
    *   Computational efficiency due to the avoidance of SVD.
    *   Unified approach for pruning and quantization.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The description of theoretical guarantees is relatively brief in the main paper. A more detailed discussion and potential limitations of the RIP/RSC/RSM assumptions would strengthen the analysis.
    *   The performance comparisons are primarily against methods from the WANDA paper, and it may be good to compare with other state-of-the-art quantization or pruning methods.
    *   Practical deployment issues may need further elaboration.
    *   The stepsize for gradient descent has to be tuned. This needs to be further studied.

*   **Potential Influence:**

    *   The work is well positioned to influence future research on LLM compression.
    *   The AWP framework can be extended and adapted to different model architectures and compression scenarios.
    *   The insights from the sparse approximation perspective may lead to new compression algorithms.
    *   This kind of method helps improve edge device applications.

*   **Overall:**

The paper presents a solid contribution to the field of LLM compression. The application of PGD/IHT within the sparse approximation framework provides a novel perspective and demonstrates tangible performance improvements. The theoretical guarantees add significant weight to the proposed method. The computational efficiency of the approach makes it relevant for practical applications. Despite some weaknesses in the depth of the theoretical analysis, the paper as a whole is significant.

**Score: 7.5**

*Reasoning: The paper presents a clear, novel, and well-motivated method with solid empirical results and theoretical backing. However, the marginal improvement compared to existing approaches in specific cases, as well as the somewhat limited theoretical details in the main paper and limited comparisons to other state-of-the-art approaches prevent it from receiving a higher score. It is a significant and valuable contribution, but not a breakthrough. The potential for future research and adaptation of the method further justifies the assigned score.*

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### ScoreMix: Improving Face Recognition via Score Composition in Diffusion Generators
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10226v1)
- **Authors**: Parsa Rahimi, Sebastien Marcel
- **Abstract**: In this paper, we propose ScoreMix, a novel yet simple data augmentation strategy leveraging the score compositional properties of diffusion models to enhance discriminator performance, particularly under scenarios with limited labeled data. By convexly mixing the scores from different class-conditioned trajectories during diffusion sampling, we generate challenging synthetic samples that significantly improve discriminative capabilities in all studied benchmarks. We systematically investigate class-selection strategies for mixing and discover that greater performance gains arise when combining classes distant in the discriminator's embedding space, rather than close in the generator's condition space. Moreover, we empirically show that, under standard metrics, the correlation between the generator's learned condition space and the discriminator's embedding space is minimal. Our approach achieves notable performance improvements without extensive parameter searches, demonstrating practical advantages for training discriminative models while effectively mitigating problems regarding collections of large datasets. Paper website: https://parsa-ra.github.io/scoremix
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces ScoreMix, a data augmentation strategy for improving face recognition performance, especially when training data is limited. ScoreMix leverages the score composition property of diffusion models, mixing scores from different class-conditioned diffusion trajectories to generate challenging synthetic training samples. The authors systematically investigate class-selection strategies for mixing, finding that greater performance gains are achieved by combining classes that are distant in the discriminator's embedding space, rather than those close in the generator's condition space.  They also empirically demonstrate a minimal correlation between the generator's learned condition space and the discriminator's embedding space. The approach leads to performance improvements on standard face recognition benchmarks, without requiring extensive parameter tuning or external data sources.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of using score composition within a diffusion model for data augmentation is moderately novel. While score composition itself has been explored in generative contexts (e.g., style transfer, concept manipulation), the application to data augmentation for discriminative tasks, particularly face recognition, seems less explored. The finding that distance in the embedding space, rather than the generator's latent space, is a key driver of augmentation effectiveness is a valuable and non-obvious insight. However, related techniques like adversarial training achieve a similar objective of creating difficult examples, reducing the relative novelty.

*   **Significance:** The significance lies in the practical benefits. The approach offers a way to improve face recognition performance, especially under limited data conditions, which is a relevant problem in many real-world scenarios. The fact that it doesn't rely on external, potentially biased, datasets is a strength.  The gains, while not necessarily groundbreaking, are consistently demonstrated across benchmarks. The implication that the generator's condition space and discriminator's embedding space are largely uncorrelated is an interesting observation that might influence future research.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear and well-written presentation.
    *   Systematic investigation of class selection strategies.
    *   Empirical validation on multiple face recognition benchmarks.
    *   Demonstration of improved performance over baselines.
    *   No reliance on external datasets, addressing potential bias issues.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The performance improvements, while positive, are incremental rather than revolutionary.
    *   The explanation for why distant classes in embedding space work better is somewhat speculative and could be further strengthened with more theoretical analysis.
    *   The computational cost (two diffusion passes) relative to AugGen, while acknowledged, could be a limiting factor in very large-scale applications.
    *   The comparison focuses on datasets and generators specifically tailored for faces, potentially limiting generalizability to other tasks or data modalities.
    *   The degree to which the augmented data improves robustness (e.g., to adversarial attacks, lighting variations) is not thoroughly explored.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper could influence research in several ways:
    *   Inspire further work on score composition for data augmentation in various tasks.
    *   Encourage investigations into the relationship between generator latent spaces and discriminator embedding spaces.
    *   Provide a practical recipe for improving face recognition performance in resource-constrained scenarios.
    *   Promote the development of data augmentation techniques that are less reliant on external datasets.

*   **Rigorous Rationale for Score:** The paper provides a solid contribution to the field, with practical implications and interesting empirical findings. While the core idea is not radically new, the specific application, systematic analysis, and the resulting insights are valuable. The limitations (incremental gains, some speculative explanations, and computational cost) temper the overall assessment.

### Prompt-Guided Latent Diffusion with Predictive Class Conditioning for 3D Prostate MRI Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10230v1)
- **Authors**: Emerson P. Grabke, Masoom A. Haider, Babak Taati
- **Abstract**: Latent diffusion models (LDM) could alleviate data scarcity challenges affecting machine learning development for medical imaging. However, medical LDM training typically relies on performance- or scientific accessibility-limiting strategies including a reliance on short-prompt text encoders, the reuse of non-medical LDMs, or a requirement for fine-tuning with large data volumes. We propose a Class-Conditioned Efficient Large Language model Adapter (CCELLA) to address these limitations. CCELLA is a novel dual-head conditioning approach that simultaneously conditions the LDM U-Net with non-medical large language model-encoded text features through cross-attention and with pathology classification through the timestep embedding. We also propose a joint loss function and a data-efficient LDM training framework. In combination, these strategies enable pathology-conditioned LDM training for high-quality medical image synthesis given limited data volume and human data annotation, improving LDM performance and scientific accessibility.
