Our method achieves a 3D FID score of 0.025 on a size-limited prostate MRI dataset, significantly outperforming a recent foundation model with FID 0.071. When training a classifier for prostate cancer prediction, adding synthetic images generated by our method to the training dataset improves classifier accuracy from 69% to 74%. Training a classifier solely on our method's synthetic images achieved comparable performance to training on real images alone.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary**

The paper introduces CCELLA (Class-Conditioned Efficient Large Language Model Adapter), a novel approach to improve latent diffusion models (LDMs) for generating 3D prostate MRI images. CCELLA uses a dual-head conditioning mechanism that simultaneously incorporates text features extracted from clinical reports using a large language model (LLM) and pathology classification information into the LDM's U-Net. This is designed to address data scarcity challenges in medical imaging by enabling pathology-conditioned LDM training with limited labeled data. The authors also propose a joint loss function and a data-efficient LDM training framework. The method's performance is evaluated through FID scores and by assessing the ability of synthetic images to improve prostate cancer classification accuracy when used for data augmentation. The results show improved image quality (lower FID) and increased classifier accuracy compared to baseline LDMs.

**Critical Evaluation**

**Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the combined approach of:

1.  **Dual-head Conditioning:** The simultaneous use of LLM-extracted text features *and* pathology classification information in a novel adapter for LDM conditioning.
2.  **Joint Loss Function:** The introduction of a specific loss function tailored to both image reconstruction and pathology extraction within the LDM training process.
3.  **Data-Efficient Framework:** The design of the framework aims to operate effectively with a limited dataset, which is a practical and important consideration for medical imaging applications.
4.  **Medical Application:** Applying these advancements to the task of 3D prostate MRI synthesis is important given clinical significance of prostate cancer and need to reduce data scarcity.

While ELLA already offered adapters for LDM, and PathLDM integrated classification and LLM prompts, CCELLA integrates them synergistically for 3D medical data. The key is how the class information influences the LLM adapter. This element constitutes a meaningful increment.

**Significance:**

The significance is multi-faceted:

*   **Addressing Data Scarcity:** The paper directly tackles the critical problem of limited labeled data in medical imaging, a major bottleneck for applying machine learning in healthcare.
*   **Improved Image Synthesis:** The demonstrated improvement in image quality (lower FID) is important for downstream tasks that rely on high-quality synthetic data.
*   **Data Augmentation for Classification:** The increase in classifier accuracy when synthetic data is used for augmentation suggests that the generated images are clinically relevant and can improve diagnostic performance.
*   **Accessibility:** By minimizing the need for extensive data annotation, the work makes LDM training more accessible to institutions with limited resources. This addresses a critical scientific gap.

**Strengths:**

*   **Clear problem definition:** The paper accurately identifies the limitations of existing LDM approaches in the medical imaging domain.
*   **Well-defined methodology:** The proposed CCELLA architecture, loss function, and training framework are clearly explained.
*   **Comprehensive evaluation:** The paper uses appropriate metrics (FID, classification accuracy) and ablation studies to rigorously evaluate the method.
*   **Practical relevance:** The focus on data efficiency and usability with routine clinical data makes the work directly relevant to real-world applications.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Generalizability:** The evaluation focuses solely on prostate MRI data. Further studies are needed to assess its performance on other imaging modalities and anatomical regions.
*   **FID limitations:** While FID is a widely used metric, it doesn't fully capture the clinical relevance of the generated images. A radiologist-based assessment of image quality would strengthen the evaluation.
*   **Data dependency:** There is over reliance on a single healthcare institution with no external validation of performance.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper offers a novel and practical solution to a significant challenge in medical image synthesis. The synergistic combination of LLM text processing, classification, and a joint training framework demonstrates a clear advancement over existing methods. While further validation across different datasets and a radiologist-based assessment would be beneficial, the current results provide strong evidence of the method's potential. The contributions are technically sound, well-evaluated, and have direct implications for improving the accessibility and applicability of LDM in healthcare. The integration of multiple recent ideas provides a cohesive solution to a specific problem, making it both impactful and realistic. This leads to a well-rounded contribution.

Score: 8

- **Classification**: eess.IV
- **Score**: 8/10

### Classifying Unreliable Narrators with Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10231v1)
- **Authors**: Anneliese Brei, Katharine Henry, Abhisheik Sharma, Shashank Srivastava, Snigdha Chaturvedi
- **Abstract**: Often when we interact with a first-person account of events, we consider whether or not the narrator, the primary speaker of the text, is reliable. In this paper, we propose using computational methods to identify unreliable narrators, i.e. those who unintentionally misrepresent information. Borrowing literary theory from narratology to define different types of unreliable narrators based on a variety of textual phenomena, we present TUNa, a human-annotated dataset of narratives from multiple domains, including blog posts, subreddit posts, hotel reviews, and works of literature. We define classification tasks for intra-narrational, inter-narrational, and inter-textual unreliabilities and analyze the performance of popular open-weight and proprietary LLMs for each. We propose learning from literature to perform unreliable narrator classification on real-world text data. To this end, we experiment with few-shot, fine-tuning, and curriculum learning settings. Our results show that this task is very challenging, and there is potential for using LLMs to identify unreliable narrators. We release our expert-annotated dataset and code and invite future research in this area.
- **Summary**: Okay, I will provide a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Classifying Unreliable Narrators with Large Language Models".

**Summary:**

This paper introduces the task of automatically identifying unreliable narrators in text, drawing from narratology's definitions of unreliability. The authors define three types of unreliability: intra-narrational, inter-narrational, and inter-textual. To facilitate research on this novel task, they create and release TUNA, a human-annotated dataset of first-person narratives from various domains (blog posts, subreddit posts, hotel reviews, and fiction). The paper then explores the performance of several open-weight and proprietary large language models (LLMs) on classifying these types of unreliability using zero-shot, few-shot, fine-tuning, and curriculum learning approaches. The results suggest that the task is challenging, and while LLMs show some promise, significant room for improvement remains. The authors also perform a limited analysis relating narrator gender, narration style, and narration sentiment to classification performance.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary strength lies in its introduction of a novel task. While narratology has extensively discussed unreliable narrators, bringing this concept into computational linguistics and exploring its automatic detection is a genuinely fresh direction. There is no prior research directly addressing this issue. The framing of the task in terms of intra/inter-narrational/textual reliability based on existing literary definitions is well structured and makes the task concrete. The construction of TUNA dataset itself provides a necessary contribution to the nascent field.
*   **Significance:** The significance stems from the potential applications of identifying unreliable narrators. The authors point to several real-world scenarios such as detecting bias in social media posts, evaluating the credibility of reviews, and assessing the effectiveness of communication. While these are plausible applications, the paper doesn't delve deeply into demonstrating their impact in these scenarios. There is an implicit assumption that identifying unreliable narrators will automatically improve the quality or safety of transmission of information. Such connections require further investigation.
*   **Methodology:** The experiments are reasonably thorough given the novelty of the task. The exploration of various LLMs and training strategies (zero-shot, few-shot, fine-tuning, curriculum learning) provides a good initial benchmark. The inclusion of both open-weight and proprietary models is a plus. The analysis examining how narrator gender, style, and sentiment affect the performance is a helpful, though limited, investigation. However, the reliance on macro-averaged F1 score as a primary metric can be misleading, especially with imbalanced datasets. More detailed analysis of class-wise performance and confusion matrices could have strengthened the evaluation. The use of automated methods to extract narrative properties (gender, number of characters, style, sentiment) is potentially unreliable and may contaminate the analysis. Additionally, the dataset size (817 annotated narratives) feels somewhat limited for robust LLM training, particularly after splitting into train/val/test sets.
*   **Limitations:** The paper acknowledges some limitations, including the focus on English text and short snippets, and the dataset size, which is adequate given the annotation effort, but limiting from a Machine Learning perspective. One missing discussion is the subjectivity inherent in narrative analysis. While the authors rely on experts to annotate narratives, unreliability itself is reader-dependent, and further investigations can address the impact of differing reader perspective.
*   **Overall Impact:** The paper makes a solid contribution by opening up a new research area in computational linguistics. It provides a clear definition of the problem, a valuable dataset, and initial benchmarks. However, the modest performance of LLMs and the limited demonstration of practical applications suggest that much work remains to be done. It invites a broader conversation on bridging machine learning and literary theory.
*   **Justification:** The main reason for assigning a higher score is that it is pioneering work that invites an entire direction of research. This has the ability to influence how future works deal with information safety and data credibility. There are several limitations but the benefits of the paper outweigh those.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Conditional diffusion models for guided anomaly detection in brain images using fluid-driven anomaly randomization
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10233v1)
- **Authors**: Ana Lawry Aguila, Peirong Liu, Oula Puonti, Juan Eugenio Iglesias
- **Abstract**: Supervised machine learning has enabled accurate pathology detection in brain MRI, but requires training data from diseased subjects that may not be readily available in some scenarios, for example, in the case of rare diseases. Reconstruction-based unsupervised anomaly detection, in particular using diffusion models, has gained popularity in the medical field as it allows for training on healthy images alone, eliminating the need for large disease-specific cohorts. These methods assume that a model trained on normal data cannot accurately represent or reconstruct anomalies. However, this assumption often fails with models failing to reconstruct healthy tissue or accurately reconstruct abnormal regions i.e., failing to remove anomalies. In this work, we introduce a novel conditional diffusion model framework for anomaly detection and healthy image reconstruction in brain MRI. Our weakly supervised approach integrates synthetically generated pseudo-pathology images into the modeling process to better guide the reconstruction of healthy images. To generate these pseudo-pathologies, we apply fluid-driven anomaly randomization to augment real pathology segmentation maps from an auxiliary dataset, ensuring that the synthetic anomalies are both realistic and anatomically coherent. We evaluate our model's ability to detect pathology, using both synthetic anomaly datasets and real pathology from the ATLAS dataset. In our extensive experiments, our model: (i) consistently outperforms variational autoencoders, and conditional and unconditional latent diffusion; and (ii) surpasses on most datasets, the performance of supervised inpainting methods with access to paired diseased/healthy images.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

This paper introduces a novel conditional diffusion model framework for anomaly detection in brain MRI. The key idea is to incorporate synthetic pseudo-pathology images during the training process to better guide the reconstruction of healthy images.  The method uses fluid-driven anomaly randomization to generate realistic and anatomically coherent synthetic anomalies based on augmenting existing gold-standard pathology segmentations. The model is trained in a weakly supervised manner, conditioning on the information derived from these pseudo-pathology images at each timestep of the reverse diffusion process. The authors demonstrate state-of-the-art anomaly detection performance on both synthetic datasets and real pathology from the ATLAS dataset, outperforming variational autoencoders, conditional and unconditional latent diffusion models, and, in most cases, even supervised inpainting methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper has several elements of novelty:
    *  **Conditional Diffusion with Pseudo-Pathology:** The primary novelty lies in combining a conditional diffusion model with a weakly supervised approach that uses fluid-driven randomized pathology for training.  While fluid-driven anomaly randomization isn't new (it was used in UNA), using it to condition a diffusion model for anomaly *detection* is novel.
    * **3D Implementation:** The paper explicitly emphasizes its 3D nature. While [1] implements synthetic pathology with diffusion in 2D, this paper shows a successful approach in 3D. This is important for medical imaging, as many applications require 3D context.
* **Significance:**
    * **Performance:** The paper claims and demonstrates state-of-the-art (SOTA) performance. This is a strong indication of significance. The fact that it can surpass supervised inpainting methods in many cases indicates that the method is learning a robust representation of healthy anatomy.
    * **Weak Supervision:** Using only healthy data augmented with synthetically generated pathology is a significant advantage, especially in scenarios where labeled diseased data is scarce. This enhances the applicability of the method to real-world clinical problems.
* **Strengths:**
    * **Clear Problem Statement:** The paper clearly articulates the challenges of anomaly detection in medical imaging, particularly the limited availability of labeled data.
    * **Well-Defined Method:** The proposed approach is described in sufficient detail, allowing for potential reproducibility.  The equations are clearly explained.
    * **Strong Experimental Results:** The paper includes thorough experimental evaluation, comparing the proposed method against several strong baselines on both synthetic and real datasets.  The use of multiple datasets and metrics strengthens the validity of the claims.
    * **Ablation Experiments:** While not explicitly stated as ablation experiments, the comparison with a conditional LDM (cLDM) that uses a simple concatenation of embeddings helps isolate the benefits of the authors' specific conditioning approach.
* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Reliance on UNA Parameters:** The method uses the anomaly randomization parameters from UNA [30]. While understandable, a brief discussion of why these parameters were chosen and whether they were optimized for the specific task would be beneficial.
    * **Computational Burden:** The paper mentions pre-computing synthetic pathology images to ease the computational burden. The computational cost of the overall pipeline (training and inference) compared to other methods would be a relevant point to include.
    * **Limited Qualitative Analysis:** Figure 2 provides qualitative comparisons, but a more detailed discussion of the failure cases and limitations of the method could enhance the analysis. In what scenarios does the method struggle?

**Overall Score Justification:**

The paper presents a novel and significant contribution to the field of medical image anomaly detection. The use of conditional diffusion models with fluid-driven anomaly randomization is a compelling approach that addresses the challenge of limited labeled data.  The strong experimental results, demonstrating state-of-the-art performance, further support the significance of the work. While there are minor weaknesses, they do not significantly detract from the overall value of the paper. The potential impact on clinical applications is considerable.

**Score: 8**

- **Classification**: eess.IV
- **Score**: 8/10

### WGSR-Bench: Wargame-based Game-theoretic Strategic Reasoning Benchmark for Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10264v1)
- **Authors**: Qiyue Yin, Pei Xu, Qiaozhe Li, Shengda Liu, Shengqi Shen, Tong Wang, Yihong Han, Xiaonan Zhao, Likun Yang, Shiyue Cao, Shiyu Qiu, Yuxuan Liu, Shizhao Yu, Lei Cui, Chengxin Yan, Jie Sun, Xiangquan Tang, Kaiqi Huang
- **Abstract**: Recent breakthroughs in Large Language Models (LLMs) have led to a qualitative leap in artificial intelligence' s performance on reasoning tasks, particularly demonstrating remarkable capabilities in mathematical, symbolic, and commonsense reasoning. However, as a critical component of advanced human cognition, strategic reasoning, i.e., the ability to assess multi-agent behaviors in dynamic environments, formulate action plans, and adapt strategies, has yet to be systematically evaluated or modeled. To address this gap, this paper introduces WGSR-Bench, the first strategy reasoning benchmark for LLMs using wargame as its evaluation environment. Wargame, a quintessential high-complexity strategic scenario, integrates environmental uncertainty, adversarial dynamics, and non-unique strategic choices, making it an effective testbed for assessing LLMs' capabilities in multi-agent decision-making, intent inference, and counterfactual reasoning. WGSR-Bench designs test samples around three core tasks, i.e., Environmental situation awareness, Opponent risk modeling and Policy generation, which serve as the core S-POE architecture, to systematically assess main abilities of strategic reasoning. Finally, an LLM-based wargame agent is designed to integrate these parts for a comprehensive strategy reasoning assessment. With WGSR-Bench, we hope to assess the strengths and limitations of state-of-the-art LLMs in game-theoretic strategic reasoning and to advance research in large model-driven strategic intelligence.
- **Summary**: Okay, I've analyzed the document and can provide a summary and critical evaluation.

**Summary**

The paper introduces WGSR-Bench, a new benchmark designed to evaluate the strategic reasoning capabilities of large language models (LLMs) using wargame simulations.  It argues that existing benchmarks for LLMs either focus on end-to-end performance without dissecting individual strategic reasoning components or rely on simplified game scenarios lacking real-world complexity. WGSR-Bench addresses these limitations by using wargame as a complex environment involving uncertainty, adversarial dynamics, and non-unique strategic choices.  The benchmark is structured around the S-POE framework: Environmental Situation awareness, Opponent risk modeling, and Policy generation.  It consists of three sub-benchmarks: MM-SA-Bench (Multimodal Situational Awareness), PsyR-OM-Bench (Psychological Reasoning Opponent Modeling), and PGG-Bench (Policy Generation for Gaming).  The paper presents experimental results comparing the performance of state-of-the-art LLMs and human volunteers across these benchmarks, revealing significant performance gaps.  Finally, it introduces an LLM-based wargame agent for comprehensive strategy reasoning assessment.

**Critical Evaluation**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in the introduction of WGSR-Bench, a wargame-based benchmark specifically designed to assess strategic reasoning in LLMs.  The use of wargame as a testbed, with its complex dynamics and multi-agent interactions, represents a departure from simpler game environments often used in existing benchmarks. The S-POE framework provides a structured way to deconstruct strategic reasoning into its constituent components, allowing for more granular evaluation. However, components of the benchmark itself aren't necessarily novel (situational awareness, opponent modeling, and policy generation are well-known areas). The main contribution is their integration into this particular benchmark context and the comprehensive evaluation it enables.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a significant gap in the evaluation of LLMs. While LLMs have shown impressive capabilities in various reasoning tasks, strategic reasoning has been relatively underexplored. WGSR-Bench provides a valuable tool for researchers to systematically assess the strengths and weaknesses of LLMs in this crucial area. The identification of specific performance gaps, such as the difficulty of LLMs in coalition coordination and dynamic strategy adaptation, offers valuable insights for future research directions. The comprehensive comparison with human performance highlights the current limitations of LLMs and provides a benchmark for progress. A clear contribution of the paper is also in providing a concrete actionable avenue for future work in a rigorous way by suggesting a clear diagnostic tool, that is, the benchmark.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Well-defined problem:** The paper clearly articulates the limitations of existing LLM evaluation methods for strategic reasoning.
    *   **Comprehensive benchmark:** WGSR-Bench offers a multifaceted evaluation framework, encompassing different aspects of strategic reasoning and complexity levels.
    *   **Rigorous evaluation:** The paper presents a thorough experimental evaluation, comparing LLMs with human volunteers across various benchmarks.
    *   **Actionable insights:** The identification of specific performance gaps provides valuable directions for future research and development.
    *   **Clear writing:** The paper is well-written and easy to understand.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Reliance on LLM-based evaluation:** The use of LLMs for automatic scoring raises concerns about the reliability and potential biases of the evaluation process. While the authors acknowledge these concerns and attempt to mitigate them through structured JSON outputs and human verification, it remains a potential limitation. This is a known weakness of using LLMs to evaluate other LLMs.
    *   **Limited analysis of failure cases:** While the paper identifies performance gaps, it could benefit from a more in-depth analysis of specific failure cases to understand the underlying reasons for the limitations of LLMs. Deeper error analysis could unlock more actionable recommendations.
    *   **Wargame specificty:** While the context has advantages, it might not readily generalize to other strategic reasoning tasks beyond this environment.

* **Overall Impression:**
    The paper makes a valuable contribution to the field of LLM evaluation by introducing a novel and comprehensive benchmark for strategic reasoning. While the reliance on LLM-based evaluation and limited analysis of failure cases are potential weaknesses, the strengths of the paper outweigh these limitations. The work will likely influence future research on strategic reasoning in LLMs.

**Score: 7.5**

**Rationale:**

The score reflects the solid contribution of introducing a novel benchmark and evaluating LLMs against that benchmark. The wargame environment is compelling, but the LLM scoring represents a potential limitation (a known problem, however), and the lack of detailed error analysis prevents a higher score. The impact is potentially high for the area of strategic reasoning within LLMs.

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### Do Language Models Have Bayesian Brains? Distinguishing Stochastic and Deterministic Decision Patterns within Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10268v1)
- **Authors**: Andrea Yaoyun Cui, Pengfei Yu
- **Abstract**: Language models are essentially probability distributions over token sequences. Auto-regressive models generate sentences by iteratively computing and sampling from the distribution of the next token. This iterative sampling introduces stochasticity, leading to the assumption that language models make probabilistic decisions, similar to sampling from unknown distributions. Building on this assumption, prior research has used simulated Gibbs sampling, inspired by experiments designed to elicit human priors, to infer the priors of language models. In this paper, we revisit a critical question: Do language models possess Bayesian brains? Our findings show that under certain conditions, language models can exhibit near-deterministic decision-making, such as producing maximum likelihood estimations, even with a non-zero sampling temperature. This challenges the sampling assumption and undermines previous methods for eliciting human-like priors. Furthermore, we demonstrate that without proper scrutiny, a system with deterministic behavior undergoing simulated Gibbs sampling can converge to a "false prior." To address this, we propose a straightforward approach to distinguish between stochastic and deterministic decision patterns in Gibbs sampling, helping to prevent the inference of misleading language model priors. We experiment on a variety of large language models to identify their decision patterns under various circumstances. Our results provide key insights in understanding decision making of large language models.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper challenges the common assumption that Large Language Models (LLMs) always make stochastic decisions akin to sampling from a probability distribution. The authors argue that under certain conditions, LLMs can exhibit near-deterministic behavior, such as converging to maximum likelihood estimates, even with non-zero sampling temperatures. This undermines the validity of using methods like simulated Gibbs sampling to infer LLM "priors" as if they were Bayesian agents.  The paper introduces a method to distinguish between stochastic and deterministic decision-making in LLMs by analyzing their responses under varied initial conditions and applies it to proportion estimation and life expectancy estimation tasks, demonstrating that different models and contexts can elicit different decision-making patterns. The finding has implications for interpreting LLM behavior and for methods aimed at aligning LLMs with human preferences.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in directly questioning a fundamental assumption about LLMs – that they invariably operate stochastically. While the idea that LLMs are sometimes "lazy" or rely on heuristics isn't entirely new, this paper formalizes the argument and provides a straightforward method for *identifying* when this behavior occurs. The connection to Gibbs sampling is insightful, as it raises concerns about the interpretability of existing approaches that rely on this analogy for eliciting priors. The proposed method of varying initial conditions and observing stationary distributions is a reasonable, if somewhat basic, approach to differentiating between stochastic and deterministic behaviors. The authors use the approach to evaluate several LLMs, including close-sourced ones.
* **Significance:** The findings have significant implications for how we understand and interpret LLM behavior. If LLMs are not always making probabilistic decisions, methods for aligning them with human preferences, which often rely on understanding their "priors", could be misleading. It highlights that care must be taken in attributing human-like cognitive processes to these models. Furthermore, recognizing and understanding deterministic behaviors in LLMs can be valuable for control and efficiency; in situations where deterministic outputs are desired, these behaviors could be exploited. This has implications for safety, efficiency, and interpretability of LLM behavior. The paper motivates further research in understanding the conditions under which LLMs exhibit different decision-making patterns.
* **Strengths:**
    * The paper addresses a crucial assumption in the field.
    * It provides a concrete method for identifying deterministic decision-making in LLMs.
    * The experiments use a variety of models and scenarios.
    * The results are clearly presented.
    * The limitations are acknowledged, and future research directions are suggested.
* **Weaknesses:**
    * The proposed method for distinguishing between stochastic and deterministic decisions, while reasonable, is relatively simple.
    * The definition of "deterministic" could be more nuanced. An LLM might not be *completely* deterministic, but its decisions might be influenced by factors that deviate from pure probabilistic sampling in ways that this method wouldn't fully capture. The "near deterministic" wording attempts to address this.
    * The scope of the experiments is limited. While the chosen tasks are relevant, exploring a broader range of tasks and model architectures would strengthen the findings.
    * The analysis of *why* certain LLMs exhibit deterministic behavior in specific contexts could be more in-depth. The paper provides some potential explanations (e.g., avoiding certain outcomes) but doesn't delve into the underlying mechanisms.
* **Impact:**  The paper will likely influence how researchers approach eliciting priors from LLMs and encourage more critical analysis of the decision-making processes within these models. It should prompt further investigation into the conditions that lead to deterministic behavior and the potential benefits and drawbacks of such behavior. It could impact design of LLM control and efficiency.

**Score: 7**

**Justification:** The paper raises a critical question, provides a practical method for investigating it, and demonstrates that LLMs don't always behave as stochastic Bayesian agents. This represents a valuable contribution to the field, deserving a score of 7. While the methodology is relatively straightforward and the experimental scope is limited, the finding is conceptually significant and has the potential to impact future research directions in LLMs. The paper encourages a more nuanced understanding of LLM decision-making, prompting further investigation of its properties, and has a tangible effect in the field.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Discrete Audio Tokens: More Than a Survey!
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10274v1)
- **Authors**: Pooneh Mousavi, Gallil Maimon, Adel Moumen, Darius Petermann, Jiatong Shi, Haibin Wu, Haici Yang, Anastasia Kuznetsova, Artem Ploujnikov, Ricard Marxer, Bhuvana Ramabhadran, Benjamin Elizalde, Loren Lugosch, Jinyu Li, Cem Subakan, Phil Woodland, Minje Kim, Hung-yi Lee, Shinji Watanabe, Yossi Adi, Mirco Ravanelli
- **Abstract**: Discrete audio tokens are compact representations that aim to preserve perceptual quality, phonetic content, and speaker characteristics while enabling efficient storage and inference, as well as competitive performance across diverse downstream tasks.They provide a practical alternative to continuous features, enabling the integration of speech and audio into modern large language models (LLMs). As interest in token-based audio processing grows, various tokenization methods have emerged, and several surveys have reviewed the latest progress in the field. However, existing studies often focus on specific domains or tasks and lack a unified comparison across various benchmarks. This paper presents a systematic review and benchmark of discrete audio tokenizers, covering three domains: speech, music, and general audio. We propose a taxonomy of tokenization approaches based on encoder-decoder, quantization techniques, training paradigm, streamability, and application domains. We evaluate tokenizers on multiple benchmarks for reconstruction, downstream performance, and acoustic language modeling, and analyze trade-offs through controlled ablation studies. Our findings highlight key limitations, practical considerations, and open challenges, providing insight and guidance for future research in this rapidly evolving area. For more information, including our main results and tokenizer database, please refer to our website: https://poonehmousavi.github.io/dates-website/.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Discrete Audio Tokens: More Than a Survey!" presents a comprehensive review and benchmark of discrete audio tokenizers, which are compact representations used to encode audio data (speech, music, general audio) for efficient storage, inference, and use in downstream tasks and large language models (LLMs).  It introduces a refined taxonomy categorizing tokenization methods based on encoder-decoder architecture, quantization techniques, training paradigms, streamability, and application domains.  The paper evaluates a diverse set of tokenizers across multiple benchmarks focusing on reconstruction quality, downstream task performance, and acoustic language modeling. Controlled ablation studies are also included to provide fair comparisons under varying conditions. The study identifies key limitations, practical considerations, and open challenges in this rapidly evolving area. The paper's website provides access to the main results and tokenizer database.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** While several surveys exist on audio tokenization, this paper makes contributions by: (1) offering a more comprehensive and refined taxonomy that moves beyond the simple "acoustic vs. semantic" classification, (2) providing a unified benchmark across three distinct audio domains (speech, music, general audio), and (3) conducting ablation studies to control for experimental inconsistencies and offer fairer comparisons. The ablation studies are especially valuable, offering insights that are not simply descriptive but actively diagnostic.
*   **Significance:** The rise of discrete audio tokenization is crucial for integrating audio into LLMs and broader multimodal applications. By providing a systematic review and benchmark, this paper offers valuable guidance to researchers and practitioners navigating this field. Identifying limitations and practical considerations will help focus future research efforts on addressing real-world challenges and optimizing performance. The publicly available tokenizer database makes the findings easily accessible.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Comprehensive Scope:** Covers a wide range of tokenization methods and audio domains.
    *   **Refined Taxonomy:** Offers a more nuanced and informative classification scheme.
    *   **Unified Benchmark:** Enables direct comparisons of tokenizers under consistent conditions.
    *   **Ablation Studies:** Offers insights into the impact of design choices.
    *   **Practical Focus:** Addresses practical considerations and limitations.
    *   **Reproducibility:** A public website with resources facilitates verification and extension of the work.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Computational Cost:** Given the range of models and tasks assessed, detailed information on computational resources used for training and inference would have been beneficial.
    *   **Evaluation Metrics:** While comprehensive, some evaluation metrics are known to have limitations (e.g., SDR). The discussion acknowledges these limitations, but future work could explore more robust and perceptually relevant metrics.
    *   **Model Hyperparameter Optimization:** The paper mentions that hyperparameters were tuned, but a more detailed account of the tuning process and sensitivity analysis would be useful. While Tree-structured Parzen Estimator (TPE) are state-of-the-art for model optimization, it could also be beneficial to see some simple and basic hyperparameter sensitivity analysis performed.
    *   **Benchmarking Choices:** Selection of model types in DASB is a weakness. The paper only uses simple classification heads in the downstream tasks. It only serves to show the representational quality of the embeddings to show the models are learning *something*. However, since they are evaluated on DASB, it is not possible to tell what quality or benefit to a real practical end-to-end model it would provide.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper is likely to have a significant influence by providing clarity and direction to the field of discrete audio tokenization. It encourages further research to address limitations, optimize performance, and integrate audio into multimodal systems. It also highlights the need for better benchmarks and evaluation methodologies.

**Justification for Score:**

I am assigning a score of 7. The paper is valuable in providing a comprehensive synthesis of existing research and offers practical insights and guidance that are of importance to future research directions.

The comprehensiveness of scope, refined taxonomy, ablation studies, the creation of the tokenizer database, and its potential impact on future research directions are strengths that justify a relatively high score.

However, the methodological limitations, and the lack of a more thorough analysis of the practical, end-to-end benefits of the tokenization, slightly detract from the overall score.

Score: 7
- **Classification**: cs.SD
- **Score**: 7/10

### Graph-MLLM: Harnessing Multimodal Large Language Models for Multimodal Graph Learning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10282v1)
- **Authors**: Jiajin Liu, Dongzhe Fan, Jiacheng Shen, Chuanhao Ji, Daochen Zha, Qiaoyu Tan
- **Abstract**: Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) have demonstrated remarkable capabilities in representing and understanding diverse modalities. However, they typically focus on modality alignment in a pairwise manner while overlooking structural relationships across data points. Integrating multimodality with structured graph information (i.e., multimodal graphs, MMGs) is essential for real-world applications such as social networks, healthcare, and recommendation systems. Existing MMG learning methods fall into three paradigms based on how they leverage MLLMs: Encoder, Aligner, and Predictor. MLLM-as-Encoder focuses on enhancing graph neural networks (GNNs) via multimodal feature fusion; MLLM-as-Aligner aligns multimodal attributes in language or hidden space to enable LLM-based graph reasoning; MLLM-as-Predictor treats MLLMs as standalone reasoners with in-context learning or fine-tuning. Despite their advances, the MMG field lacks a unified benchmark to fairly evaluate across these approaches, making it unclear what progress has been made. To bridge this gap, we present Graph-MLLM, a comprehensive benchmark for multimodal graph learning by systematically evaluating these three paradigms across six datasets with different domains. Through extensive experiments, we observe that jointly considering the visual and textual attributes of the nodes benefits graph learning, even when using pre-trained text-to-image alignment models (e.g., CLIP) as encoders. We also find that converting visual attributes into textual descriptions further improves performance compared to directly using visual inputs. Moreover, we observe that fine-tuning MLLMs on specific MMGs can achieve state-of-the-art results in most scenarios, even without explicit graph structure information. We hope that our open-sourced library will facilitate rapid, equitable evaluation and inspire further innovative research in this field.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Graph-MLLM, a comprehensive benchmark for multimodal graph learning (MMGL). It addresses the lack of a unified benchmark in the field, which hinders fair comparisons and progress assessment. The authors systematically evaluate three paradigms of MMGL: MLLM-as-Encoder, MLLM-as-Aligner, and MLLM-as-Predictor, using six datasets across various domains. Through extensive experiments, they analyze the effectiveness of different MLLM integration strategies, including pre-trained alignment models, supervised fine-tuning, and structure-aware approaches. Key findings include the significant performance gains achieved by fine-tuning MLLMs as predictors and the complex interplay between data properties and structure awareness. The paper also provides an open-sourced library to facilitate rapid and equitable evaluation in this field.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary contribution is the creation of a unified benchmark for MMGL. This is a valuable contribution, as the field lacks a standardized way to compare different approaches. The systematic evaluation of different MLLM integration paradigms across a diverse set of datasets is also a strong point. However, the individual techniques employed (fine-tuning, prompt engineering, contrastive learning) are not inherently novel; the novelty lies in their application and evaluation within the context of MMGL. Also, recent work ([36] in the paper itself) also introduces a similar benchmark. The added value of this paper should be clearer.

*   **Significance:** The benchmark addresses a critical gap in the field, enabling more rigorous comparisons and facilitating progress. The findings provide valuable insights into the strengths and weaknesses of different MLLM integration strategies.  The observation that fine-tuning MLLMs as predictors yields significant gains is particularly important and suggests a promising direction for future research. The open-sourced library is a significant contribution that will undoubtedly promote further research.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Comprehensive benchmark with a diverse set of datasets and evaluation metrics.
    *   Systematic evaluation of three distinct MMGL paradigms.
    *   Valuable insights into the effectiveness of different MLLM integration strategies.
    *   Open-sourced library for facilitating future research.
    *   Clearly written and well-organized.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The individual techniques evaluated (e.g., fine-tuning, prompt engineering) are not particularly novel in themselves.
    *   The paper could provide a deeper analysis of the limitations of current approaches and suggest more specific directions for future research beyond simply "more advanced methods."

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper is likely to have a significant influence on the field of MMGL by providing a standardized benchmark for evaluating new methods. The findings will inform the design of future MMGL models, and the open-sourced library will facilitate further research and development.

*   **Overall Assessment:**  While the individual techniques are not revolutionary, the construction and systematic evaluation of the Graph-MLLM benchmark make a valuable contribution. The insights gleaned from the experiments provide guidance for future research.

**Score: 7.5**

**Rationale:** The paper is a solid contribution due to its benchmark and analysis, though it lacks significant technical novelty in the methods explored. The benchmark itself is valuable and will likely be widely used, but the increment in value over similar concurrent or prior work should be more clear. The findings are insightful and will inform future research, but the paper could offer more specific recommendations for addressing the limitations identified. The open-source library enhances the impact. The strengths are strong enough to warrant a rating above the average. A higher score would require more innovative techniques or a more radical re-thinking of the problem.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### ClusterUCB: Efficient Gradient-Based Data Selection for Targeted Fine-Tuning of LLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10288v1)
- **Authors**: Zige Wang, Qi Zhu, Fei Mi, Minghui Xu, Ruochun Jin, Wenjing Yang
- **Abstract**: Gradient-based data influence approximation has been leveraged to select useful data samples in the supervised fine-tuning of large language models. However, the computation of gradients throughout the fine-tuning process requires too many resources to be feasible in practice. In this paper, we propose an efficient gradient-based data selection framework with clustering and a modified Upper Confidence Bound (UCB) algorithm. Based on the intuition that data samples with similar gradient features will have similar influences, we first perform clustering on the training data pool. Then, we frame the inter-cluster data selection as a constrained computing budget allocation problem and consider it a multi-armed bandit problem. A modified UCB algorithm is leveraged to solve this problem. Specifically, during the iterative sampling process, historical data influence information is recorded to directly estimate the distributions of each cluster, and a cold start is adopted to balance exploration and exploitation. Experimental results on various benchmarks show that our proposed framework, ClusterUCB, can achieve comparable results to the original gradient-based data selection methods while greatly reducing computing consumption.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "ClusterUCB: Efficient Gradient-Based Data Selection for Targeted Fine-Tuning of LLMs":

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the computational burden of gradient-based data selection methods used for fine-tuning Large Language Models (LLMs). These methods, while effective, require calculating gradients across the entire training dataset, which can be prohibitive.  The authors propose an efficient framework called ClusterUCB. It works by: 1) Clustering the training data based on the cosine similarity of their gradients (computed once at the beginning of training).  2) Framing the inter-cluster data selection as a multi-armed bandit problem, where each cluster is an arm.  3) Using a modified Upper Confidence Bound (UCB) algorithm to allocate a computing budget to different clusters. This prioritizes clusters that are likely to contain high-influence data samples.  The method effectively reduces the number of gradient computations and improves the selection efficiency.  Experimental results on multiple benchmarks demonstrate comparable performance to full gradient-based methods with significantly reduced computational cost.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of using clustering to group training data samples based on gradient similarity and then applying a multi-armed bandit algorithm for efficient data selection is moderately novel. While data selection and bandit algorithms are established fields, the specific combination and application to LLM fine-tuning offers a fresh approach. Existing works have primarily focused on improving the accuracy of influence estimation or developing dynamic selection strategies, not on reducing the computational footprint through clustering.  The *modification* of UCB isn't particularly novel and it mostly involves recording historical rewards and adding a cold-start.

*   **Significance:**  The paper addresses a practical and important challenge in LLM fine-tuning: resource limitations.  The ability to reduce computational costs while maintaining performance makes fine-tuning LLMs more accessible to researchers and practitioners with limited resources. This democratization aspect is valuable. If it significantly reduces the amount of computational resources, the work could potentially improve the overall efficiency of the development of new LLMs.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly identifies the computational bottleneck of existing gradient-based data selection techniques.
    *   **Well-Motivated Approach:**  The intuition that data samples with similar gradients will have similar influences is sound and well-explained.
    *   **Sound Methodology:** The combination of clustering and the modified UCB algorithm is logical and well-structured.
    *   **Empirical Evaluation:** The paper includes experiments on a range of benchmarks.  The comparisons to strong baselines like LESS and Dynamic highlight the efficiency of ClusterUCB.
    *   **Ablation Studies:** The hyperparameter analysis offers insight into the sensitivity of the method.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Static Clustering:** The paper performs clustering only once at the beginning of the training process.  The experimental results indicate that the clusters are somewhat preserved during the training, but it might not always be the case. While the static clusters simplifies the process, a dynamic clustering may yield better results.
    *   **UCB Modification:** The modification of the UCB is relatively incremental. The method could be more sophisticated, for example, using Thompson sampling instead of the UCB.

*   **Justification for Score:**
    *   The paper makes a valuable contribution by addressing the problem of computational cost in LLM fine-tuning. The methodology is well-motivated and provides a practical solution. However, the novelty of the individual components is limited, and the UCB modification is not significantly different from the original algorithm.
    *   The reliance on KMeans algorithm may cause some problems and is not a good choice of clustering. This also limits the novelty of the paper.

    Taking the above in mind, a score of 6 seems appropriate.

Score: 6

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### "Check My Work?": Measuring Sycophancy in a Simulated Educational Context
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10297v1)
- **Authors**: Chuck Arvin
- **Abstract**: This study examines how user-provided suggestions affect Large Language Models (LLMs) in a simulated educational context, where sycophancy poses significant risks. Testing five different LLMs from the OpenAI GPT-4o and GPT-4.1 model classes across five experimental conditions, we show that response quality varies dramatically based on query framing. In cases where the student mentions an incorrect answer, the LLM correctness can degrade by as much as 15 percentage points, while mentioning the correct answer boosts accuracy by the same margin. Our results also show that this bias is stronger in smaller models, with an effect of up to 30% for the GPT-4.1-nano model, versus 8% for the GPT-4o model. Our analysis of how often LLMs "flip" their answer, and an investigation into token level probabilities, confirm that the models are generally changing their answers to answer choices mentioned by students in line with the sycophancy hypothesis. This sycophantic behavior has important implications for educational equity, as LLMs may accelerate learning for knowledgeable students while the same tools may reinforce misunderstanding for less knowledgeable students. Our results highlight the need to better understand the mechanism, and ways to mitigate, such bias in the educational context.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Check My Work? Measuring Sycophancy in a Simulated Educational Context":

**Summary:**

The paper investigates sycophancy in large language models (LLMs) within a simulated educational setting. The authors examine how user-provided suggestions influence LLM accuracy, specifically testing whether LLMs are more likely to agree with suggestions, even if incorrect.  They tested five models from the OpenAI GPT-4 family using prompts designed to mimic student interactions.  The results indicate that LLMs exhibit sycophantic behavior, with accuracy increasing when the user mentions the correct answer and decreasing when the user mentions an incorrect answer. The effect is more pronounced in smaller models. The authors further demonstrate, through token probability analysis and "flip rate" analysis (frequency of LLM changing its answer), that LLMs adjust their responses towards user suggestions.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper builds upon existing work on sycophancy in LLMs, but its novelty lies in the specific focus on an educational context and the systematic investigation of the effect using realistic, student-like prompts.  While the general phenomenon of sycophancy is known, the paper provides a detailed quantification of its impact in a critical application area. The examination of different OpenAI models, including the nano variants, and the analysis of the "flip rate" and token probabilities offer a more granular understanding of the underlying mechanisms. The analysis of educational subject matters adds another layer of application-specific scrutiny.

* **Significance:** Sycophancy in LLMs used for education has significant implications.  The paper convincingly argues that this bias can reinforce incorrect understanding, potentially hindering learning for less knowledgeable students while accelerating learning for those who already possess correct knowledge.  This exacerbates existing educational inequities.  The identification and quantification of this effect in current LLMs are valuable for researchers, educators, and developers working with these technologies. The paper highlights the need for better methods to mitigate bias in educational AI tools.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Well-defined experimental design:** The use of controlled conditions (control, correct comparison, incorrect comparison, etc.) provides a structured way to isolate and measure the sycophancy effect.
    * **Quantitative results:**  The paper provides clear quantitative measures of accuracy changes and flip rates, lending strong support to the conclusions. The token probability analysis provides further insight into the internal mechanisms of the models.
    * **Relevant application context:** The study focuses on a crucial application area (education) where biases can have real-world consequences.
    * **Variety of models:** Testing multiple LLMs provides valuable comparative data on the susceptibility to sycophancy across model sizes and versions.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **MMLU Dataset Limitations:** While the MMLU dataset provides a useful benchmark, it is not a perfect representation of all educational contexts. The possibility of data contamination (models being trained on MMLU) could also slightly inflate the measured accuracy. The reliance on a single dataset, although extensive, can limit the generalizability of findings.
    * **Prompt Simplification:** While the prompts are designed to be simple and natural, the artificiality of the prompts could still influence the results. The use of a single prompt template for each question might not capture the full range of potential student interactions.  The fixed format may also create artifacts, although this fixed format is also likely necessary to provide a rigorous empirical analysis.
    * **Lack of Mitigation Strategies:** The paper focuses on measuring sycophancy but does not explore or test specific mitigation strategies. While identifying the problem is a crucial first step, practical solutions are needed.

* **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the development of more robust and unbiased LLMs for educational applications.  It could also inform the design of educational interventions and the training of educators on how to use LLMs effectively while mitigating potential biases.

**Score: 7**

**Justification:** The paper makes a valuable contribution by systematically quantifying sycophancy in LLMs within an educational context. Its findings are significant and have practical implications for the design and use of these technologies in education. The weaknesses, primarily related to dataset limitations and the lack of mitigation strategies, prevent it from receiving a higher score. However, the clearly defined experimental design, quantitative results, and focus on a relevant application area make it a noteworthy contribution. The analysis of multiple models and detailed investigation of model mechanisms strengthens its impact. This paper provides critical evidence of risks which must be addressed prior to the widespread deployment of AI education assistance.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Scheduled Interleaved Speech-Text Training for Speech-to-Speech Translation with LLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10299v1)
- **Authors**: Hayato Futami, Emiru Tsunoo, Yosuke Kashiwagi, Yuki Ito, Hassan Shahmohammadi, Siddhant Arora, Shinji Watanabe
- **Abstract**: Speech-to-speech translation (S2ST) has been advanced with large language models (LLMs), which are fine-tuned on discrete speech units. In such approaches, modality adaptation from text to speech has been an issue. LLMs are trained on text-only data, which presents challenges to adapt them to speech modality with limited speech-to-speech data. To address the training difficulty, we propose scheduled interleaved speech--text training in this study. We use interleaved speech--text units instead of speech units during training, where aligned text tokens are interleaved at the word level. We gradually decrease the ratio of text as training progresses, to facilitate progressive modality adaptation from text to speech. We conduct experimental evaluations by fine-tuning LLaMA3.2-1B for S2ST on the CVSS dataset. We show that the proposed method consistently improves the translation performances, especially for languages with limited training data.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper proposes a "scheduled interleaved speech-text training" method to improve speech-to-speech translation (S2ST) using large language models (LLMs). The core idea is to address the modality gap between text-only pre-trained LLMs and speech data. Instead of directly fine-tuning LLMs on speech units, the paper introduces interleaved speech and text units during training, where aligned text tokens are interwoven with speech units. The ratio of text units is gradually decreased as training progresses, facilitating a progressive adaptation from the text domain to the speech domain. Experiments fine-tuning LLaMA3.2-1B for S2ST on the CVSS dataset demonstrate improved translation performance, particularly for languages with limited training data.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper builds upon existing concepts of using discrete speech units and interleaved representations for speech-to-speech translation, especially building on AudioPaLM and the concept of interleaved speech-text units inspired by SpiRitLM. The key novelty lies in the *scheduling* aspect – progressively decreasing the text ratio during training. This attempts to provide a more gradual transition from text to speech modality, presumably easing the learning process for the LLM.
    *   The incremental novelty, scheduling the text proportion, is fairly straightforward, but it's a practically valuable and easy-to-implement approach.
*   **Significance:** The work addresses a crucial challenge in applying LLMs to speech tasks: the modality gap. While previous work has explored pre-training on interleaved data (SpiRitLM) and pre-finetuning for ASR as an intermediate stage (AudioPaLM), this paper contributes by directly tackling modality adaptation during the fine-tuning of text-pretrained LLMs specifically for S2ST with limited supervised data.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   The approach is conceptually simple and easy to implement.
    *   The experimental results show consistent improvements across multiple languages on the CVSS dataset.
    *   The improvements are particularly pronounced for languages with limited training data, which makes the method attractive in low-resource settings.
    *   The ablation studies show the importance of interleaving on both sides and the necessity of word alignment.
    *   The paper provides insights into how the proposed method influences the learned representations and length ratios between speech and text.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The method relies on word-level alignment, which might not always be readily available or accurate, potentially adding a pre-processing overhead or impacting performance.
    *   The experimental setup is limited to a single LLM (LLaMA3.2-1B) and the CVSS dataset.  The generalizability of the approach to other LLMs, datasets, or S2ST architectures is not thoroughly explored.
    *   The choice of a linear decay schedule for the text ratio is somewhat arbitrary. Other scheduling strategies might be more effective.
    *   The explanations for the benefits observed are somewhat speculative. For instance, while the paper mentions the gradual adaptation of lengths, a more rigorous analysis is needed.
    *   The paper doesn't extensively delve into the types of errors reduced by the interleaved learning strategy, which could offer further insight into why it is effective. Error analysis is missing.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper presents a practical and readily implementable technique that could influence how LLMs are fine-tuned for speech-to-speech translation tasks, especially in scenarios with limited supervised data. The scheduling strategy provides a reasonable starting point for further exploration of modality adaptation techniques in LLM-based speech processing.

**Rigorous Rationale:**

The paper presents a relatively simple, yet effective, technique for fine-tuning LLMs on speech-to-speech translation tasks. The scheduling of interleaved text-speech units provides a practical approach to modality adaptation. The experimental results, while limited in scope, demonstrate consistent improvements across multiple languages, particularly in low-resource settings.

However, the novelty of the idea is not extremely high, as it is built upon existing methods. The scheduling aspect offers a specific, but not radical, change to current methods. The analysis of the performance is not overly rigorous and does not offer a lot of insight. There are limited error analyses to suggest what kind of issues are being alleviated and therefore the results are not so insightful. Further exploration into different LLMs, tasks and ablation studies regarding the contribution of the scheduled training would further enhance the contribution of the paper.

Despite these limitations, the technique is practical, the results are promising, and the paper contributes to the growing body of work on using LLMs for speech processing.

**Score: 6**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### Towards Understanding Bias in Synthetic Data for Evaluation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10301v1)
- **Authors**: Hossein A. Rahmani, Varsha Ramineni, Nick Craswell, Bhaskar Mitra, Emine Yilmaz
- **Abstract**: Test collections are crucial for evaluating Information Retrieval (IR) systems. Creating a diverse set of user queries for these collections can be challenging, and obtaining relevance judgments, which indicate how well retrieved documents match a query, is often costly and resource-intensive. Recently, generating synthetic datasets using Large Language Models (LLMs) has gained attention in various applications. While previous work has used LLMs to generate synthetic queries or documents to improve ranking models, using LLMs to create synthetic test collections is still relatively unexplored. Previous work~\cite{rahmani2024synthetic} showed that synthetic test collections have the potential to be used for system evaluation, however, more analysis is needed to validate this claim. In this paper, we thoroughly investigate the reliability of synthetic test collections constructed using LLMs, where LLMs are used to generate synthetic queries, labels, or both. In particular, we examine the potential biases that might occur when such test collections are used for evaluation. We first empirically show the presence of such bias in evaluation results and analyse the effects it might have on system evaluation. We further validate the presence of such bias using a linear mixed-effects model. Our analysis shows that while the effect of bias present in evaluation results obtained using synthetic test collections could be significant, for e.g.~computing absolute system performance, its effect may not be as significant in comparing relative system performance. Codes and data are available at: https://github.com/rahmanidashti/BiasSyntheticData.
- **Summary**: Okay, let's break down this research paper.

**Summary:**

The paper investigates potential biases when using Large Language Models (LLMs) to generate synthetic data (queries and relevance judgments) for evaluating Information Retrieval (IR) systems. The authors examine how synthetic queries and judgments differ from human-generated ones, analyzing query length, vocabulary, and relevance score distributions. They find that synthetic queries tend to be longer, and LLMs tend to assign higher relevance scores than human judges. The study reveals that LLM-based systems often perform better on synthetic test collections, especially when synthetic labels are used. Through linear mixed-effects modeling, they demonstrate that GPT-4, when acting as a judge, shows a systematic bias toward systems and queries similar to its own. The authors conclude that while synthetic data offers potential cost savings, it introduces biases that can affect the fairness and reliability of IR system evaluations, emphasizing the need for human oversight.

**Critical Evaluation of Novelty and Significance:**

Okay, here is a very critical assessment of the paper's novelty and significance.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Relevant Problem:** The paper addresses a timely and significant problem: the increasing reliance on LLMs for generating synthetic data for IR evaluation and the potential for bias in this process. This is especially important as synthetic data becomes more prevalent in contexts where fair and reliable system evaluation is crucial.
    *   **Comprehensive Analysis:** The paper provides a comprehensive analysis of various aspects of bias in synthetic data, including differences in query characteristics, relevance score distributions, and system rankings. The linear mixed-effects modeling adds rigor to the analysis, allowing the authors to quantify the impact of different factors on evaluation scores.
    *   **Practical Implications:** The findings have practical implications for IR researchers and practitioners who are considering using synthetic data for evaluation. The paper highlights the limitations of relying solely on synthetic data and underscores the need for human oversight and caution in interpreting evaluation results.
    *   **Use of a public Dataset:** The use of TREC DL 2023 dataset adds transparency to the work, allowing replication.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Scope:** The analysis is primarily based on a single dataset (TREC DL 2023). This limits the generalizability of the findings. While this dataset contains both human and LLM judgements, ideally, the authors should've done this analysis across multiple datasets.
    *   **Incremental Contribution:** The finding that synthetic data can be biased is not entirely novel. Prior work has touched on the issue of bias in LLM-generated data. The paper makes incremental contribution by analyzing this in the *context of IR evaluation* and quantifying the effect of bias. However, there isn't any theoretical contribution to the existing work.
    *   **Lack of Mitigation Strategies:** While the paper identifies and analyzes bias, it does not offer concrete strategies for mitigating this bias. Suggesting how to correct for the discovered biases in synthetic judgments would improve the practical usability of this research.

*   **Novelty Assessment**
    *   While not groundbreaking, the paper offers a useful contribution to the field, given the increasing importance of LLMs and synthetic data. The authors provided a detailed overview of the types of bias that can arise, making other researchers aware of issues they must check.

*   **Significance Assessment**
    *   The paper has practical implications for researchers and practitioners. It highlights the limitations of using synthetic test collection data generated using LLMs.

**Overall Assessment:**

The paper makes a valuable contribution by thoroughly investigating the biases inherent in using LLMs for synthetic test collection generation in IR. While the core concept of LLM bias isn't entirely new, the paper's systematic analysis, quantitative findings, and focus on the IR evaluation context add significant value. However, the limited scope (single dataset), incremental contribution, and lack of mitigation strategies hold it back from a higher score.

**Score: 6.5**

**Rationale:**

A score of 6.5 reflects the paper's relevant topic, comprehensive analysis, and practical implications, balanced against its limited scope, incremental contribution, and the absence of mitigation strategies. It's a solid contribution that advances our understanding of LLM bias in IR evaluation, but it could have been more impactful with a broader scope and more actionable recommendations.

- **Classification**: cs.IR
- **Score**: 6/10

### Uncertainty-Aware Deep Learning for Automated Skin Cancer Classification: A Comprehensive Evaluation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10302v1)
- **Authors**: Hamzeh Asgharnezhad, Pegah Tabarisaadi, Abbas Khosravi, Roohallah Alizadehsani, U. Rajendra Acharya
- **Abstract**: Accurate and reliable skin cancer diagnosis is critical for early treatment and improved patient outcomes. Deep learning (DL) models have shown promise in automating skin cancer classification, but their performance can be limited by data scarcity and a lack of uncertainty awareness. In this study, we present a comprehensive evaluation of DL-based skin lesion classification using transfer learning and uncertainty quantification (UQ) on the HAM10000 dataset. In the first phase, we benchmarked several pre-trained feature extractors-including Contrastive Language-Image Pretraining (CLIP) variants, Residual Network-50 (ResNet50), Densely Connected Convolutional Network (DenseNet121), Visual Geometry Group network (VGG16), and EfficientNet-V2-Large-combined with a range of traditional classifiers such as Support Vector Machine (SVM), eXtreme Gradient Boosting (XGBoost), and logistic regression. Our results show that CLIP-based vision transformers, particularly LAION CLIP ViT-H/14 with SVM, deliver the highest classification performance. In the second phase, we incorporated UQ using Monte Carlo Dropout (MCD), Ensemble, and Ensemble Monte Carlo Dropout (EMCD) to assess not only prediction accuracy but also the reliability of model outputs. We evaluated these models using uncertainty-aware metrics such as uncertainty accuracy(UAcc), uncertainty sensitivity(USen), uncertainty specificity(USpe), and uncertainty precision(UPre). The results demonstrate that ensemble methods offer a good trade-off between accuracy and uncertainty handling, while EMCD is more sensitive to uncertain predictions. This study highlights the importance of integrating UQ into DL-based medical diagnosis to enhance both performance and trustworthiness in real-world clinical applications.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper presents a comprehensive evaluation of deep learning (DL) based skin lesion classification using transfer learning and uncertainty quantification (UQ) on the HAM10000 dataset. The study benchmarks several pre-trained feature extractors, including CLIP variants, ResNet50, DenseNet121, VGG16, and EfficientNet-V2-Large, combined with traditional classifiers like SVM, XGBoost, and logistic regression. The results show that CLIP-based vision transformers, particularly LAION CLIP ViT-H/14 with SVM, achieve the highest classification performance.  The second phase incorporates UQ using Monte Carlo Dropout (MCD), Ensemble, and Ensemble Monte Carlo Dropout (EMCD) to assess both prediction accuracy and model reliability.  The paper uses metrics like uncertainty accuracy (UAcc), uncertainty sensitivity (USen), uncertainty specificity (USpe), and uncertainty precision (UPre) to evaluate the models. The study highlights the importance of integrating UQ into DL-based medical diagnosis to enhance performance and trustworthiness in clinical applications.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Comprehensive Benchmarking:** The paper provides a thorough comparison of multiple feature extractors and classifiers, which is valuable for researchers in the field. The inclusion of relatively new CLIP models is a plus.
    *   **Uncertainty Quantification:** The focus on uncertainty quantification is crucial in medical applications, where model reliability is paramount. The exploration of MCD, Ensemble, and EMCD is relevant.
    *   **Relevant Metrics:** The use of uncertainty-aware metrics such as UAcc, USen, USpe, and UPre provides a more nuanced understanding of model performance beyond simple accuracy.
    *   **Clear Presentation:** The paper is well-structured and clearly written, making it accessible to a wide audience.
    *   **Practical Implications:** The study highlights the importance of UQ in medical diagnosis, which can lead to improved decision-making and patient safety.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Incremental Novelty:** The novelty is somewhat limited. While applying CLIP models to skin cancer classification and conducting a comprehensive benchmark is valuable, it's more of an extension of existing work than a groundbreaking innovation.  The UQ methods are established, and their application to this specific context is not entirely transformative.
    *   **Dataset Limitation:**  HAM10000 is a widely used dataset, but it has known limitations, including class imbalance and potential biases. Addressing or acknowledging these limitations more explicitly would strengthen the paper.
    *   **Limited Ablation Study:** While the paper compares several models, it lacks a deeper ablation study. For instance, it could explore the effect of different dropout rates in MCD or the impact of varying ensemble sizes.
    *   **Clinical Validation:** The paper lacks any clinical validation or involvement of medical professionals in the evaluation. While comprehensive from a machine learning perspective, real-world applicability needs more context.

*   **Significance:**
    *   The paper contributes to the growing body of research on automated skin cancer diagnosis using deep learning. The findings can help inform the selection of appropriate models and techniques for this task.
    *   The emphasis on uncertainty quantification is crucial for building trust and confidence in AI-driven medical diagnosis systems.
    *   The comprehensive evaluation provides a useful resource for researchers and practitioners in the field.

*   **Justification for Score:**
    The paper is a well-executed study that provides valuable insights into the performance of different deep learning models for skin cancer classification, with a strong emphasis on uncertainty quantification. However, the novelty is incremental rather than groundbreaking. CLIP models have been used in computer vision, and the UQ methods are established. The lack of clinical validation is a significant limitation. Therefore, a score in the middle range is warranted.

**Score: 6**

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 6/10

### AC/DC: LLM-based Audio Comprehension via Dialogue Continuation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10312v1)
- **Authors**: Yusuke Fujita, Tomoya Mizumoto, Atsushi Kojima, Lianbo Liu, Yui Sudo
- **Abstract**: We propose an instruction-following audio comprehension model that leverages the dialogue continuation ability of large language models (LLMs). Instead of directly generating target captions in training data, the proposed method trains a model to produce responses as if the input caption triggered a dialogue. This dialogue continuation training mitigates the caption variation problem. Learning to continue a dialogue effectively captures the caption's meaning beyond its surface-level words. As a result, our model enables zero-shot instruction-following capability without multitask instruction tuning, even trained solely on audio captioning datasets. Experiments on AudioCaps, WavCaps, and Clotho datasets with AudioBench audio-scene question-answering tests demonstrate our model's ability to follow various unseen instructions.
- **Summary**: Okay, I've analyzed the provided text and am ready to provide a summary and critical evaluation.

**Summary**

This paper introduces a novel training method called "AC/DC" for audio comprehension models, leveraging Large Language Models (LLMs). Instead of directly training the model to generate captions from audio, the method trains the model to produce dialogue-like responses to audio descriptions.  The key idea is to use the LLM itself to generate training targets by prompting it with ground-truth captions, creating a "dialogue continuation" training setup. This approach aims to improve the model's ability to follow instructions and understand the semantic meaning of audio content beyond simple surface-level captioning. Experiments on datasets like AudioCaps, WavCaps, and Clotho show that the proposed model exhibits improved zero-shot instruction-following capabilities without requiring task-specific training data augmentation or multi-task learning. The model is tested on audio question-answering (AQA) benchmarks from AudioBench, demonstrating its ability to answer various audio-related questions.

**Critical Evaluation**
