The paper introduces LayoutCoder, a novel MLLM-based framework for automating UI2Code (converting user interfaces to code) for real-world web pages. It addresses the challenges of complex UI layouts and accurate code generation with layout preservation. LayoutCoder consists of three modules: (1) Element Relation Construction (identifying and grouping UI components with similar structures), (2) UI Layout Parsing (generating UI layout trees), and (3) Layout-Guided Code Fusion (producing accurate code with preserved layout). The paper introduces a new benchmark dataset, Snap2Code, comprising real-world websites, along with the well known Design2Code dataset, to evaluate the framework. Experimental results show that LayoutCoder outperforms state-of-the-art approaches in terms of BLEU score and CLIP score. The paper also includes ablation studies and human evaluations to demonstrate the effectiveness of individual components and overall performance.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in the specific combination of techniques and the comprehensive framework design. While the individual components (MLLMs, UI element detection, image segmentation) are not entirely new, their integration into a cohesive system optimized for UI2Code is a significant contribution. The development of the Snap2Code dataset is also a valuable contribution, since most earlier works do not leverage real-world websites. In contrast to current works which use datasets generated with the help of AI, or simple datasets such as Design2Code, which replaces images with placeholders, Snap2Code includes more complex structure and images. The novel combination of grouping structural and relational UI elements helps prevent the layout models from over segmenting the UI.
*   **Significance:** UI2Code automation is a significant problem in web development, and the paper addresses a critical gap in handling complex layouts and preserving visual fidelity. The improved performance demonstrated by LayoutCoder, compared to existing methods, suggests a meaningful advancement in the field. The comprehensive evaluation including ablation studies and human assessment adds credibility to the results. The framework enables users to understand complex designs through the UI layout trees, which is a significant design feature for UI2Code. The use of both BLEU and CLIP scores is helpful for evaluating textual and visual quality.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   Well-structured and clearly written.
    *   Comprehensive framework with a logical flow.
    *   Introduction of a new, more realistic benchmark dataset (Snap2Code).
    *   Strong empirical results demonstrating improved performance.
    *   Ablation studies providing insights into the importance of individual components.
    *   Human evaluation validating the qualitative improvements.
    *   Comprehensive comparison against related work highlighting key differences and improvements.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   While the approach uses MLLMs, it doesn't deeply explore or adapt the MLLM architecture itself; it mainly uses them as a component.  This could be seen as a missed opportunity for even greater performance gains.
    *   The reliance on existing UI element detection (UIED) could be a limitation. The overall framework's performance is directly dependent on the accuracy of the UIED stage.
    *   While Snap2Code is more realistic than previous datasets, there is still a gap between the websites used in the Snap2Code dataset compared to real-world professional websites.
    *   The improvements, though statistically significant, might not always translate to drastic visual differences in all generated UIs.
    *   A small number of participants in the human evaluation is a weakness. Larger and more diverse groups will provide more robust insight.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the field by:
    *   Providing a new state-of-the-art approach for UI2Code automation.
    *   Highlighting the importance of layout understanding in UI2Code.
    *   Providing a new benchmark dataset for evaluating UI2Code models.
    *   Inspiring further research on MLLM-based approaches for UI2Code.
    *   Providing clear comparisons between approaches, and an analysis into which components are key.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a valuable contribution to the field of UI2Code automation. The LayoutCoder framework addresses a critical problem (complex UI layouts) and demonstrates tangible improvements over existing methods. The comprehensive evaluation and the introduction of the Snap2Code dataset add significant value. While the paper doesn't introduce revolutionary new techniques, the novel combination of components, the optimized design for UI2Code, and thorough evaluation justify a high score. The limitations, especially regarding the shallow exploration of MLLMs, and human evaluation, prevent it from achieving a higher score.

### Chance and Mass Interpretations of Probabilities in Markov Decision Processes (Extended Version)
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10377v1)
- **Authors**: Yun Chen Tsai, Kittiphon Phalakarn, S. Akshay, Ichiro Hasuo
- **Abstract**: Markov decision processes (MDPs) are a popular model for decision-making in the presence of uncertainty. The conventional view of MDPs in verification treats them as state transformers with probabilities defined over sequences of states and with schedulers making random choices. An alternative view, especially well-suited for modeling dynamical systems, defines MDPs as distribution transformers with schedulers distributing probability masses. Our main contribution is a unified semantical framework that accommodates these two views and two new ones. These four semantics of MDPs arise naturally through identifying different sources of randomness in an MDP (namely schedulers, configurations, and transitions) and providing different ways of interpreting these probabilities (called the chance and mass interpretations). These semantics are systematically unified through a mathematical construct called chance-mass (CM) classifier. As another main contribution, we study a reachability problem in each of the two new semantics, demonstrating their hardness and providing two algorithms for solving them.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper presents a unified semantic framework for Markov Decision Processes (MDPs) by considering different interpretations of probabilities arising from schedulers and transitions. It identifies four distinct semantics: (CS, CT), (MS, CT), (CS, MT), and (MS, MT), based on whether probabilities are treated as "chance" (random events) or "mass" (rates of splitting). The paper systematically unifies these semantics using a construct called the "Chance-Mass (CM) classifier." It investigates the threshold reachability problem under the two new semantics (CS, MT) and (MS, CT), demonstrating their complexity and providing algorithms. A key contribution lies in showing undecidability for the (CS, MT) case, developing an antichain-based algorithm, and proving #P-hardness for the (MS, CT) case, along with a template-based synthesis algorithm. Prototype implementations validate the feasibility of the proposed algorithms.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in providing a structured and unified way to view different semantics for MDPs, particularly those relevant to dynamical systems. While the individual "chance" and "mass" interpretations have been explored before (especially in dynamical systems and AI, respectively), the explicit categorization and unification using CM classifiers is a significant contribution. Also, analyzing the reachability problem under the newly defined (MS, CT) and (CS, MT) semantics is novel. The negative results (undecidability and #P-hardness) are also important contributions.
*   **Significance:** The work's significance stems from bridging the gap between traditional MDP verification (state transformer view) and distribution transformer semantics (used in dynamical systems and AI).  The unified framework could lead to better tools and techniques that leverage both perspectives. Furthermore, highlighting the complexity of reachability under the new semantics and providing solution approaches paves the way for future research in this area. The work opens the door for defining algorithms and tool support applicable to different application domains.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear and comprehensive framework:** The CM classifier provides an elegant way to differentiate and relate various MDP semantics.
    *   **Rigorous analysis:** The complexity results for the new semantics are well-supported and offer valuable insights.
    *   **Algorithmic contributions:** The antichain-based and template-based algorithms provide concrete approaches to solving the threshold reachability problem in specific cases.
    *   **Practical validation:** The prototype implementation helps demonstrate the feasibility of the proposed algorithms.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Algorithms are somewhat limited:** The antichain-based and template-based algorithms are not complete solutions and their complexity might be a barrier to wider adoption. It remains to be seen whether these approaches can be scaled to larger, more complex models.
    *   **Lack of a "killer app" application:** While examples are provided, there is no compelling real-world application that definitively demonstrates the practical benefits of using the new semantics compared to existing approaches. The benefits are more apparent from a theoretical understanding.
    *   **The evaluation is not sufficiently rigorous**: Some details are skipped (e.g., the choice of test parameters). The results are not comprehensive and no comparison to previous results can be made, which hinders the impact of the paper.

*   **Potential Impact:** The paper is likely to influence future research in MDPs, particularly in areas involving dynamical systems, AI, and probabilistic verification. The unified framework could lead to novel tools and techniques. It increases theoretical understanding even if algorithms are currently incomplete.

**Score: 7**

**Rationale:** The paper offers a valuable contribution by unifying different perspectives on MDP semantics and by highlighting the complexities of the reachability problem under the new semantics. While the algorithms presented are not complete and the lack of a definitive application limits the practical impact, the theoretical contributions and clear framework warrant a score of 7. The novelty and rigor are strong, but the lack of real-world impact and limitations of the proposed algorithms prevent a higher score.

- **Classification**: cs.FL
- **Score**: 7/10

### ReconMOST: Multi-Layer Sea Temperature Reconstruction with Observations-Guided Diffusion
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10391v1)
- **Authors**: Yuanyi Song, Pumeng Lyu, Ben Fei, Fenghua Ling, Wanli Ouyang, Lei Bai
- **Abstract**: Accurate reconstruction of ocean is essential for reflecting global climate dynamics and supporting marine meteorological research. Conventional methods face challenges due to sparse data, algorithmic complexity, and high computational costs, while increasing usage of machine learning (ML) method remains limited to reconstruction problems at the sea surface and local regions, struggling with issues like cloud occlusion. To address these limitations, this paper proposes ReconMOST, a data-driven guided diffusion model framework for multi-layer sea temperature reconstruction. Specifically, we first pre-train an unconditional diffusion model using a large collection of historical numerical simulation data, enabling the model to attain physically consistent distribution patterns of ocean temperature fields. During the generation phase, sparse yet high-accuracy in-situ observational data are utilized as guidance points for the reverse diffusion process, generating accurate reconstruction results. Importantly, in regions lacking direct observational data, the physically consistent spatial distribution patterns learned during pre-training enable implicitly guided and physically plausible reconstructions. Our method extends ML-based SST reconstruction to a global, multi-layer setting, handling over 92.5% missing data while maintaining reconstruction accuracy, spatial resolution, and superior generalization capability. We pre-train our model on CMIP6 numerical simulation data and conduct guided reconstruction experiments on CMIP6 and EN4 analysis data. The results of mean squared error (MSE) values achieve 0.049 on guidance, 0.680 on reconstruction, and 0.633 on total, respectively, demonstrating the effectiveness and robustness of the proposed framework. Our source code is available at https://github.com/norsheep/ReconMOST.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces ReconMOST, a novel framework for reconstructing global, multi-layer sea temperature fields. It leverages a guided diffusion model trained on historical numerical simulation data to learn physically consistent patterns.  The model then utilizes sparse real-world observational data (like buoy data) to guide the reverse diffusion process, generating accurate reconstructions.  The key advantages are handling extreme data sparsity (over 92% missing data), maintaining reconstruction accuracy and spatial resolution, and demonstrating good generalization capabilities.  The authors pre-train on CMIP6 data and test on both CMIP6 and EN4 analysis data. Results show promising MSE values, highlighting the effectiveness and robustness of the approach.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper makes a significant contribution by extending ML-based sea temperature reconstruction to a global, multi-layer setting under conditions of extreme data sparsity. Previous methods focused on surface-level or localized reconstruction with denser data. The use of guided diffusion is a novel approach in this domain, effectively combining prior knowledge (from simulations) with limited observational data. The soft-extension strategy to propagate observational constraints addresses a critical limitation of sparse data, enhancing reconstruction quality in unobserved regions.
* **Significance:** Accurate sea temperature reconstruction is crucial for climate modeling, understanding global warming, and predicting extreme weather events. The ReconMOST framework tackles a significant challenge in this area by enabling high-quality reconstructions even with limited observational data. This opens up possibilities for improved climate monitoring and prediction, particularly in regions with sparse data coverage.
* **Strengths:**
    * **Effective handling of data sparsity:** The model's ability to reconstruct sea temperature fields with over 92% missing data is a major achievement.
    * **Physically consistent reconstructions:** Training on numerical simulations ensures that the reconstructed fields adhere to realistic spatial distribution patterns and physical principles.
    * **Good generalization performance:** Demonstrating effectiveness on both CMIP6 and EN4 datasets shows the model's ability to generalize to real-world observations and different data modalities.
    * **Comprehensive ablation studies:** Detailed ablation experiments provide robust evidence of the importance of various components of the framework, such as pre-training mode, dataset size, and guidance parameters.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Computational cost:** Training diffusion models can be computationally expensive, potentially limiting the scalability of the framework to even larger datasets or more complex models.  The paper mentions 24 hours on an A100 GPU, which is reasonable, but a detailed analysis of inference time and scalability would strengthen the paper.
    * **Lack of temporal dynamics:**  The current framework focuses on static spatial reconstructions and does not explicitly incorporate temporal dynamics. While addressing this is mentioned as future work, it's a notable limitation given the time-varying nature of ocean temperatures.
    * **Dependence on simulation data:** The reliance on numerical simulations for pre-training introduces a potential bias. The accuracy of the reconstruction depends on the quality and representativeness of the simulation data. The paper acknowledges the distribution shift but does not fully address it.  A discussion of the impact of biases present in the CMIP6 dataset on the reconstruction would be beneficial.
    * **Justification for specific parameter choices:** Some parameter choices, such as the Gaussian kernel size and the guidance strength 's' seem somewhat arbitrary. A more principled approach to selecting these parameters, perhaps through a sensitivity analysis or optimization procedure, would strengthen the results.
    * **Limited comparison with existing methods:**  While the introduction mentions limitations of traditional methods, a more direct comparison with state-of-the-art statistical reconstruction techniques (e.g., Optimal Interpolation, Kriging) on a common dataset would provide a stronger benchmark.

* **Potential Impact:**  The paper has the potential to significantly impact the field of oceanography and climate modeling by providing a more accurate and robust method for reconstructing sea temperature fields. This could lead to improved climate predictions, better understanding of ocean dynamics, and more effective monitoring of ocean health.  The methodology can potentially be extended to other climate variables and spatial-temporal reconstruction problems.

**Justification for the Score:**

The paper presents a novel and effective method for a challenging problem with significant practical implications. It demonstrates strong results in handling data sparsity and achieving physically plausible reconstructions. The thorough experimental evaluation and ablation studies add credibility to the findings.  However, there are some weaknesses in terms of computational cost, dependency on simulation data, and limited comparison to existing methods.  While the impact is promising, further research is needed to address the limitations and fully realize the potential of the ReconMOST framework.

### Pisces: An Auto-regressive Foundation Model for Image Understanding and Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10395v1)
- **Authors**: Zhiyang Xu, Jiuhai Chen, Zhaojiang Lin, Xichen Pan, Lifu Huang, Tianyi Zhou, Madian Khabsa, Qifan Wang, Di Jin, Michihiro Yasunaga, Lili Yu, Xi Victoria Lin, Shaoliang Nie
- **Abstract**: Recent advances in large language models (LLMs) have enabled multimodal foundation models to tackle both image understanding and generation within a unified framework. Despite these gains, unified models often underperform compared to specialized models in either task. A key challenge in developing unified models lies in the inherent differences between the visual features needed for image understanding versus generation, as well as the distinct training processes required for each modality. In this work, we introduce Pisces, an auto-regressive multimodal foundation model that addresses this challenge through a novel decoupled visual encoding architecture and tailored training techniques optimized for multimodal generation. Combined with meticulous data curation, pretraining, and finetuning, Pisces achieves competitive performance in both image understanding and image generation. We evaluate Pisces on over 20 public benchmarks for image understanding, where it demonstrates strong performance across a wide range of tasks. Additionally, on GenEval, a widely adopted benchmark for image generation, Pisces exhibits robust generative capabilities. Our extensive analysis reveals the synergistic relationship between image understanding and generation, and the benefits of using separate visual encoders, advancing the field of unified multimodal models.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and a critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces PISCES, a new auto-regressive multimodal foundation model designed for both image understanding and generation.  A core contribution is a decoupled visual encoding architecture, using separate encoders and training approaches for image understanding (requiring high resolution and detail) and image generation (benefiting from a compact visual representation). This design allows PISCES to perform competitively on a suite of over 20 image understanding benchmarks and the GenEval image generation benchmark. The authors also highlight a synergistic relationship where training for both tasks enhances performance in each domain.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**  The novelty of PISCES lies primarily in the architecture and training strategy.

*   **Decoupled Visual Encoding:** Using separate visual encoders tailored for image understanding and generation is the paper's most significant contribution. While other unified models exist, the distinct encoder design allows for better optimization across the tasks compared to using a single encoder setup. The ablation studies show the benefit of separate encoders. This approach directly addresses the challenges caused by the inherently different visual feature requirements of these two modalities, which is important.
*   **Training Regime:** The three-stage training (pretraining on high-quality image-caption pairs, fine-grained alignment with detailed captions, and instruction tuning) is relatively standard, but the authors claim to curate a dataset for it, though this is not the major novelty point. The exploration of the number of visual tokens is a good ablation but it doesn't represent a huge novelty.
*   **Architecture:** The model combines existing components (pretrained LLMs, CLIP encoders, diffusion models) in a specific configuration. The way that these models are combined is not really novel since it is borrowed from other state-of-the-art multi-modal architectures.

**Significance:** The significance of the paper rests on the empirical results and the insights gained.

*   **Performance:** PISCES achieves competitive or state-of-the-art results across several image understanding benchmarks and demonstrated strong image generation capability. This demonstrates the effectiveness of the decoupled encoder and tailored training. The image understanding benchmarks, while important, are not a completely revolutionary result and some of the gains appear to be marginal with respect to other large multi-modal models.
*   **Synergistic Training:** The finding that joint training of image understanding and generation improves performance in both tasks is valuable. This supports the idea that learning to generate images can help a model understand images better, and vice versa.  This observation contributes to a better understanding of the interplay between visual and textual data in multimodal learning.
*   **Limitations:** The authors leverage existing large language models and vision encoders. While this allows them to achieve strong performance, it also means that the core architectural components aren't entirely novel. Also, the model is reliant on commercially unavailable models. The dataset curation process, though mentioned, could be described in more detail for reproducibility.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a reasonably novel and effective approach to unified multimodal modeling. The decoupled visual encoding architecture is a clever design choice that addresses a known problem in the field. The empirical results, while not completely shattering benchmarks, demonstrate the effectiveness of the approach and are likely to be compelling.  The synergistic training observation is an important contribution.

However, the reliance on existing pretrained models limits the overall novelty. It does not appear that the gains shown over other existing solutions are extremely high. Therefore the model has good results, solid engineering, but only modest contributions to the field.

### Time To Impeach LLM-as-a-Judge: Programs are the Future of Evaluation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10403v1)
- **Authors**: Tzu-Heng Huang, Harit Vishwakarma, Frederic Sala
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) are widely used to evaluate the quality of LLM generations and responses, but this leads to significant challenges: high API costs, uncertain reliability, inflexible pipelines, and inherent biases. To address these, we introduce PAJAMA (Program-As-a-Judge for Automated Model Assessment), a new alternative that uses LLMs to synthesize executable judging programs instead of directly scoring responses. These synthesized programs can be stored and run locally, costing orders of magnitude less while providing interpretable, and auditable judging logic that can be easily adapted. Program-based judges mitigate biases, improving judgment consistency by 15.83% and reducing biased responses by 23.7% on average compared to a Qwen2.5-14B-based LLM-as-a-judge. When program judgments are distilled into a model, PAJAMA outperforms LLM-as-a-judge on the challenging CHAT-HARD subset of RewardBench, outperforming metrics by 2.19% on Prometheus and 8.67% on the JudgeLM dataset, all at three orders of magnitude lower cost.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Time To Impeach LLM-as-a-Judge: Programs are the Future of Evaluation":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces PAJAMA (Program-As-a-Judge for Automated Model Assessment), a novel approach to evaluating the quality of LLM-generated responses. Instead of directly prompting an LLM to score responses (LLM-as-a-judge), PAJAMA uses LLMs to synthesize executable judging programs (e.g., Python code) that encode evaluation criteria. These programs are then executed locally to assess response quality, offering lower API costs, interpretable logic, and reduced biases compared to traditional LLM-as-a-judge methods. The authors demonstrate PAJAMA's effectiveness in reducing costs, improving consistency, and mitigating biases across several experiments. They also show that reward models distilled from PAJAMA-generated judgments outperform LLM-as-a-judge distilled models on challenging benchmarks while significantly reducing API costs.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of synthesizing executable judging programs is relatively novel. While program synthesis has been explored in other contexts (e.g., generating code from natural language), applying it to the specific task of evaluating LLM outputs and using those programs as standalone judges is a distinct contribution. The combination of program synthesis with weak supervision is also innovative.

*   **Significance:** The potential impact is significant. LLM evaluation is a critical bottleneck in developing and deploying these models. The issues of cost, bias, and lack of transparency in LLM-as-a-judge approaches are well-recognized. PAJAMA addresses these directly. A method that significantly reduces cost (orders of magnitude, as claimed) while maintaining or improving quality and reducing bias would be highly valuable to the research community.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Cost Reduction:**  The experimental results clearly show a substantial reduction in API costs, which is a significant practical advantage.
    *   **Bias Mitigation:** The experiments demonstrate improved consistency and reduced bias in judgments compared to a standard LLM-as-a-judge, suggesting that PAJAMA is better at generating unbiased programmatic judges.
    *   **Interpretability:** The authors effectively highlight the enhanced interpretability of the synthesized judging programs, which promotes auditing and fine-tuning of evaluation rubrics.
    *   **Experimental Validation:** The authors conduct experiments across different datasets and biases to evaluate PAJAMA's performance, providing solid empirical evidence for their claims.
    *   **Adaptability:** The framework's modularity allows for easy swapping of criteria and judging principles, demonstrating the tool's inherent adaptability.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Program Quality Dependency:** The effectiveness of PAJAMA heavily relies on the LLM's ability to synthesize high-quality judging programs. The prompt engineering aspect could be more thoroughly discussed (what prompts are used and how they impact the variety and performance). It's unclear how the generated programs handle edge cases or complex scenarios.
    *   **Scalability Challenges**: Although the paper emphasizes the low-cost evaluation process, the approach might encounter scalability issues when evaluating extremely complex generation outputs. It could require intricate and computationally intensive programs.
    *   **Generalization**: The generalization capability of the "synthesized" judging program should be carefully considered. LLM might encode bias into the logic and replicate the bias to the programmatic judges.

*   **Potential Influence:** If the approach proves robust and scalable, it could become a standard alternative to LLM-as-a-judge. It promotes more transparent, auditable, and customizable evaluation pipelines. The idea of generating specialized evaluation code could influence research in other areas of AI. The combination with weak supervision could become a common paradigm for integrating various noisy judgment sources.

* **Justification:**

The paper presents a novel and practically relevant approach to LLM evaluation. It demonstrably addresses key challenges associated with existing LLM-as-a-judge methods: cost, bias, and interpretability. The experimental results are convincing, showing significant cost reductions and bias mitigation. While some weaknesses persist regarding program quality dependency, the modular and adaptable nature of PAJAMA allows for flexibility and refinement of judgment rubrics. The paper’s potential to shift the LLM evaluation landscape toward more transparent and scalable methods warrants a high score.

### PAG: Multi-Turn Reinforced LLM Self-Correction with Policy as Generative Verifier
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10406v1)
- **Authors**: Yuhua Jiang, Yuwen Xiong, Yufeng Yuan, Chao Xin, Wenyuan Xu, Yu Yue, Qianchuan Zhao, Lin Yan
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) have demonstrated impressive capabilities in complex reasoning tasks, yet they still struggle to reliably verify the correctness of their own outputs. Existing solutions to this verification challenge often depend on separate verifier models or require multi-stage self-correction training pipelines, which limit scalability. In this paper, we propose Policy as Generative Verifier (PAG), a simple and effective framework that empowers LLMs to self-correct by alternating between policy and verifier roles within a unified multi-turn reinforcement learning (RL) paradigm. Distinct from prior approaches that always generate a second attempt regardless of model confidence, PAG introduces a selective revision mechanism: the model revises its answer only when its own generative verification step detects an error. This verify-then-revise workflow not only alleviates model collapse but also jointly enhances both reasoning and verification abilities. Extensive experiments across diverse reasoning benchmarks highlight PAG's dual advancements: as a policy, it enhances direct generation and self-correction accuracy; as a verifier, its self-verification outperforms self-consistency.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Policy as Generative Verifier (PAG), a novel framework for multi-turn self-correction in large language models (LLMs). Unlike existing methods that either rely on separate verifier models or always generate a second attempt, PAG enables LLMs to alternate between policy (generating solutions) and generative verifier (evaluating solutions) roles within a unified reinforcement learning (RL) paradigm. The key innovation is a selective revision mechanism: the model only revises its answer if its own generative verification step detects an error. This verify-then-revise workflow aims to mitigate model collapse, enhance both reasoning and verification abilities, and streamline the training process. The authors present extensive experiments across diverse reasoning benchmarks, demonstrating PAG's effectiveness in enhancing direct generation, self-correction accuracy, and self-verification capabilities.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The core idea of alternating policy and verifier roles in a multi-turn fashion with a selective revision mechanism is reasonably novel.  The "verify-then-revise" approach directly addresses the identified limitations of always-revising methods like SCoRe and explicitly tries to avoid model collapse.  The idea of employing the *same* LLM for both tasks, guided by reinforcement learning, is also a solid contribution to reducing complexity and computational costs, although this idea has been explored in the literature recently, with similar approaches.

**Significance:** The significance lies in the potential to improve the reliability of LLMs in complex reasoning tasks. A model that can effectively verify its own answers is crucial for real-world applications where accuracy is paramount (mathematical reasoning, scientific research, etc.). The reported performance gains across multiple benchmarks are promising and suggest PAG could be a viable alternative to more complex or resource-intensive methods. Simplification of the training pipeline compared to SCoRe (removal of warm-up phase) is a practical advantage. However, the reliance on external ground-truth verifiers for training does limit its applicability in certain domains, particularly where labelled data or automated judgement metrics are less available.

**Strengths:**

*   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly identifies the challenge of verification in LLMs and the limitations of existing solutions.
*   **Simple and Effective Framework:** PAG is relatively straightforward to implement and doesn't require significant architectural changes.
*   **Strong Empirical Results:** Extensive experiments across diverse benchmarks demonstrate the effectiveness of PAG in improving both generation and verification.
*   **Ablation Studies:** Ablation studies provide insights into the importance of key components of PAG, such as the selective revision mechanism.
*   **Addresses Model Collapse:** By implementing a selective revision mechanism that conditions revisions on detected errors, the architecture mitigates model collapse.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Reliance on External Reward:** The framework relies on external ground-truth verifiers during training, which restricts its applicability to tasks where such supervision is unavailable. Addressing this limitation in future work is imperative.
*   **Limited Scalability Evaluation:** While the paper reports results with Qwen and Llama models, there needs to be a wider evaluation of PAGs scaling properties with different sized architectures, i.e 70B or larger.
*   **Incremental Improvement:** While the improvements are noticeable, the marginal gain in performance is relatively small compared to the base models which also limits the real world impact
*    **Hyperparameter Sensitivity**: It's unclear how sensitive PAG's performance is to different hyperparameter settings. A more detailed analysis of hyperparameter tuning would be beneficial.

**Potential Influence:** PAG could influence future research on self-correction and verification in LLMs. The idea of alternating between policy and verifier roles could be extended to other tasks beyond reasoning. Additionally, the selective revision mechanism could be incorporated into other self-improvement frameworks.

**Justification for Score:**

I assign a score of **7**.  The paper presents a novel and reasonably effective approach to self-correction with well-supported experimental results.  It addresses an important problem in LLMs and offers a practical solution that simplifies the training process. However, the reliance on external ground-truth verifiers significantly limits its applicability. Also, the improvements while statistically significant are incremental. The method's contribution is notable, and could drive some further exploration in the space, but it doesn't represent a paradigm shift.

### Reasoning RAG via System 1 or System 2: A Survey on Reasoning Agentic Retrieval-Augmented Generation for Industry Challenges
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10408v1)
- **Authors**: Jintao Liang, Gang Su, Huifeng Lin, You Wu, Rui Zhao, Ziyue Li
- **Abstract**: Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) has emerged as a powerful framework to overcome the knowledge limitations of Large Language Models (LLMs) by integrating external retrieval with language generation. While early RAG systems based on static pipelines have shown effectiveness in well-structured tasks, they struggle in real-world scenarios requiring complex reasoning, dynamic retrieval, and multi-modal integration. To address these challenges, the field has shifted toward Reasoning Agentic RAG, a paradigm that embeds decision-making and adaptive tool use directly into the retrieval process. In this paper, we present a comprehensive review of Reasoning Agentic RAG methods, categorizing them into two primary systems: predefined reasoning, which follows fixed modular pipelines to boost reasoning, and agentic reasoning, where the model autonomously orchestrates tool interaction during inference. We analyze representative techniques under both paradigms, covering architectural design, reasoning strategies, and tool coordination. Finally, we discuss key research challenges and propose future directions to advance the flexibility, robustness, and applicability of reasoning agentic RAG systems. Our collection of the relevant research has been organized into a https://github.com/ByebyeMonica/Reasoning-Agentic-RAG.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper presents a comprehensive survey of Reasoning Agentic Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) methods, a field that addresses the limitations of traditional RAG by integrating decision-making and adaptive tool use into the retrieval process. The survey categorizes these methods into two primary systems: predefined reasoning, which follows fixed modular pipelines, and agentic reasoning, where the model autonomously orchestrates tool interaction. The authors analyze representative techniques under both paradigms, covering architectural design, reasoning strategies, and tool coordination. The paper concludes by discussing key research challenges and proposing future directions to improve the flexibility, robustness, and applicability of reasoning agentic RAG systems.  It also attempts to connect the predefined and agentic systems to System 1 and System 2 thinking from cognitive science.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Comprehensive Scope:** The paper provides a well-structured overview of the emerging field of Reasoning Agentic RAG. It effectively categorizes the different approaches and offers a clear taxonomy.
    *   **Clear Categorization:** The distinction between predefined and agentic reasoning is insightful and provides a useful framework for understanding the landscape of these techniques.
    *   **Emphasis on Practical Challenges:** The paper highlights the limitations of existing methods when applied to real-world, industrial-scale problems. This focus on practical challenges makes the survey relevant and impactful.
    *   **Future Directions:** The discussion of future research directions is thoughtful and identifies key areas for improvement, such as enhancing tool interaction, developing finer-grained reward functions, and improving generalization and robustness.
    *   **Cognitive Science connection:** Drawing the comparison to System 1 and System 2, though not entirely novel, provides a good way to contextualize the efficiency vs. adaptability tradeoff.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Depth in Specific Techniques:** While the survey covers a broad range of methods, it sometimes lacks depth in the explanation of specific techniques. This might make it difficult for readers unfamiliar with the field to fully grasp the nuances of each approach.
    *   **Somewhat Speculative Tone in Future Directions:** The future directions section, while insightful, can at times be speculative, lacking concrete proposals for addressing the identified challenges.
    *   **Novelty:** The paper categorizes existing research, but does not introduce new concepts or models. While the survey is useful, it does not present groundbreaking research.
    *   **Limited Evaluation:** The paper summarizes the field but doesn't offer a comparative empirical evaluation of the surveyed methods. It is difficult to gauge which methods truly excel in practice based on the reported summaries alone.

*   **Novelty and Significance:**

    The survey's novelty lies primarily in its structured organization of a rapidly evolving field, Reasoning Agentic RAG. The integration of different architectures and categorizations helps to contextualize the overall direction of the area. However, much of the discussed material comes from existing resources and doesn't break new ground. Overall the survey can improve accessibility and comprehension of the field.

*   **Impact:**

    The paper has the potential to guide future research and development efforts in Reasoning Agentic RAG by highlighting key challenges and promising directions. It can also serve as a valuable resource for researchers and practitioners seeking to understand the current state-of-the-art in this area.

**Score: 6**

**Rationale:**

The paper offers a valuable and well-structured survey of Reasoning Agentic RAG. However, its limited depth in the explanation of specific techniques, combined with some speculative tone in future directions, and lack of empirical comparisons prevents it from achieving a higher score. While it organizes the existing research well and highlights relevant challenges, it doesn't introduce new methods, therefore the novelty, while present is not high. Nonetheless, it will likely become a frequently cited resource for anyone entering the field.

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 6/10

### Burn After Reading: Do Multimodal Large Language Models Truly Capture Order of Events in Image Sequences?
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10415v1)
- **Authors**: Yingjin Song, Yupei Du, Denis Paperno, Albert Gatt
- **Abstract**: This paper introduces the TempVS benchmark, which focuses on temporal grounding and reasoning capabilities of Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) in image sequences. TempVS consists of three main tests (i.e., event relation inference, sentence ordering and image ordering), each accompanied with a basic grounding test. TempVS requires MLLMs to rely on both visual and linguistic modalities to understand the temporal order of events. We evaluate 38 state-of-the-art MLLMs, demonstrating that models struggle to solve TempVS, with a substantial performance gap compared to human capabilities. We also provide fine-grained insights that suggest promising directions for future research. Our TempVS benchmark data and code are available at https://github.com/yjsong22/TempVS.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces TempVS, a new benchmark for evaluating the temporal grounding and reasoning capabilities of Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) when presented with image sequences. The benchmark includes three main tasks: event relation inference, sentence ordering, and image ordering, each with an associated grounding task. The authors evaluated 38 state-of-the-art MLLMs and found that they struggled with TempVS, showing a performance gap compared to human capabilities. The paper includes fine-grained analysis that suggests promising directions for future research to improve temporal reasoning in MLLMs.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a novel benchmark specifically designed to test temporal reasoning with image sequences. This is a significant contribution, as most existing benchmarks focus on single images or cross-image recognition rather than temporal understanding. The detailed analysis of MLLM performance on various aspects of temporal reasoning is also valuable and potentially new.

*   **Significance:** The benchmark addresses a critical gap in the evaluation of MLLMs. Temporal understanding is crucial for many real-world applications, and TempVS provides a more challenging and realistic evaluation than existing benchmarks. The paper’s findings highlight the limitations of current MLLMs in temporal reasoning and offer valuable insights for future research directions, such as improving architectural design, training objectives, or post-training methods.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Well-defined and comprehensive benchmark with clear tasks and evaluation metrics.
    *   Extensive evaluation of a wide range of MLLMs.
    *   Detailed analysis provides valuable insights into the strengths and weaknesses of current models and suggests promising directions for future research.
    *   The benchmark is publicly available.
    *   Addresses a previously relatively overlooked aspect of MLLMs.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   While the paper mentions avoiding shortcuts, it would be beneficial to have a more detailed discussion on specific strategies used to mitigate biases and prevent models from exploiting dataset artifacts.

**Score & Justification:**

Score: 8

**Rationale:**
This paper earns a high score because it introduces a timely and relevant benchmark addressing a crucial aspect of MLLM evaluation that has been relatively unexplored. While there might be room for further refinement in bias mitigation techniques, the paper is comprehensive, well-analyzed, and provides valuable insights into the current limitations and future directions for improving temporal reasoning in MLLMs. The detailed analysis and public availability of the benchmark further increase its impact. While incremental, it’s a substantial step forward and a solid contribution to the field. The significance of the problem and the quality of the work justify the score.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### Can Sound Replace Vision in LLaVA With Token Substitution?
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10416v1)
- **Authors**: Ali Vosoughi, Jing Bi, Pinxin Liu, Yunlong Tang, Chenliang Xu
- **Abstract**: While multimodal systems have achieved impressive advances, they typically rely on text-aligned representations rather than directly integrating audio and visual inputs. This reliance can limit the use of acoustic information in tasks requiring nuanced audio understanding. In response, SoundCLIP explores direct audio-visual integration within multimodal large language models (MLLMs) by substituting CLIP's visual tokens with audio representations and selecting sound-relevant patch tokens in models such as LLaVA. We investigate two configurations: (1) projecting audio features into CLIP's visual manifold via a multilayer perceptron trained with InfoNCE on paired audio-video segments, and (2) preserving raw audio embeddings with minimal dimensional adjustments. Experiments with five state-of-the-art audio encoders reveal a fundamental trade-off. While audio-to-video retrieval performance increases dramatically (up to 44 percentage points in Top-1 accuracy) when audio is projected into CLIP's space, text generation quality declines. Encoders pre-trained with text supervision (CLAP, Whisper, ImageBind) maintain stronger generative capabilities than those focused primarily on audiovisual alignment (Wav2CLIP, AudioCLIP), highlighting the value of language exposure for generation tasks. We introduce WhisperCLIP, an architecture that fuses intermediate representations from Whisper, as well as AudioVisual Event Evaluation (AVE-2), a dataset of 580,147 three-second audiovisual clips with fine-grained alignment annotations. Our findings challenge the assumption that stronger cross-modal alignment necessarily benefits all multimodal tasks; instead, a Pareto frontier emerges wherein optimal performance depends on balancing retrieval accuracy with text generation quality. Codes and datasets: https://github.com/ali-vosoughi/SoundCLIP.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Can Sound Replace Vision in LLaVA With Token Substitution?" explores a method called SoundCLIP for directly integrating audio information into multimodal Large Language and Vision Assistants (MLLMs) like LLaVA, bypassing the traditional text-based intermediary. SoundCLIP substitutes CLIP's visual tokens with audio representations, investigating two configurations: (1) projecting audio features into CLIP's visual manifold using a trained multi-layer perceptron (MLP), and (2) preserving raw audio embeddings with minimal dimensional adjustments. The core finding is a trade-off: projecting audio into CLIP's space significantly improves audio-to-video retrieval performance but reduces text generation quality. The paper introduces WhisperCLIP, an architecture that fuses intermediate representations from Whisper to balance retrieval and generation. They also release AVE-2, a new dataset for audio-visual alignment evaluation with fine-grained annotations across five quality dimensions. The study concludes that optimal performance depends on balancing retrieval accuracy and text generation quality, challenging the assumption that tighter cross-modal alignment always benefits downstream tasks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a novel approach to integrating audio directly into vision-language models by substituting visual tokens, which is a departure from the more common text-based intermediary. The identification and quantification of the retrieval-generation trade-off is a significant contribution and hasn't been extensively addressed in existing research. The release of the AVE-2 dataset and the proposed WhisperCLIP architecture further adds to the novelty. However, token substitution, while novel in this *specific* context of directly replacing CLIP visual tokens, has been used in other multimodal scenarios.
*   **Significance:** The findings have important implications for multimodal AI research.  The identification of the retrieval-generation trade-off pushes the field towards more task-specific optimization in cross-modal representation learning. The release of a high-quality, richly annotated audio-visual dataset (AVE-2) addresses a recognized gap in resources and facilitates further research. The demonstrated practical benefits of direct audio-visual integration within existing MLLMs makes the approach valuable.  While WhisperCLIP represents a novel architecture, its incremental improvements over standard Whisper might be viewed as a smaller step. The reliance on frozen pre-trained encoders has both advantages (efficiency) and disadvantages (potential limitations on representation learning).
*   **Strengths:**
    *   Well-defined problem and clear research question.
    *   Rigorous experimental setup with comprehensive evaluation across multiple datasets and audio encoders.
    *   Identification and quantification of a practically important trade-off.
    *   Introduction of a new dataset and architecture contributing to the field.
    *   Clear presentation of results and insightful discussion.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The benefits of WhisperCLIP, while present, are more incremental than groundbreaking.
    *   The approach relies heavily on pre-trained models, limiting the scope for end-to-end optimization of audio-visual representations.
    *   While the paper identifies the retrieval-generation trade-off, more in-depth investigation into *why* specific encoders manage this trade-off better is warranted (beyond superficial discussion of training paradigms). The theoretical formalization is useful, but higher level more interpretable explanations would improve its impact.
    *   The ablation studies, while included in supplementary, would benefit from being integrated more closely into the main paper, as they provide more insights for future researchers.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper makes a valuable contribution to the field of multimodal AI by proposing a novel approach to audio integration, quantifying an important trade-off, and releasing useful resources.  However, the incremental nature of some of the architectural improvements, and the reliance on pre-trained models, prevent it from achieving a higher score. Furthermore, while the study *identifies* the trade-off between retrieval and generation in cross-modal learning, it does not provide a complete *solution*. Future work would need to explore more sophisticated strategies to fully mitigate the drawbacks of the tighter alignment and provide a mechanism to navigate the identified Pareto frontier. The emphasis on directly practical integration reduces some of the broader theory advancements, while the theoretical model is useful, a more complete characterization might benefit the broader multimodal learning landscape.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.MM
- **Score**: 7/10

### Beyond the Battlefield: Framing Analysis of Media Coverage in Conflict Reporting
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10421v1)
- **Authors**: Avneet Kaur, Arnav Arora
- **Abstract**: Framing used by news media, especially in times of conflict, can have substantial impact on readers' opinion, potentially aggravating the conflict itself. Current studies on the topic of conflict framing have limited insights due to their qualitative nature or only look at surface level generic frames without going deeper. In this work, we identify indicators of war and peace journalism, as outlined by prior work in conflict studies, in a corpus of news articles reporting on the Israel-Palestine war. For our analysis, we use computational approaches, using a combination of frame semantics and large language models to identify both communicative framing and its connection to linguistic framing. Our analysis reveals a higher focus on war based reporting rather than peace based. We also show substantial differences in reporting across the US, UK, and Middle Eastern news outlets in framing who the assailant and victims of the conflict are, surfacing biases within the media.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Beyond the Battlefield: Framing Analysis of Media Coverage in Conflict Reporting":

**Summary:**

The paper presents a computational framing analysis of media coverage of the Israel-Palestine conflict. It aims to identify biases in news reporting across different geographical regions (US, UK, and Middle East). The authors operationalize Galtung's framework for war versus peace journalism using a combination of frame semantics and large language models (LLMs). The analysis extracts generic frames, issue-specific frames related to war and peace, and semantic frames. The findings reveal a higher focus on war-based reporting compared to peace-based reporting and substantial differences in framing across news outlets in terms of who is portrayed as the assailant and victim, indicating regional biases.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its computational approach to analyzing conflict framing using Galtung's framework. While framing analysis and Galtung's framework are not new in themselves, their combination with LLMs for automated large-scale analysis of conflict reporting is a valuable contribution. It moves beyond qualitative analysis, addressing its limitations in scalability. Furthermore, the comparison of framing across different geographical regions adds to the understanding of potential biases. However, similar works that use LLMs and other approaches to analyze news and detect framing do exist in the literature, which slightly diminishes the novelty.

**Significance:** The paper addresses a significant issue: the potential impact of biased media framing on public opinion and the escalation of conflict. Identifying indicators of war journalism and comparing their prevalence across regions is important for understanding how different media outlets portray the conflict. The results highlight the potential for media to reinforce negative stereotypes and contribute to polarization.  The use of Galtung's Peace Journalism framework to analyze this is relevant and well-established. However, the actual impact of this framing analysis on directly influencing journalism practices remains to be seen, limiting the tangible impact for now.

**Strengths:**

*   **Clear Research Questions:** The paper outlines clear research questions guiding the analysis.
*   **Operationalization of Theory:** The authors effectively operationalize Galtung's framework into a computable approach.
*   **Methodological Rigor:** The paper uses a combination of frame semantics and LLMs, which represents a reasonable methodology given the task. The paper provides details about the LLM prompts used.
*   **Analysis of Diverse Regions:** Comparing media coverage across US, UK, and Middle East allows for identifying regional biases.
*   **Comprehensive Framing Analysis:** The analysis covers generic frames, issue-specific frames, and semantic frames, providing a multi-faceted understanding of framing.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Data Limitations:** The dataset size (22k articles after filtering) is not exceptionally large for LLM-based analysis. Larger and more diverse data could strengthen the findings. Limiting the analysis to English-language sources in a region of multilingual complexity means there's a bias in the source dataset.
*   **LLM Bias:** The paper acknowledges LLM bias, which is a limitation inherent in using LLMs. The paper discusses the LLM usage but could have included steps to further mitigate or evaluate the impact of this bias on the results.
*   **Manual Verification:** The filtering process depends on qualitative judgement of "reputable" news domains. Using a more objective metric for reputation would strengthen the reliability.
*   **Limited Evaluation of LLM Performance:** Although the LLM is evaluated, the detailed metrics per frame label are included in the appendix. A clearer discussion of the model's capabilities and potential errors in frame identification within the main body could add more context to interpret the findings.

**Potential Influence:**

The paper has the potential to influence future research in computational framing analysis, particularly in conflict reporting. It provides a valuable methodology for identifying and comparing media biases. The findings can be used to inform media literacy initiatives and encourage more balanced and nuanced reporting of conflicts. However, further research is needed to validate the findings and investigate the impact of these framing differences on public opinion.

**Score: 6.5**

**Rationale:**  The paper presents a moderately novel approach to analyzing conflict framing by combining Galtung's framework with LLMs. The findings reveal regional biases in media coverage, which is a significant issue. However, data and methodological limitations and the relatively small impact on actual journalism practices limit the overall score. Further, the paper needs a strong argument that can be done from Galtung's theories and the related works. The score reflects the value of the approach and findings while acknowledging its limitations and the need for further validation and refinement.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### PAL: Probing Audio Encoders via LLMs -- A Study of Information Transfer from Audio Encoders to LLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10423v1)
- **Authors**: Tony Alex, Wish Suharitdamrong, Sara Atito, Armin Mustafa, Philip J. B. Jackson, Imran Razzak, Muhammad Awais
- **Abstract**: The integration of audio perception capabilities into Large Language Models (LLMs) has enabled significant advances in Audio-LLMs. Although application-focused developments, particularly in curating training data for specific capabilities e.g., audio reasoning, have progressed rapidly, the underlying mechanisms that govern efficient transfer of rich semantic representations from audio encoders to LLMs remain under-explored. We conceptualize effective audio-LLM interaction as the LLM's ability to proficiently probe the audio encoder representations to satisfy textual queries. This paper presents a systematic investigation on how architectural design choices can affect that. Beginning with a standard Pengi/LLaVA-style audio-LLM architecture, we propose and evaluate several modifications guided by hypotheses derived from mechanistic interpretability studies and LLM operational principles. Our experiments demonstrate that: (1) delaying audio integration until the LLM's initial layers establish textual context that enhances its ability to probe the audio representations for relevant information; (2) the LLM can proficiently probe audio representations exclusively through LLM layer's attention submodule, without requiring propagation to its Feed-Forward Network (FFN) submodule; (3) an efficiently integrated ensemble of diverse audio encoders provides richer, complementary representations, thereby broadening the LLM's capacity to probe a wider spectrum of audio information. All hypotheses are evaluated using an identical three-stage training curriculum on a dataset of 5.6 million audio-text pairs, ensuring controlled comparisons. Our final architecture, which incorporates all proposed modifications, achieves relative improvements from 10\% to 60\% over the baseline, validating our approach to optimizing cross-modal information transfer in audio-LLMs. Project page: https://ta012.github.io/PAL/
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "PAL: Probing Audio Encoders via LLMs - A Study of Information Transfer from Audio Encoders to LLMs" investigates how to improve the integration of audio perception capabilities into Large Language Models (LLMs). The authors focus on the mechanisms that govern the efficient transfer of semantic representations from audio encoders to LLMs, conceptualizing this interaction as the LLM's ability to "probe" audio encoder representations to satisfy textual queries. They propose and evaluate several architectural modifications to a standard Pengi/LLaVA-style audio-LLM, guided by mechanistic interpretability studies and LLM operational principles. Key findings include the benefits of delayed audio integration, the sufficiency of attention mechanisms for processing audio, and the advantage of using diverse audio encoder ensembles. The paper presents a systematic comparative study using a standardized training curriculum and dataset. The final architecture, incorporating all proposed modifications, achieves relative improvements over the baseline, validating the approach to optimizing cross-modal information transfer in audio-LLMs.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper has reasonable novelty in its systematic exploration of architectural choices for audio-LLM integration.  While individual components (like using attention, ensembling, or delayed fusion) might have been explored in other contexts, their specific application and rigorous evaluation within the audio-LLM framework, guided by mechanistic interpretability insights, is a contribution. The focus on *probing* audio encoders is a reasonable conceptual framing.
* **Significance:** The potential significance lies in providing actionable guidelines for designing more effective audio-LLMs. The findings related to delayed fusion, attention sufficiency, and encoder ensembles offer practical insights that could lead to improved performance and efficiency.  The standardized training curriculum and comparative methodology strengthen the validity of the results.  However, the limited scale of the LLM used (1B parameters) may limit the generalizability of the findings to larger, more capable models. The focus on audio-LLM information transfer mechanisms is relatively focused, possibly overlooking broader perspectives on improving audio-LLM performance.
* **Strengths:**
    * **Systematic investigation:**  The paper presents a well-structured and systematic study, testing each hypothesis with carefully controlled experiments.
    * **Mechanistic interpretability guidance:** The use of insights from mechanistic interpretability provides a strong rationale for the proposed architectural modifications.
    * **Standardized curriculum:** The use of a standardized training curriculum ensures a fair comparison between different architectures.
    * **Clear findings:** The key findings are clearly articulated and supported by empirical evidence.
* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Limited model scale:** The use of a relatively small LLM (1B parameters) raises concerns about the scalability and generalizability of the findings to larger, more powerful models. This limits the broader impact of the work.
    * **Incremental improvements:**  While the paper shows relative improvements over the baseline, the absolute performance gains (Table 1 and Table 2) might not be substantial enough to justify significant changes. A comparison that includes confidence intervals to better describe significance could improve the study.
    * **Limited qualitative analysis:** Though a qualitative analysis is provided, it is rather limited, and a more robust evaluation that provides a nuanced comparison of samples that explicitly highlights the benefits of different architectural modifications would further support the quantitative evidence.
    * **Dataset limitations**: While using a known dataset provides a basis for comparison, this also limits the novelty of the study in terms of data and dataset design. The evaluation also leans heavily toward the dataset with in-domain data; zero-shot and weak zero-shot could indicate better generalizability of the results.
    * **Lack of comparison with state-of-the-art audio encoders:** The paper benchmarks with SSLAM; a more comprehensive analysis with more recent audio encoders would strengthen the results.
* **Potential Influence:** The paper could influence the design of future audio-LLMs, particularly by encouraging researchers to consider delayed fusion, attention-only processing, and encoder ensembles. The methodology of using mechanistic interpretability to guide architectural choices could also be adopted in other areas of multimodal learning. However, the limitations discussed above may temper its immediate impact.

**Justification for Score:**

I assign a score of 6/10. While the paper presents a systematic and reasonably novel investigation of audio-LLM integration techniques, the limited model scale and somewhat incremental performance gains limit its overall significance and potential influence on the field. The methodology and the findings regarding attention sufficiency and encoder ensembles are valuable, but the lack of scalability to larger models and reliance on in-domain datasets detract from the overall impact. The paper provides a solid foundation for future research, but it falls short of being a truly groundbreaking contribution. The authors could consider extending this analysis to modern, capable LLMs as future work, and benchmarking with SOTA audio encoders for comparison.

Score: 6

- **Classification**: cs.SD
- **Score**: 6/10

### SOFT: Selective Data Obfuscation for Protecting LLM Fine-tuning against Membership Inference Attacks
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10424v1)
- **Authors**: Kaiyuan Zhang, Siyuan Cheng, Hanxi Guo, Yuetian Chen, Zian Su, Shengwei An, Yuntao Du, Charles Fleming, Ashish Kundu, Xiangyu Zhang, Ninghui Li
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) have achieved remarkable success and are widely adopted for diverse applications. However, fine-tuning these models often involves private or sensitive information, raising critical privacy concerns. In this work, we conduct the first comprehensive study evaluating the vulnerability of fine-tuned LLMs to membership inference attacks (MIAs). Our empirical analysis demonstrates that MIAs exploit the loss reduction during fine-tuning, making them highly effective in revealing membership information. These findings motivate the development of our defense. We propose SOFT (\textbf{S}elective data \textbf{O}bfuscation in LLM \textbf{F}ine-\textbf{T}uning), a novel defense technique that mitigates privacy leakage by leveraging influential data selection with an adjustable parameter to balance utility preservation and privacy protection. Our extensive experiments span six diverse domains and multiple LLM architectures and scales. Results show that SOFT effectively reduces privacy risks while maintaining competitive model performance, offering a practical and scalable solution to safeguard sensitive information in fine-tuned LLMs.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "SOFT: Selective Data Obfuscation for Protecting LLM Fine-tuning against Membership Inference Attacks" introduces a novel defense mechanism, SOFT, to mitigate privacy leakage in fine-tuned large language models (LLMs) against membership inference attacks (MIAs). The authors perform a comprehensive empirical analysis demonstrating the vulnerability of fine-tuned LLMs to MIAs, where attacks exploit the loss reduction during fine-tuning. SOFT addresses this by selectively obfuscating influential data samples (those most vulnerable to MIA) in the fine-tuning dataset using paraphrasing.  The approach involves warm-up fine-tuning, influential data selection based on validation loss, data obfuscation through paraphrasing, and final fine-tuning. Extensive experiments across diverse domains, model architectures, and scales demonstrate that SOFT effectively reduces privacy risks while preserving model performance.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary strength lies in identifying and addressing a critical gap in the privacy literature surrounding fine-tuned LLMs. While previous work focused on MIAs against pre-trained models or explored privacy-preserving fine-tuning techniques like DP-LoRA, this paper presents a comprehensive study of MIA vulnerabilities *during* the fine-tuning process itself. The SOFT method itself is a novel contribution, taking inspiration from influence functions and applying a targeted data obfuscation technique based on sample vulnerability to MIAs.
*   **Significance:** The increasing use of LLMs in downstream applications, particularly in sensitive domains like healthcare and law, makes the privacy concerns addressed by this paper highly significant. The demonstrated effectiveness of MIAs against fine-tuned LLMs underscores the need for practical and scalable defenses like SOFT. By balancing privacy protection and model utility, SOFT offers a potentially valuable solution for practitioners.
*   **Technical Soundness:** The paper presents a well-defined methodology. The experiments are extensive, covering a variety of datasets, LLM architectures, and MIA techniques. The comparison with existing defenses, especially DP-LoRA, is well-executed. The ablation studies provide valuable insights into the importance of the various components of SOFT. However, more detail on the nuances and computational overheads of different approaches is desired.
*   **Scope and Limitations:** The study focuses primarily on loss-based MIAs, which is a reasonable starting point. However, it would be helpful to discuss the potential effectiveness of SOFT against other more advanced MIA techniques. Furthermore, while the paper conducts adaptive attacks, it could delve deeper into the limitations of SOFT in the face of more sophisticated attackers who possess additional knowledge about the defense mechanism. The generalizability of SOFT to different paraphrasing models needs to be investigated beyond the models used in the study.
*   **Clarity and Presentation:** The paper is well-written and clearly structured. The figures and tables are informative, and the experimental setup is well-described. The introduction provides a good overview of the problem and the proposed solution.

**Justification for Score:**

I assign a score of **7**.

*   **Strong positives:** The paper addresses a significant and timely problem with a novel and well-engineered solution. The empirical evaluation is comprehensive and supports the claims made by the authors.
*   **Areas for improvement:** While the paper is solid, the novelty lies primarily in the application of influence-function-inspired targeted obfuscation in the LLM domain and lacks depth and granularity as more recent papers have. Some limitations remain, particularly regarding the robustness of SOFT against more advanced adaptive attacks and the generalizability across a broader set of paraphrasing models. Furthermore, more detailed analysis of the computational costs and benefits for different parameterizations of data/model obfuscation methods and models would be more beneficial.
*   **Impact:** The paper provides a good reference point for the performance trade-off between model size and privacy. The presented findings will serve to motivate future research in designing more robust and scalable defenses against MIAs in fine-tuned LLMs.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CR
- **Score**: 7/10

### Towards Understanding Bugs in Distributed Training and Inference Frameworks for Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10426v1)
- **Authors**: Xiao Yu, Haoxuan Chen, Feifei Niu, Xing Hu, Jacky Wai Keung, Xin Xia
- **Abstract**: With the rapid development of large language models (LLMs), distributed training and inference frameworks like DeepSpeed have become essential for scaling model training and inference across multiple GPUs or nodes. However, the increasing complexity of these frameworks brings non-trivial software bugs, which may degrade training performance, cause unexpected failures, and result in significant resource waste. Understanding framework bugs' characteristics is fundamental for quality assurance, allowing the design of more effective debugging and repair methods. Thus, our paper conducts the first large-scale empirical analysis of 308 fixed bugs across three popular distributed training/inference frameworks: DeepSpeed, Megatron-LM, and Colossal-AI. We examine bug symptoms, root causes, bug identification and fixing efforts, and common low-effort fixing strategies. Additionally, the distributed nature of these frameworks introduces unique bug root causes, such as allocation strategy error and distributed communication error. Diagnosing and fixing complex bugs remains challenging due to factors like the disconnect between symptoms and root causes, high bug reproduction costs, and low-level or cross-component interactions. Interestingly, we observe that 48% of bug fixes require minimal code changes (<=10 LOC) and follow simple strategies such as conditional logic optimization, parameter handling enhancement, or version compatibility handling, indicating potential for automation. Based on these insights, we offer several implications for improving the reliability of both distributed training and inference frameworks and their dependent LLM projects, while also identifying opportunities to leverage LLM-based tools for automated debugging and repair.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper presents a large-scale empirical study of bugs found in three popular distributed training and inference frameworks for large language models (LLMs): DeepSpeed, Megatron-LM, and Colossal-AI. The authors analyze 308 resolved bugs, examining their symptoms, root causes, the effort required for identification and fixing, and common low-effort fixing strategies. The study identifies frequent symptoms (crash, incorrect functionality, build failure), prevalent root causes (API misuse, incorrect implementation, configuration errors, missing preconditions), and unique distributed-specific issues (allocation strategy errors, communication errors).  The paper finds that a significant portion of fixes are small and follow simple patterns, suggesting potential for automation. Based on these findings, the authors provide implications for framework developers, users, and researchers, highlighting areas for improvement in reliability, debugging, and automated repair.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in being the **first large-scale empirical study** specifically focusing on bugs within distributed training and inference frameworks for LLMs. While prior research has examined bugs in general machine learning and deep learning frameworks, this paper distinguishes itself by targeting the unique challenges posed by *distributed* LLM systems. This specialization is significant because these systems introduce complexities not present in single-machine or traditional distributed computing scenarios.
*   **Significance:** The significance stems from the increasing reliance on these frameworks for training and deploying massive LLMs. The study directly addresses the critical need for framework reliability, as bugs can lead to wasted computational resources, performance degradation, and unexpected failures.  The identification of common root causes, challenges in debugging, and potential automation strategies provides valuable insights for improving the quality and robustness of these frameworks.
*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Large-Scale Empirical Analysis:** The analysis of 308 bugs is a substantial contribution, providing a solid foundation for understanding the characteristics of these bugs.
    *   **Specific Focus on Distributed LLM Frameworks:**  The focus on the unique challenges of these systems sets it apart from prior work.
    *   **Practical Implications:** The paper offers actionable recommendations for framework developers, users, and researchers, directly addressing the identified problems.
    *   **Detailed Categorization:** The thorough classification of symptoms, root causes, and fixing strategies helps paint a clearer picture of bugs' life cycle within LLM training.
    *   **Data Availability:** Providing the annotated dataset publicly to facilitate future research is a major strength.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Generalizability:** The study is confined to three specific frameworks, and the findings might not be fully generalizable to *all* distributed LLM frameworks. However, the selected frameworks are widely used, representing a significant portion of the ecosystem.
