We present a fine-grained evaluation of uncertainty estimation methods for clinical multiple-choice question answering, covering ten open-source LLMs (general-purpose, biomedical, and reasoning models) across two datasets, eleven medical specialties, and six question types. We compare standard single-generation and sampling-based methods, and present a case study exploring simple, single-pass estimators based on behavioral signals in reasoning traces. These lightweight methods approach the performance of Semantic Entropy while requiring only one generation. Our results reveal substantial variation across specialties and question types, underscoring the importance of selecting models based on both the nature of the question and model-specific strengths.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the uncertainty and calibration of Large Language Models (LLMs) in clinical question answering (QA). It performs a fine-grained evaluation across ten open-source LLMs (general, biomedical, and reasoning-focused), two datasets, eleven medical specialties, and six question types. The study compares single-generation and sampling-based uncertainty estimation methods, introducing a case study exploring single-pass estimators based on behavioral signals in reasoning traces. The results reveal substantial variation in uncertainty calibration across specialties and question types, underscoring the importance of selecting models based on both the nature of the question and model-specific strengths. The paper finds that reasoning models can provide more reliable uncertainty estimates and introduces behavior signals such as length, self-posed questions, and occurrence of "wait" tokens.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper makes several important contributions that increase its novelty:

    *   **Fine-grained Evaluation:** The paper's most significant strength is its comprehensive, fine-grained evaluation of uncertainty in clinical QA. Prior works often lack depth, failing to examine variations across medical specialties and question types. This paper significantly advances the field by providing a more granular understanding of model performance.
    *   **Behavioral Signals:** The introduction of behavioral signals derived from the reasoning traces as lightweight proxies for uncertainty is relatively novel and promising. While related to self-consistency, it offers a computationally cheaper alternative to sampling-based methods, making it more suitable for resource-constrained environments.
    *   **Updated LLM Landscape:** The paper evaluates more recent LLMs than many earlier clinical NLP studies, including instruction-tuned and domain-specific models, providing a more up-to-date assessment of current capabilities.
    *   **Question type annotation:** the authors provided a new manual annotation that categorized the type of question with a high agreement.
*   **Significance:** The findings have significant implications for the deployment of LLMs in clinical settings.

    *   **Informing Model Selection:** The paper's results can guide practitioners in selecting the most appropriate model for specific clinical tasks based on the specialty and question type.
    *   **Highlighting Limitations:** The work effectively demonstrates that high accuracy doesn't guarantee well-calibrated uncertainty, emphasizing the need for thorough evaluation beyond simple accuracy metrics.
    *   **Promising Alternative for UQ:** The Behavioral approach to UQ, is a promising method for resource-constrained settings.

*   **Weaknesses:** While the paper presents valuable contributions, there are also some limitations:

    *   **Limited Behavioral Feature Space:** While the idea of behavioral features is novel, the features themselves (length, self-questioning, "Wait" tokens) are relatively simple and task-specific. Further investigation is needed to explore more sophisticated and generalizable features.
    *   **Dataset Bias:** Although the datasets are standard, there is still inherent bias in medical question-answering datasets. Future works could explore datasets with more representation of rare cases and different patient populations.
    *   **Limited to Multiple-Choice QA:** The focus on multiple-choice question answering limits the generalizability of the findings to other clinical NLP tasks that involve free-form text generation.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research in the following ways:

    *   **Encouraging Fine-Grained Evaluation:** The study's methodology sets a precedent for more rigorous and granular evaluations of LLMs in specialized domains.
    *   **Inspiring Novel Uncertainty Estimation Techniques:** The behavioral signal approach opens a new avenue for developing lightweight and interpretable uncertainty estimation methods.
    *   **Guiding Clinical Implementation:** The findings provide practical guidance for the responsible and effective implementation of LLMs in clinical decision support.

**Justification for Score:**

Considering both the strengths and weaknesses, the paper represents a valuable contribution to the field of clinical NLP. The fine-grained evaluation and the introduction of behavioral signals for uncertainty estimation are particularly noteworthy. However, the limitations regarding the dataset bias, the limited nature of the behavioral feature set, and narrow focus on multiple-choice Q&A prevent it from achieving a higher score. A score of '7' is appropriate, reflecting the paper's solid contributions and potential for future impact.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### ME: Trigger Element Combination Backdoor Attack on Copyright Infringement
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10776v1)
- **Authors**: Feiyu Yang, Siyuan Liang, Aishan Liu, Dacheng Tao
- **Abstract**: The capability of generative diffusion models (DMs) like Stable Diffusion (SD) in replicating training data could be taken advantage of by attackers to launch the Copyright Infringement Attack, with duplicated poisoned image-text pairs. SilentBadDiffusion (SBD) is a method proposed recently, which shew outstanding performance in attacking SD in text-to-image tasks. However, the feasible data resources in this area are still limited, some of them are even constrained or prohibited due to the issues like copyright ownership or inappropriate contents; And not all of the images in current datasets are suitable for the proposed attacking methods; Besides, the state-of-the-art (SoTA) performance of SBD is far from ideal when few generated poisoning samples could be adopted for attacks. In this paper, we raised new datasets accessible for researching in attacks like SBD, and proposed Multi-Element (ME) attack method based on SBD by increasing the number of poisonous visual-text elements per poisoned sample to enhance the ability of attacking, while importing Discrete Cosine Transform (DCT) for the poisoned samples to maintain the stealthiness. The Copyright Infringement Rate (CIR) / First Attack Epoch (FAE) we got on the two new datasets were 16.78% / 39.50 and 51.20% / 23.60, respectively close to or even outperformed benchmark Pokemon and Mijourney datasets. In condition of low subsampling ratio (5%, 6 poisoned samples), MESI and DCT earned CIR / FAE of 0.23% / 84.00 and 12.73% / 65.50, both better than original SBD, which failed to attack at all.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and a critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary**

The paper addresses the copyright infringement risks associated with diffusion models (DMs), specifically focusing on Stable Diffusion (SD). It builds upon the SilentBadDiffusion (SBD) attack, which inserts backdoors into DMs via poisoned image-text pairs. The paper introduces two new datasets (Style and DiffusionDB) to address limitations in existing datasets due to copyright and content restrictions. It also proposes a modified attack method, Multi-Element (ME), which increases the number of trigger elements in poisoned samples and incorporates Discrete Cosine Transform (DCT) to maintain stealthiness. The experiments demonstrate that the proposed datasets and ME+DCT attack achieve comparable or even superior performance to the original SBD method, especially in scenarios with low subsampling ratios (where only a small fraction of poisoned samples are used).

**Critical Evaluation**

*   **Novelty:**
    *   The introduction of the Style and DiffusionDB datasets fills a gap in the availability of accessible and legally less problematic datasets for copyright infringement attack research. This is a valuable contribution, as it enables further research in this area without the legal constraints of existing datasets.
    *   The ME attack method, which combines multiple trigger elements with DCT, is a non-trivial extension of SBD.  The idea of enhancing attack effectiveness by increasing the density of trigger elements per sample is intuitive, but the combination with DCT to preserve stealthiness is a well-reasoned and potentially impactful approach.
*   **Significance:**
    *   The paper highlights the vulnerability of diffusion models to copyright infringement attacks, which is an important concern given the widespread use of these models. The improvements to the SBD attack make these attacks more practical, particularly in resource-constrained scenarios.
    *   The results on the new datasets provide evidence that these attacks can be effective on a wider range of data, which is significant for the generalizability of the findings.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   The paper is well-structured and clearly explains the methodology.
    *   The experimental evaluation is comprehensive, with comparisons to the baseline SBD method and evaluations across different datasets and subsampling ratios.
    *   The inclusion of visual and statistical evidence of stealthiness adds to the credibility of the proposed method.
    *   The thorough discussion of limitations and future work is a sign of careful research.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   While the ME+DCT attack improves performance in low subsampling ratio scenarios, it is still not clear how it scales to larger datasets and more complex models.  The experiments are somewhat limited in scale.
    *   The stealthiness analysis, while present, could be more rigorous. The UMAP visualization is helpful, but a more quantitative analysis of the perceptual difference between poisoned and clean samples would strengthen this claim.
    *   The ABL defense method does not seems to make the attacks too difficult.

*   **Impact:**
    *   The paper's findings could inform the development of more robust defenses against copyright infringement attacks on diffusion models. The new datasets and attack methods provide valuable tools for researchers in this area.
    *   The paper could raise awareness among developers and users of diffusion models about the potential risks of copyright infringement.

**Justification for Score**

This paper makes a significant contribution to the study of copyright infringement attacks on diffusion models, particularly in addressing the issue of data accessibility and improving attack effectiveness. The combination of novel datasets, a well-designed attack method (ME+DCT), and a thorough experimental evaluation make this a valuable addition to the field. However, it's essential to acknowledge the limitations related to scalability, the strength of the stealthiness claims, and the fact that only relatively simple defensive mechanisms were tested. Despite these limitations, the paper offers practical insights and provides a foundation for future research in this area.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CR
- **Score**: 7/10

### What Users Value and Critique: Large-Scale Analysis of User Feedback on AI-Powered Mobile Apps
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10785v1)
- **Authors**: Vinaik Chhetri, Krishna Upadhyay, A. B. Siddique, Umar Farooq
- **Abstract**: Artificial Intelligence (AI)-powered features have rapidly proliferated across mobile apps in various domains, including productivity, education, entertainment, and creativity. However, how users perceive, evaluate, and critique these AI features remains largely unexplored, primarily due to the overwhelming volume of user feedback. In this work, we present the first comprehensive, large-scale study of user feedback on AI-powered mobile apps, leveraging a curated dataset of 292 AI-driven apps across 14 categories with 894K AI-specific reviews from Google Play. We develop and validate a multi-stage analysis pipeline that begins with a human-labeled benchmark and systematically evaluates large language models (LLMs) and prompting strategies. Each stage, including review classification, aspect-sentiment extraction, and clustering, is validated for accuracy and consistency. Our pipeline enables scalable, high-precision analysis of user feedback, extracting over one million aspect-sentiment pairs clustered into 18 positive and 15 negative user topics. Our analysis reveals that users consistently focus on a narrow set of themes: positive comments emphasize productivity, reliability, and personalized assistance, while negative feedback highlights technical failures (e.g., scanning and recognition), pricing concerns, and limitations in language support. Our pipeline surfaces both satisfaction with one feature and frustration with another within the same review. These fine-grained, co-occurring sentiments are often missed by traditional approaches that treat positive and negative feedback in isolation or rely on coarse-grained analysis. To this end, our approach provides a more faithful reflection of the real-world user experiences with AI-powered apps. Category-aware analysis further uncovers both universal drivers of satisfaction and domain-specific frustrations.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper presents a large-scale analysis of user feedback on AI-powered mobile apps. The authors curate a dataset of 292 AI-driven apps across 14 categories, analyzing 894K AI-specific reviews from Google Play. They develop a multi-stage analysis pipeline using Large Language Models (LLMs) to classify reviews, extract aspect-sentiment pairs, and cluster the resulting data. This pipeline enables a scalable analysis, extracting over one million aspect-sentiment pairs and revealing prevalent user topics. The analysis identifies common positive sentiments (productivity, reliability, personalized assistance) and negative sentiments (technical failures, pricing, language support limitations). The study also explores the co-occurrence of positive and negative feedback within the same review and how these sentiments vary across app categories. The authors conclude with actionable recommendations for developers to align AI features with user expectations.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

The paper's novelty lies primarily in its scale and comprehensive approach. While previous studies have explored user feedback in mobile apps and AI-related issues, this work is the first to conduct a large-scale systematic analysis specifically focused on AI-powered features across a wide range of app categories. The use of LLMs for review classification, aspect-sentiment extraction, and clustering is relatively new in this specific domain, though the individual techniques are established in NLP. The identification of co-occurring positive and negative feedback also adds nuance, going beyond simple sentiment analysis.

However, the individual components of the methodology (LLM usage, clustering, topic analysis) aren't radically new. The application is novel, but the building blocks are established. The insights, while valuable, largely confirm what one might intuitively expect (e.g., users value reliability, dislike pricing issues). The paper may be considered a "strong engineering effort" that systematically confirms and quantifies pre-existing assumptions.

**Significance:**

The significance of the paper stems from its ability to provide actionable insights for developers. The study identifies key areas for improvement in AI-powered mobile apps, helping developers prioritize their efforts and align their features with user expectations. The category-aware analysis further allows for targeted improvements in specific domains. The released dataset and pipeline code are valuable resources for future research in this area.

However, the impact could be greater.  The analysis primarily focuses on *what* users say, rather than *why* they say it.  A deeper investigation into the root causes of certain frustrations (e.g., why specific AI features are perceived as unreliable) could lead to even more impactful recommendations. The paper is valuable for providing a broad overview, but it lacks depth in exploring the underlying user psychology and specific design flaws that contribute to the observed issues.

**Strengths:**

*   **Scale:** Large dataset and comprehensive analysis.
*   **Methodology:** Well-defined, multi-stage pipeline.
*   **Actionable Insights:** Provides practical recommendations for developers.
*   **Reproducibility:** Publicly available dataset and code.
*   **Systematic:**  Covers a broad range of AI applications and user concerns

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Novelty of Individual Techniques:** Relies on existing NLP techniques.
*   **Confirmatory Insights:** Mostly confirms what might be expected intuitively.
*   **Lack of Depth:** Lacks a deeper investigation of user psychology and root causes.
*   **Focus on Explicit Feedback:** Heavily relies on explicitly stated opinions within reviews

**Justification for Score:**

The paper makes a valuable contribution by providing a large-scale systematic analysis of user feedback on AI-powered mobile apps. The use of LLMs for this task is relatively new, and the insights generated are actionable for developers. The publicly available dataset and code are also valuable resources for future research. However, the paper's novelty is limited by its reliance on existing NLP techniques, and its impact is somewhat reduced by the lack of depth in exploring the underlying user psychology and design flaws.

Score: 7

**Rigorous rationale for the score:**

The paper doesn't revolutionize the field, but it provides a very solid and useful systematic analysis of a growing area. A score of 7 reflects a significant contribution (given the scale and comprehensiveness) with some limitations in both novelty and the depth of generated insights. This places it above average, showing that its contribution provides an impactful direction. However, it is not exceptional enough to reach the higher echelons of truly ground-breaking research. The analysis confirms rather than disrupts existing understanding.

- **Classification**: cs.SE
- **Score**: 7/10

### FASCIST-O-METER: Classifier for Neo-fascist Discourse Online
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10789v1)
- **Authors**: Rudy Alexandro Garrido Veliz, Martin Semmann, Chris Biemann, Seid Muhie Yimam
- **Abstract**: Neo-fascism is a political and societal ideology that has been having remarkable growth in the last decade in the United States of America (USA), as well as in other Western societies. It poses a grave danger to democracy and the minorities it targets, and it requires active actions against it to avoid escalation. This work presents the first-of-its-kind neo-fascist coding scheme for digital discourse in the USA societal context, overseen by political science researchers. Our work bridges the gap between Natural Language Processing (NLP) and political science against this phenomena. Furthermore, to test the coding scheme, we collect a tremendous amount of activity on the internet from notable neo-fascist groups (the forums of Iron March and Stormfront.org), and the guidelines are applied to a subset of the collected posts. Through crowdsourcing, we annotate a total of a thousand posts that are labeled as neo-fascist or non-neo-fascist. With this labeled data set, we fine-tune and test both Small Language Models (SLMs) and Large Language Models (LLMs), obtaining the very first classification models for neo-fascist discourse. We find that the prevalence of neo-fascist rhetoric in this kind of forum is ever-present, making them a good target for future research. The societal context is a key consideration for neo-fascist speech when conducting NLP research. Finally, the work against this kind of political movement must be pressed upon and continued for the well-being of a democratic society. Disclaimer: This study focuses on detecting neo-fascist content in text, similar to other hate speech analyses, without labeling individuals or organizations.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "FASCIST-O-METER: Classifier for Neo-fascist Discourse Online":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel approach to identifying neo-fascist discourse online in the context of the United States. It presents a coding scheme developed in consultation with political science researchers to address the complex nature of neo-fascist ideology. The authors collected data from neo-fascist forums (Iron March and Stormfront.org), annotated a subset using crowdsourcing, and trained both Small Language Models (SLMs) and Large Language Models (LLMs) to classify neo-fascist discourse. The paper highlights the prevalence of such rhetoric on these forums, discusses the challenges in annotating this type of content, and explores how language models perform in this task. It concludes by emphasizing the importance of continued research to combat neo-fascism online.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

The paper's primary claim to novelty is being the "first-of-its-kind" neo-fascist coding scheme and classifier for digital discourse. While hate speech detection is a well-established area of NLP, the specific focus on neo-fascist discourse, guided by political science expertise, offers a valuable, new perspective. The collection of a dataset from specific forums known for such activity and the subsequent annotation process are also novel in this domain.

**Significance:**

The work addresses a growing societal concern: the rise of neo-fascism and its impact on democracy. Identifying and understanding neo-fascist rhetoric online is crucial for developing strategies to counter its spread and mitigate its potential harms. The paper contributes to this effort by:

*   Providing a concrete coding scheme, which can serve as a basis for future research.
*   Creating a labeled dataset, which is a valuable resource for training and evaluating machine learning models.
*   Demonstrating the feasibility of using NLP techniques to identify neo-fascist discourse.
*   Raising awareness about the importance of societal context and theoretical underpinnings in hate speech research.

**Strengths:**

*   **Interdisciplinary Approach:** The collaboration between NLP researchers and political scientists strengthens the validity and relevance of the work.
*   **Well-Defined Scope:** By focusing on the US context, the authors are able to develop a more targeted and nuanced coding scheme.
*   **Comprehensive Methodology:** The paper describes a complete pipeline, from data collection and annotation to model training and evaluation.
*   **Ethical Considerations:** The inclusion of ethical considerations is important, demonstrating awareness of the potential risks associated with this type of research.
*   **Thoroughness:** The work is thorough, with well-explained theoretical underpinnings, dataset collection procedures, and model selection methodologies. The inclusion of an error analysis adds depth to the study.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Dataset Size:** The annotated dataset of 1000 posts is relatively small, especially for training robust machine learning models, and so conclusions from this small dataset are tentative. It is unclear whether the results would hold with more annotated data.
*   **Crowdsourcing Limitations:** While the authors implemented quality control measures, crowdsourcing annotations for politically charged content can be subjective. The inter-rater reliability (Kappa) values should have been thoroughly scrutinized and reported. More information on the annotator pool would also be valuable.
*   **LLM Evaluation Methodology:** The hyperparameter tuning for the LLMs was cursory. A more systematic approach could have yielded better results.
*   **Generalizability:** The models were trained on data from two specific forums. How well they generalize to other online platforms is unclear.
*   **Lack of comparative analysis**: Other recent studies use advanced transformer models in the hate speech detection task, and benchmarking against those models would have strengthened the paper.

**Overall Impact and Justification for Score:**

The paper represents a significant first step towards using NLP to tackle the complex problem of neo-fascist discourse online. While there are weaknesses related to dataset size, annotation, and LLM Evaluation, the novelty of the coding scheme and the potential impact on future research outweigh these limitations. This paper's value is as a foundational study, providing a methodology and baseline for others to build upon.

**Score: 6**

- **Classification**: cs.CY
- **Score**: 6/10

### Mitigating Negative Interference in Multilingual Sequential Knowledge Editing through Null-Space Constraints
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10800v1)
- **Authors**: Wei Sun, Tingyu Qu, Mingxiao Li, Jesse Davis, Marie-Francine Moens
- **Abstract**: Efficiently updating multilingual knowledge in large language models (LLMs), while preserving consistent factual representations across languages, remains a long-standing and unresolved challenge. While deploying separate editing systems for each language might seem viable, this approach incurs substantial costs due to the need to manage multiple models. A more efficient solution involves integrating knowledge updates across all languages into a unified model. However, performing sequential edits across languages often leads to destructive parameter interference, significantly degrading multilingual generalization and the accuracy of injected knowledge. To address this challenge, we propose LangEdit, a novel null-space constrained framework designed to precisely isolate language-specific knowledge updates. The core innovation of LangEdit lies in its ability to project parameter updates for each language onto the orthogonal complement of previous updated subspaces. This approach mathematically guarantees update independence while preserving multilingual generalization capabilities. We conduct a comprehensive evaluation across three model architectures, six languages, and four downstream tasks, demonstrating that LangEdit effectively mitigates parameter interference and outperforms existing state-of-the-art editing methods. Our results highlight its potential for enabling efficient and accurate multilingual knowledge updates in LLMs. The code is available at https://github.com/VRCMF/LangEdit.git.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the challenge of multilingual sequential knowledge editing in large language models (LLMs). The authors identify a problem they call "negative interference," where editing knowledge in one language degrades performance in other languages. They propose a novel framework called "LangEdit" which uses null-space constraints to isolate language-specific knowledge updates. LangEdit projects parameter updates for each language onto the orthogonal complement of previously updated subspaces, ensuring update independence and preserving multilingual generalization.  The paper presents experimental results across several model architectures, languages, and downstream tasks, demonstrating that LangEdit mitigates parameter interference and outperforms existing editing methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper introduces a practical and theoretically grounded approach to a challenging problem in multilingual LLMs. The concept of using null-space projection for multilingual sequential knowledge editing appears to be a novel application of established techniques. While null-space projection itself isn't new, its use to decouple knowledge edits across languages within a single shared LLM is a significant contribution.  This approach directly contrasts with naive approaches of independently editing or retraining models per language. The paper successfully ties the method to mitigation of *negative interference*, offering a specific hypothesis and evaluation concerning this phenomenon.

*   **Significance:**  The significance lies in the ability to efficiently and accurately update factual knowledge in multilingual LLMs without incurring substantial costs or degrading existing knowledge. This is crucial for real-world applications where models need to adapt to new information across different languages. The problem tackled is practical, and the solution allows for better management of multilingual LLMs. The paper provides strong empirical evidence to support its claims, comparing LangEdit against several baseline methods and across multiple datasets and models.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Clear problem definition and motivation.
    *   Well-defined theoretical framework.
    *   Comprehensive experimental evaluation with multiple models, languages, and tasks.
    *   Convincing demonstration of mitigating negative interference.
    *   The code release enhances reproducibility and wider adoption of the method.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   Computational complexity: The computation of covariance matrices (and SVD) could be costly, especially with very large knowledge bases. Although the paper discusses using approximate methods, the scaling behavior compared to methods like direct fine-tuning could be a concern.
    *   While the paper highlights scaling benefits, the scalability of LangEdit wasn't explicitly demonstrated on significantly larger LLMs (e.g., 70B+ parameter models).
    *   The evaluation primarily focuses on factual knowledge editing. It's unclear how well LangEdit would generalize to other types of knowledge or skills, such as reasoning or code generation.

*   **Impact:** The paper has the potential to influence future research in multilingual LLMs, particularly in the areas of knowledge editing, continual learning, and cross-lingual transfer. The "language safeguards" concept and null-space projection technique can inspire other researchers to develop more robust and efficient methods for updating multilingual models. The availability of the code makes it easier for other researchers to build upon and extend LangEdit.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a novel and technically sound solution to a significant problem in multilingual LLMs. The experimental results are compelling, and the paper provides a solid theoretical foundation for its approach. However, some limitations remain regarding scalability to much larger models and generalization to broader types of knowledge editing. The paper demonstrates that negative interference is a real issue, and LangEdit provides a valuable technique to combat this. Therefore, based on these arguments, and taking into account the caveats, the score is as follows:

**Score: 7.5**
- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Detecting High-Stakes Interactions with Activation Probes
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10805v1)
- **Authors**: Alex McKenzie, Urja Pawar, Phil Blandfort, William Bankes, David Krueger, Ekdeep Singh Lubana, Dmitrii Krasheninnikov
- **Abstract**: Monitoring is an important aspect of safely deploying Large Language Models (LLMs). This paper examines activation probes for detecting "high-stakes" interactions -- where the text indicates that the interaction might lead to significant harm -- as a critical, yet underexplored, target for such monitoring. We evaluate several probe architectures trained on synthetic data, and find them to exhibit robust generalization to diverse, out-of-distribution, real-world data. Probes' performance is comparable to that of prompted or finetuned medium-sized LLM monitors, while offering computational savings of six orders-of-magnitude. Our experiments also highlight the potential of building resource-aware hierarchical monitoring systems, where probes serve as an efficient initial filter and flag cases for more expensive downstream analysis. We release our novel synthetic dataset and codebase to encourage further study.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Detecting High-Stakes Interactions with Activation Probes":

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the use of activation probes for detecting "high-stakes" interactions in Large Language Models (LLMs). High-stakes interactions are defined as those where the text suggests the interaction might lead to significant harm. The authors train several probe architectures on synthetic data generated using GPT-4o and evaluate their generalization capability on various diverse, out-of-distribution, real-world datasets.  They compare probes against finetuned and prompted LLM monitors. The core findings are that activation probes can achieve performance comparable to medium-sized LLMs while offering significantly better computational efficiency. The paper also explores a hierarchical monitoring system where probes are used as an initial filter to flag interactions for further, more computationally expensive analysis with full LLMs. The authors release their synthetic dataset and codebase.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The application of activation probes to detect specifically *high-stakes* interactions is a relatively novel approach. While probes themselves are not new, focusing on a specific type of interaction with real-world consequences is a valuable contribution. The generation of a synthetic dataset tailored for high-stakes scenarios is also a positive aspect. Previous work with linear probes mostly focused on tasks unrelated to identifying potential harm (like truth detection). So the task itself and the application of probes to this task presents a degree of novelty.

*   **Significance:** The potential impact of this work is significant. The efficient detection of high-stakes interactions is crucial for the safe deployment of LLMs in real-world settings. The paper provides evidence that probes can be a cost-effective way to implement initial monitoring, potentially reducing the resources required for continuous monitoring. It addresses a pressing need given the growing use of LLMs in consequential decision-making. The hierarchical monitoring strategy is also promising and aligns with practical deployment considerations.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Robust Evaluation:** The evaluation is a strong point. By using several diverse, real-world datasets, the authors demonstrate the generalization capabilities of their probes, and the comparison against LLMs highlights its potential impact.
    *   **Cost-Effectiveness:** Demonstrating a probe's ability to achieve performance comparable to LLMs while being computationally cheaper is a key strength. This makes it a valuable solution for practical deployment.
    *   **Hierarchical Approach:** Proposing and evaluating a tiered monitoring system that utilizes probes as a first pass filter, and selectively routes to more complex models is highly practical.
    *   **Open Resources:** Releasing the synthetic dataset and code facilitates further research and development in this area.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Synthetic Data Dependence:**  While the probes generalize well to real-world datasets, the reliance on GPT-4o to generate synthetic training data is a limitation. There is a risk that the probes learn to mimic GPT-4o's understanding of "high-stakes" rather than a broader, more universally applicable concept. The manual analysis indicates that some probes can still be misled.
    *   **Definition of High-Stakes:** The definition of high-stakes interactions could be made more precise. The term encompasses many situations. A more refined classification of different types of high-stakes contexts would improve the task and probe development.
    *   **TPR at low FPR:** The results indicate the probes do not perform as well as LLMs in detecting High-Stakes contexts when configured for low False Positive Rates, which would be crucial in an interactive system.
    *   **Limited Baseline Optimization:** While comparisons are made against other baselines, the paper admits to limited hyperparameter optimization for LLM baselines, making a conclusive comparison more difficult.
    *   **Cause-effect:** It is not clear what probe architectures and associated training dynamics make the probes work well. This lack of transparency makes it difficult to generalize/extrapolate findings and apply them to different use cases.

*   **Potential Influence:**  The paper has the potential to influence how LLM monitoring systems are designed and implemented. The results encourage the use of probes as a practical and cost-effective tool for early detection of potentially harmful interactions. The release of the dataset will also foster further research and development in this area.

**Justification for the Score:**

Despite its weaknesses (primarily concerning the dependence on synthetic data generation and the relative lack of comparative performance to LLM baselines in low FPR situations), the paper presents a valuable contribution to the field. It offers a cost-effective and efficient method for detecting high-stakes interactions. The thorough evaluation and hierarchical monitoring system strengthen its practical relevance. The release of data and code supports future research.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Prompts to Summaries: Zero-Shot Language-Guided Video Summarization
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10807v1)
- **Authors**: Mario Barbara, Alaa Maalouf
- **Abstract**: The explosive growth of video data intensified the need for flexible user-controllable summarization tools that can operate without domain-specific training data. Existing methods either rely on datasets, limiting generalization, or cannot incorporate user intent expressed in natural language. We introduce Prompts-to-Summaries: the first zero-shot, text-queryable video summarizer that converts off-the-shelf video-language models (VidLMs) captions into user-guided skims via large language models (LLMs) judging, without the use of training data at all, beating all unsupervised and matching supervised methods. Our pipeline (i) segments raw video footage into coherent scenes, (ii) generates rich scene-level descriptions through a memory-efficient, batch-style VidLM prompting scheme that scales to hours-long videos on a single GPU, (iii) leverages an LLM as a judge to assign scene-level importance scores under a carefully crafted prompt, and finally, (iv) propagates those scores to short segments level via two new metrics: consistency (temporal coherency) and uniqueness (novelty), yielding fine-grained frame importance. On SumMe and TVSum, our data-free approach surpasses all prior data-hungry unsupervised methods. It also performs competitively on the Query-Focused Video Summarization (QFVS) benchmark, despite using no training data and the competing methods requiring supervised frame-level importance. To spur further research, we release VidSum-Reason, a new query-driven dataset featuring long-tailed concepts and multi-step reasoning; our framework attains robust F1 scores and serves as the first challenging baseline. Overall, our results demonstrate that pretrained multimodal models, when orchestrated with principled prompting and score propagation, already provide a powerful foundation for universal, text-queryable video summarization.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces "Prompts to Summaries," a novel zero-shot, text-queryable video summarization framework.  It leverages pre-trained Video Language Models (VidLMs) to generate scene descriptions from a segmented video and then utilizes Large Language Models (LLMs) with carefully designed prompts to score the importance of each scene based on the video content and optionally a user-provided text query. The framework refines these scene scores down to the frame level, resulting in a summarized video. The key innovation is the ability to create user-guided summaries without any task-specific training or fine-tuning of the models, achieving state-of-the-art results compared to unsupervised methods and competitive performance with supervised methods. The authors also introduce a new benchmark, VidSum-Reason, to evaluate fine-grained, reasoning-driven video summarization.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its **zero-shot approach** to video summarization using a combination of VidLMs and LLMs. While VidLMs and LLMs have been applied in video understanding tasks previously, their orchestration in a data-free, queryable summarization pipeline is a significant contribution. The carefully engineered prompts that enable LLMs to act as effective scene judges are a key innovation. The introduction of the VidSum-Reason dataset, designed to evaluate more nuanced reasoning and comprehension capabilities for video summarization systems, is also valuable and helps address limitations in existing datasets.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance stems from its potential to address the practical limitations of existing video summarization methods. By eliminating the need for task-specific training data, the framework is highly adaptable and can be applied to a wide variety of video domains without requiring significant manual effort. This is a major advantage over supervised approaches that are often limited by the availability and cost of labeled data. Furthermore, the framework's ability to incorporate user intent through natural language queries provides a more flexible and personalized summarization experience, which is a key feature for many real-world applications.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Strong Experimental Results:** The paper presents comprehensive experimental results on standard video summarization datasets (SumMe, TVSum) demonstrating state-of-the-art performance compared to unsupervised methods and competitive performance with supervised methods.
    *   **Zero-Shot Capability:** The core strength is its ability to perform well without any training on the target dataset.
    *   **Queryability:** The framework seamlessly integrates user-defined textual queries to guide the summarization process.
    *   **New Dataset:** Introduces VidSum-Reason, a new dataset specifically designed to assess reasoning abilities.
    *   **Thorough Ablation Study:** The paper conducts a thorough ablation study that isolates the impact of key components, providing insights into the design choices and the effectiveness of different modules.
    *   **Scalability:** The proposed approach is demonstrated to be scalable.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Prompt Sensitivity:** The performance of the LLM-based scene scoring relies heavily on the prompt's design. The authors acknowledge this limitation and state that prompt stability still requires further study. This is a critical dependency.
    *   **Computational Overhead:** The use of VideoLMs and LLMs can result in significant computational overhead, particularly for longer videos. While the paper introduces optimizations to mitigate this, the resource requirements might still be a barrier to wider adoption.
    *   **Dataset limitations**: The VidSum-Reason is relatively small, with only 9 videos. A larger dataset would be beneficial for more robust evaluation.
    *   **Lack of Comparison with CLIP-It on VidSum-Reason**:  The authors only compare against a random baseline for the novel VidSum-Reason dataset. It would have been useful to compare the proposed method against CLIP-It, since its performance was better on QFVS.
    *   **Limited Discussion of Failure Cases:** While the paper highlights the strengths of the framework, there is limited discussion of potential failure cases or scenarios where the approach might struggle.

*   **Potential Influence:** This work can significantly impact the field of video summarization by establishing a new paradigm for data-free and user-guided approaches. It offers a viable alternative to traditional supervised methods, opening up new avenues for research and development in this area. Future research can build upon this work by focusing on improving the robustness and efficiency of LLM-based scene scoring, exploring different prompting strategies, and developing more sophisticated methods for integrating user intent. Additionally, a strong, thorough study into prompt stability could help increase trustworthiness in similar zero-shot methods.

**Justification for Score:**

Considering the paper's novelty, significance, strengths, and weaknesses, I assign it a score of **7**.

*   The zero-shot framework and introduction of VidSum-Reason represent a clear advance in the field.
*   The experimental results are compelling and demonstrate the effectiveness of the approach.
*   The method tackles a real and important problem with a practical solution, which makes it more impactful.
*   However, the dependence on carefully crafted prompts and the computational overhead limit its immediate widespread applicability. The small dataset size of the novel VidSum-Reason dataset does not allow for robust evaluation.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### VideoDeepResearch: Long Video Understanding With Agentic Tool Using
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10821v1)
- **Authors**: Huaying Yuan, Zheng Liu, Junjie Zhou, Ji-Rong Wen, Zhicheng Dou
- **Abstract**: Long video understanding (LVU) presents a significant challenge for current multi-modal large language models (MLLMs) due to the task's inherent complexity and context window constraint. It is widely assumed that addressing LVU tasks requires foundation MLLMs with extended context windows, strong visual perception capabilities, and proficient domain expertise. In this work, we challenge this common belief by introducing VideoDeepResearch, a novel agentic framework for long video understanding. Our approach relies solely on a text-only large reasoning model (LRM) combined with a modular multi-modal toolkit, including multimodal retrievers and visual perceivers, all of which are readily available in practice. For each LVU task, the system formulates a problem-solving strategy through reasoning, while selectively accessing and utilizing essential video content via tool using. We conduct extensive experiments on popular LVU benchmarks, including MLVU, Video-MME, and LVBench. Our results demonstrate that VideoDeepResearch achieves substantial improvements over existing MLLM baselines, surpassing the previous state-of-the-art by 9.6%, 6.6%, and 3.9% on MLVU (test), LVBench, and LongVideoBench, respectively. These findings highlight the promise of agentic systems in overcoming key challenges in LVU problems.
- **Summary**: Okay, I will provide a summary and a critical evaluation of the paper "VideoDeepResearch: Long Video Understanding With Agentic Tool Using".

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the challenge of long video understanding (LVU) for multimodal large language models (MLLMs). It proposes VideoDeepResearch, an agentic framework that utilizes a text-only large reasoning model (LRM) combined with a modular multi-modal toolkit. This toolkit includes readily available multi-modal retrievers and visual perceivers. The framework reasons about the LVU task, selectively accesses and utilizes essential video content via tools, and solves the problem in an iterative fashion. Experiments on MLVU, Video-MME, and LVBench benchmarks demonstrate significant improvements over existing MLLM baselines, suggesting the promise of agentic systems for LVU problems. The method shows improvements over state-of-the-art MLLMs while being more cost effective.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its agentic approach to LVU. While agentic systems and tool-using have been explored in other domains, applying them to LVU with a focus on long videos and leveraging readily available components is a contribution. The core idea of using a text-only LRM for high-level reasoning and delegating specific perception tasks to specialized tools is appealing. The iterative and selective retrieval strategy is more novel than just using a RAG architecture. This improves efficiency and reduces compute requirements.

*   **Significance:** The significance of this work is moderate. The empirical results demonstrate tangible improvements on established benchmarks, outperforming strong MLLMs like GPT-4o, Gemini 1.5 Pro and Qwen2.5-VL-72B. The results indicate a paradigm shift for LVU tasks by moving from a paradigm where solely extending the context window size is adequate to improve results to an intelligent strategy. This addresses the limitations of relying solely on enormous context windows of MLLMs, especially considering their computational cost. The emphasis on readily available and modular components also makes the framework more practical and accessible. The framework also outperforms previous retrieval-augmented generation strategies.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Strong empirical results:** The paper provides convincing evidence of the effectiveness of VideoDeepResearch across multiple LVU benchmarks.
    *   **Cost-effectiveness:** The agentic approach improves cost-effectiveness because it uses fewer computational resources than loading the entire video into MLLMs.
    *   **Modularity:** The modular design allows for easy integration of new and improved tools.
    *   **Clear Explanation and Methodology:** The paper provides clear explanations of the framework and experimental setup. The inference process and the tooling were clearly defined.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Dependency on retriever performance:** The performance heavily relies on the quality of the video clip retriever. The paper acknowledges underperformance in the EgoQA and SportsQA tasks, indicating the retriever module struggles on specific video types.
    *   **Limited novelty in tool selection:** While the overall framework is novel, the individual tools (retrievers, visual perceivers) are mostly off-the-shelf components. The contribution resides in orchestrating these tools effectively.
    *   **Limited Task types:** Tasks such as anomaly detection are better in certain instances but are not robust to more complex tasks.

*   **Potential Influence:** This work has the potential to influence future research in LVU by promoting agentic approaches and tool-using. It can encourage researchers to focus on designing better tools and reasoning strategies rather than solely relying on scaling up MLLMs. The use of accessible components can also lead to broader adoption of these techniques.

*   **Score:** 7

*   **Justification:** I assign a score of 7 because the paper introduces a novel and effective agentic framework for long video understanding. The framework demonstrates significant improvements over existing MLLM baselines. However, it also faces challenges with retriever performance in certain scenarios, relies on existing tools, and can potentially be improved with better modularity. The cost effectiveness of the strategy also makes it worthwhile as an alternative to simply extending the size of MLLMs.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### ReCUT: Balancing Reasoning Length and Accuracy in LLMs via Stepwise Trails and Preference Optimization
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10822v1)
- **Authors**: Zhensheng Jin, Xinze Li, Yifan Ji, Chunyi Peng, Zhenghao Liu, Qi Shi, Yukun Yan, Shuo Wang, Furong Peng, Ge Yu
- **Abstract**: Recent advances in Chain-of-Thought (CoT) prompting have substantially improved the reasoning capabilities of Large Language Models (LLMs). However, these methods often suffer from overthinking, leading to unnecessarily lengthy or redundant reasoning traces. Existing approaches attempt to mitigate this issue through curating multiple reasoning chains for training LLMs, but their effectiveness is often constrained by the quality of the generated data and prone to overfitting. To address the challenge, we propose Reasoning Compression ThroUgh Stepwise Trials (ReCUT), a novel method aimed at balancing the accuracy and length of reasoning trajectory. Specifically, ReCUT employs a stepwise exploration mechanism and a long-short switched sampling strategy, enabling LLMs to incrementally generate diverse reasoning paths. These paths are evaluated and used to construct preference pairs to train two specialized models (Gemini LLMs)-one optimized for reasoning accuracy, the other for shorter reasoning. A final integrated model is obtained by interpolating the parameters of these two models. Experimental results across multiple math reasoning datasets and backbone models demonstrate that ReCUT significantly reduces reasoning lengths by approximately 30-50%, while maintaining or improving reasoning accuracy compared to various baselines. All codes and data will be released via https://github.com/NEUIR/ReCUT.
- **Summary**: Okay, I'll provide a summary and a critical evaluation of the "ReCUT: Balancing Reasoning Length and Accuracy in LLMs via Stepwise Trials and Preference Optimization" paper, including a novelty and significance score.

**Summary**

The paper introduces ReCUT (Reasoning Compression Through Stepwise Trials), a novel method for balancing reasoning length and accuracy in large language models (LLMs). ReCUT addresses the problem of "overthinking" in LLMs, where they generate unnecessarily long and redundant reasoning chains. The core idea is to use a stepwise exploration mechanism combined with a long-short switched sampling strategy to generate diverse reasoning paths. These paths are then used to train two specialized "Gemini LLMs": one focused on accuracy and the other on brevity. Finally, parameter interpolation is used to create an integrated model that balances both aspects. Experimental results on math reasoning datasets show that ReCUT can significantly reduce reasoning lengths (30-50%) while maintaining or improving accuracy compared to baselines.

**Critical Evaluation**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Addresses a Relevant Problem:** Overthinking in LLMs is a significant issue affecting efficiency and, potentially, accuracy. The paper tackles a practical and growing concern.
    *   **Novel Approach:** The combination of stepwise exploration, long-short sampling, and preference optimization is a relatively novel approach to the problem.  It moves beyond simple prompt engineering or basic SFT.
    *   **Clear Methodology:** The paper clearly explains the ReCUT pipeline. The stepwise trial strategy and the dual Gemini LLM framework are well-defined.
    *   **Strong Empirical Results:** The experiments demonstrate significant reductions in reasoning length while maintaining or improving accuracy across various datasets and backbones. Ablation studies provide further insights into the contributions of each component. The results are quantifiable and convincing.
    *   **Mitigation of Overfitting:** The method is less prone to overfitting than directly synthesizing SFT data, as the stepwise approach allows for a broader exploration of solution spaces.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Reliance on Prompting and LLM Capability:** The effectiveness of ReCUT depends on the LLM's ability to follow the prompting instructions for generating reasoning chains, especially during the stepwise exploration phase. A failure to effectively follow the prompting reduces the potential quality and diversity of the generated reasoning paths. This is mentioned as a limitation in the paper.
    *   **Computational Cost:** While ReCUT aims to improve efficiency at inference time, the training process itself involves generating multiple reasoning trajectories and training two separate models. While the paper claims a reduction in inference cost, there is no explicit mention of an increase in training cost. The overall computational cost should be taken into account.
    *   **Limited Theoretical Justification:** While empirically effective, the paper lacks a deeper theoretical analysis of why the specific choices in ReCUT (e.g., the DARE-Ties parameters, the design of the reward function) are optimal. It would be beneficial to delve deeper into what the method learns and how it reshapes the model's reasoning representations.
    *   **Dependence on Math Reasoning:** The method is primarily evaluated in the math reasoning domain. The generalizability to other reasoning domains (e.g., commonsense reasoning, planning) could be further investigated.
    *   **Parameter Interpolation:** The implementation of parameter interpolation depends on DARE-Ties, which uses empirical findings.

*   **Novelty:**

    *   The *stepwise reasoning trajectory exploration* strategy is a novel way to generate diverse reasoning trajectories.
    *   The *long-short switched sampling* to diversify solution candidates is also a new contribution.
    *   The design and training of two *specialized "Gemini LLMs"*, is new.

*   **Significance:**
    * It makes LLMs more efficient in computation.
    * The balance between accuracy and efficiency contributes to practical use of LLMs.
    * Offers a framework to balance performance of LLMs.

**Overall Assessment:**

ReCUT presents a well-executed and empirically sound approach to address the overthinking problem in LLMs. The novelty lies in the specific combination of techniques, particularly the stepwise exploration and the creation/integration of specialized models.  While the reliance on prompting and the empirical nature of certain design choices are limitations, the clear methodology and significant experimental results make it a valuable contribution to the field.

**Score: 7.5**

**Rationale:** The paper addresses a relevant problem with a novel and empirically validated approach. The results are promising, and the method is well-defined. The limitations around prompting dependence, theoretical justification, and generalization to other domains prevent a higher score. The paper does offer a very practical approach to dealing with over-thinking LLMs. The integration of Gemini LLMs gives the model a unique approach to improving performance and accuracy.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Generalist Models in Medical Image Segmentation: A Survey and Performance Comparison with Task-Specific Approaches
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10825v1)
- **Authors**: Andrea Moglia, Matteo Leccardi, Matteo Cavicchioli, Alice Maccarini, Marco Marcon, Luca Mainardi, Pietro Cerveri
- **Abstract**: Following the successful paradigm shift of large language models, leveraging pre-training on a massive corpus of data and fine-tuning on different downstream tasks, generalist models have made their foray into computer vision. The introduction of Segment Anything Model (SAM) set a milestone on segmentation of natural images, inspiring the design of a multitude of architectures for medical image segmentation. In this survey we offer a comprehensive and in-depth investigation on generalist models for medical image segmentation. We start with an introduction on the fundamentals concepts underpinning their development. Then, we provide a taxonomy on the different declinations of SAM in terms of zero-shot, few-shot, fine-tuning, adapters, on the recent SAM 2, on other innovative models trained on images alone, and others trained on both text and images. We thoroughly analyze their performances at the level of both primary research and best-in-literature, followed by a rigorous comparison with the state-of-the-art task-specific models. We emphasize the need to address challenges in terms of compliance with regulatory frameworks, privacy and security laws, budget, and trustworthy artificial intelligence (AI). Finally, we share our perspective on future directions concerning synthetic data, early fusion, lessons learnt from generalist models in natural language processing, agentic AI and physical AI, and clinical translation.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Generalist Models in Medical Image Segmentation: A Survey and Performance Comparison with Task-Specific Approaches":

**Summary:**

This paper presents a comprehensive survey of generalist models in medical image segmentation, focusing on the shift from task-specific approaches to pre-train-and-adapt paradigms. It covers the fundamental concepts, various declinations of the Segment Anything Model (SAM) and SAM 2, other innovative models trained on images or text and images, and compares their performance with task-specific state-of-the-art models. The survey emphasizes the challenges of regulatory compliance, privacy, security, budget, and trustworthy AI and suggests future directions including synthetic data, lessons from NLP, and agentic/physical AI. The authors propose a unified taxonomy for generalist models, perform architectural dissection, construct a performance trajectory analysis, establish a performance leaderboard, and analyze regulatory and deployment constraints.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its comprehensive, comparative analysis of generalist models in the context of medical image segmentation. While surveys exist on individual models like SAM, this work offers a broader view, including SAM variants, models trained on images alone or text+images, and comparison against task-specific architectures. The proposed taxonomy and performance trajectory analysis are valuable contributions. The authors also address important practical considerations like regulatory compliance, a vital but often neglected aspect in AI research.
*   **Significance:** The paper is highly significant because it tackles a crucial emerging trend in medical imaging – the shift towards generalist AI. By thoroughly evaluating these models and comparing them to task-specific ones, the paper provides a valuable resource for researchers and practitioners in the field. This is particularly important as the community grapples with the trade-offs between generalizability, performance, computational cost, and real-world deployment considerations. Addressing issues like regulatory compliance and ethics also increases its practical relevance.
*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Comprehensive Coverage:** The survey provides a broad and deep analysis of various generalist models, their architectures, and adaptation methods.
    *   **Comparative Analysis:** Rigorous comparisons against state-of-the-art task-specific models on relevant datasets.
    *   **Taxonomy:** Well-defined and extensible taxonomy provides a structured framework.
    *   **Addressing Practical Concerns:** Highlighting regulatory, ethical, and deployment challenges, which are often overlooked.
    *   **Future Directions:** Identification of promising avenues for further research, such as synthetic data and agentic AI.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Empirical Validation:** While performance comparisons are presented, a more extensive empirical evaluation with standardized protocols across a wider range of datasets would strengthen the findings.
    *   **Lack of Specificity in Future Directions:** While the future directions are interesting, they can be more refined, with specific examples, and a discussion of feasibility.
    *   **Reliance on Publications for Data:** The survey relies on previously published results and doesn’t include a novel empirical investigation. This reliance can introduce biases from variations in experimental setup, metrics, and data handling across publications.
*   **Potential Influence:** This survey is likely to influence the field significantly by:

    *   Providing a clear understanding of the current landscape of generalist models in medical image segmentation.
    *   Guiding future research directions by highlighting promising areas and unsolved challenges.
    *   Raising awareness of practical considerations for deployment, such as regulatory compliance and ethical issues.

**Justification for Score:**

Given the comprehensive nature of the survey, insightful analysis of generalist models, the explicit discussion of practical limitations, and the proposition of future directions, this paper represents a substantial contribution to the field. Its thoroughness and practical focus make it a valuable resource for researchers and practitioners. While the paper lacks an extensive original empirical evaluation, the comprehensive synthesis of existing research more than compensates for it.

**Score: 8**
- **Classification**: eess.IV
- **Score**: 8/10

### LLM-Driven Personalized Answer Generation and Evaluation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10829v1)
- **Authors**: Mohammadreza Molavi, Mohammadreza Tavakoli, Mohammad Moein, Abdolali Faraji, Gábor Kismihók
- **Abstract**: Online learning has experienced rapid growth due to its flexibility and accessibility. Personalization, adapted to the needs of individual learners, is crucial for enhancing the learning experience, particularly in online settings. A key aspect of personalization is providing learners with answers customized to their specific questions. This paper therefore explores the potential of Large Language Models (LLMs) to generate personalized answers to learners' questions, thereby enhancing engagement and reducing the workload on educators. To evaluate the effectiveness of LLMs in this context, we conducted a comprehensive study using the StackExchange platform in two distinct areas: language learning and programming. We developed a framework and a dataset for validating automatically generated personalized answers. Subsequently, we generated personalized answers using different strategies, including 0-shot, 1-shot, and few-shot scenarios. The generated answers were evaluated using three methods: 1. BERTScore, 2. LLM evaluation, and 3. human evaluation. Our findings indicated that providing LLMs with examples of desired answers (from the learner or similar learners) can significantly enhance the LLMs' ability to tailor responses to individual learners' needs.
- **Summary**: Okay, I will summarize the paper, and then provide a critical evaluation with a score and detailed justification.

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the use of Large Language Models (LLMs) for generating personalized answers to learners' questions in online education settings. The goal is to improve learner engagement and reduce the burden on educators. The authors created a dataset from StackExchange focusing on language learning and programming domains, where users explicitly indicate accepted answers. They then generated personalized answers using GPT-4 with different prompting strategies: 0-shot, 1-shot (using the user's previous accepted answer), 3-shot (using the user's three previous accepted answers), and similar-user 1-shot and 3-shot. The generated answers were evaluated using BERTScore, LLM-based evaluation, and human evaluation. The results suggest that providing LLMs with examples of desired answers, particularly those from the same user, significantly improves their ability to generate tailored responses.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Strengths:**

*   **Relevance and Timeliness:** The research addresses a critical and timely issue in online education: personalization. The increasing reliance on online learning necessitates tools that can adapt to individual learner needs. Exploring LLMs for personalized answer generation is highly relevant in this context.
*   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper employs a multifaceted evaluation approach, combining automated metrics (BERTScore), LLM-based evaluation, and human expert judgment. This strengthens the validity of the findings.  The use of GPT-4 to evaluate other generated answers adds an interesting dimension to the study, showing how LLMs can be used not only for generating content but also for assessing its quality.
*   **Dataset Creation:** Creating a dataset specifically for personalized answer generation is a valuable contribution in itself. Publicly releasing such a dataset would further enhance its impact. The clever use of StackExchange data and the focus on accepted answers as a proxy for user preference are well-reasoned.
*   **Clear Methodology:** The methodology is clearly described, making it easy to understand and replicate the study. The different prompting strategies are well-defined.
*   **Practical Implications:** The findings provide actionable insights for educators and developers of online learning platforms. Knowing that providing even a few examples of desired answers can improve personalization is a valuable takeaway.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Novelty in Approach:** The prompt engineering strategies (0-shot, 1-shot, few-shot) are well-established in the LLM literature. The application of these techniques to personalized answer generation is novel, but the techniques themselves are not.
*   **Focus on Superficial Personalization:** The personalization achieved is somewhat superficial. It primarily focuses on stylistic adaptation based on previous answers. Deeper personalization, such as tailoring answers based on learner's knowledge level or learning style, is not addressed. The paper only captures aspects of personalization in previous users' answers, so although it is a good first step, there is room to improve and grow.
*   **Dependence on GPT-4:** The evaluation heavily relies on GPT-4 as both a generator and an evaluator. This raises concerns about potential biases inherent in the model.  While using LLMs for evaluation is gaining traction, a more diverse range of evaluation methods would further strengthen the claims. Also, the authors need to clearly report the version of GPT-4 used.
*   **Generalizability Concerns:** The dataset is limited to StackExchange data from only two domains (language learning and programming). This raises questions about the generalizability of the findings to other subjects and platforms.
*   **Lack of Error Analysis:** While the paper presents quantitative results, it lacks a detailed qualitative error analysis. Identifying specific types of errors made by LLMs in different scenarios would provide valuable insights for future research. It is useful to understand the 'why' behind the GPT-4 decisions to better understand how to refine personalized answers.
*   **Absence of Statistical Significance:** The results section presents percentage improvements, but it doesn't include any statistical significance testing. This weakens the conclusions as we cannot be certain that the observed differences are not due to chance.

**Significance:**

The paper contributes to the growing body of research on using LLMs in education. It provides empirical evidence that LLMs can be effectively used to generate personalized answers, thus enhancing the learning experience and reducing educator workload. The insights gained from this study can inform the development of AI-powered tools for online learning. While the technical novelty is limited, the problem it addresses is important, and the empirical evaluation is well-conducted. Also, the LLM evaluation with GPT4 adds an interesting layer to the evaluation of the generated answers.

**Justification for Score:**

Considering the strengths and weaknesses, I assign a score of **6.5**. While the paper addresses a relevant problem and provides empirical evidence of the effectiveness of LLMs in personalized answer generation, it lacks significant technical novelty and has some limitations in generalizability and statistical rigor. The reliance on existing prompting techniques and the focus on superficial personalization diminish its overall impact. However, the comprehensive evaluation and the practical implications for online learning platforms make it a worthwhile contribution to the field.

**Score: 6.5**

- **Classification**: cs.CY
- **Score**: 6/10

### Evaluating Large Language Models on Non-Code Software Engineering Tasks
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10833v1)
- **Authors**: Fabian C. Peña, Steffen Herbold
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) have demonstrated remarkable capabilities in code understanding and generation; however, their effectiveness on non-code Software Engineering (SE) tasks remains underexplored. We present the first comprehensive benchmark, which we name `Software Engineering Language Understanding' (SELU), for evaluating LLMs on 17 non-code tasks, spanning from identifying whether a requirement is functional or non-functional to estimating the effort and complexity of backlog items. SELU covers classification, regression, Named Entity Recognition (NER), and Masked Language Modeling (MLM) targets, with data drawn from diverse sources such as code repositories, issue tracking systems, and developer forums. We fine-tune 22 open-source LLMs, prompt two proprietary alternatives, and train two baselines. Performance is measured using metrics such as F1-macro, SMAPE, F1-micro, and accuracy, and compared via the Bayesian signed-rank test. Our results show that moderate-scale decoder-only models consistently form a top-tier, exhibiting high mean performance and low across-task variance, while domain adaptation via code-focused pre-training might yield only modest improvements. These insights guide model selection for non-code SE workflows and highlight directions for expanding SELU to generative and design-oriented scenarios.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces SELU (Software Engineering Language Understanding), a new benchmark for evaluating Large Language Models (LLMs) on 17 non-code tasks within the software engineering life cycle.  These tasks span activities from requirements analysis to maintenance and management, including classification, regression, Named Entity Recognition (NER), and Masked Language Modeling (MLM). The authors fine-tune 22 open-source LLMs, prompt two proprietary models, and train two classical machine learning baselines. The models are evaluated using metrics like F1-macro, SMAPE, F1-micro, and accuracy, and compared statistically using a Bayesian signed-rank test.  The results focus on decoder-only models and the effectiveness of domain adaptation.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary strength lies in its focus on evaluating LLMs on *non-code* software engineering tasks. While there has been extensive work on code generation and understanding, SELU addresses a significant gap in the literature by focusing on the less-explored but equally important aspects of the SE lifecycle that rely on Natural Language Understanding (NLU). The creation of a comprehensive benchmark with diverse task types and sources adds significant value. However, the individual tasks included in SELU are not new; the novelty comes from the *combination* and systematic evaluation across a diverse set of models.
*   **Significance:** The paper's findings offer actionable insights for practitioners. The identification of moderate-scale decoder-only models as a consistent top-tier performer for non-code SE tasks provides practical guidance for model selection. The observation that code-focused pre-training provides only modest improvements (compared to architectural and scaling effects) challenges the assumption that simply pre-training on code data automatically enhances LLM performance on *all* SE tasks. Highlighting task-specific performance differences (e.g., the effect of scaling on multi-label classification) helps refine resource allocation strategies.

**Strengths:**

*   **Comprehensive Benchmark:** SELU provides a valuable resource for the community.
*   **Rigorous Evaluation:** The use of Bayesian signed-rank tests enhances the robustness of the model comparison.
*   **Actionable Insights:** The study translates findings into practical guidelines for model selection and deployment.
*   **Replication Kit:**  Providing code, models, datasets, and results ensures reproducibility.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Task Coverage Limitations:** While diverse, the 17 tasks might not fully represent the breadth of non-code SE activities, especially regarding the tasks in Design. The selection of the specific tasks is also limited by the availability of pre-existing datasets.
*   **Domain Adaptation Analysis:**  While the paper analyzes domain adaptation, a deeper dive into *different types* of SE domain data could yield more nuanced insights.  The authors acknowledge this point as a future direction.
*   **Proprietary Model Limitations:** Relying on zero-shot prompting for proprietary models limits the ability to fully explore their potential, compared to fine-tuning the open-source models. There is a bias in the comparison, considering that the experiments do not allow the proprietary models to use in-context learning.
*   **MLM Task Abstraction:** The custom definition of the MLM task using only POS verbs is a strong abstraction, meaning this task is not directly comparable to a standard MLM task.
*   **Validity Threats:** The paper acknowledges important validity threats, such as dependence on pre-existing dataset labels and potential metric biases.

**Potential Influence:**

The paper sets a precedent for systematic evaluation of LLMs beyond code generation and understanding. It will likely spur further research in:

*   Developing new tasks and datasets that capture a broader range of non-code SE activities.
*   Investigating more sophisticated domain adaptation techniques using diverse types of SE data.
*   Designing specialized LLMs for specific SE tasks and sub-domains.
*   Creating benchmarks related to the design process

**Justification of Score:**

The paper makes a solid contribution by addressing a less-explored area of LLM application in SE. The creation of the SELU benchmark, the systematic evaluation, and the derivation of actionable insights are valuable. While the limitations mentioned above affect the generalizability to some extent, the study provides a solid foundation for future research. Thus, it is reasonable to award the following score:

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.SE
- **Score**: 7/10

### Accelerating Diffusion Large Language Models with SlowFast: The Three Golden Principles
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10848v1)
- **Authors**: Qingyan Wei, Yaojie Zhang, Zhiyuan Liu, Dongrui Liu, Linfeng Zhang
- **Abstract**: Diffusion-based language models (dLLMs) have emerged as a promising alternative to traditional autoregressive LLMs by enabling parallel token generation and significantly reducing inference latency.
